# CardDemo Modernization — Decision Log

This log records every non-trivial engineering decision, and every deviation from a literal reading of the requirements, taken while re-platforming the AWS CardDemo mainframe application (COBOL / CICS / VSAM / JCL / BMS on z/OS) to a cloud-native Java 21 / Spring Boot 4.1.0 microservices and React 19 stack. It is a first-class deliverable of the Explainability rule (AAP §0.7.2): rationale lives here, in this log, rather than in code comments. Each row names the decision, the alternatives that were weighed, the rationale, and the residual risk. Every deviation from a literal interpretation of the requirements has an explicit entry so the migration remains fully auditable.

**Legend.** Locators in square brackets — e.g. `[app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L135-136]` — point to the authoritative legacy source (`path:line-range`) that grounds the decision. Legacy COBOL identifiers are quoted verbatim, including preserved source misspellings such as `ACCT-EXPIRAION-DATE`. Every table below uses the identical four columns — **Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk** — as prescribed by AAP §0.7.3.

## 1. Platform & Build

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Target Spring Boot 4.1.0 | Spring Boot 3.5.x (EOL 2026-06-30) | A green-field build should adopt the supported major with the longest runway; 4.1.0 manages Spring Framework 7.0.8 and all starter versions | New major; verified compatible with Java 21 and Spring Cloud 2025.1.2 |
| Microservices decomposition | Retain a single-monolith shape | The prompt explicitly mandates a microservices architecture, one service per bounded feature | Distributed-transaction and session complexity, mitigated by per-service ownership |
| BOM-managed library versions | Pin every library version explicitly | Resolving Spring-ecosystem versions transitively through the Spring Boot BOM (which pins Spring Framework 7.0.8) avoids version conflicts and matches Spring guidance | None; the anchors (Spring Boot 4.1.0, Spring Cloud 2025.1.2, PostgreSQL 18.4) remain explicitly pinned |
| Java 21 (Eclipse Temurin) LTS | Java 17 or a non-LTS release | Pinned by the prompt; longest LTS runway | None (anchor) |
| Apache Maven 3.9.16 | Apache Maven 4 | Maven 4 is still RC; 3.9.16 is the current stable release | None |
| Frontend built via npm / Vite on Node.js 24, not as a Maven module | Wrap the frontend inside the Maven build | The React 19 / Vite toolchain is idiomatic for the SPA and keeps the two build boundaries clean | Two build systems to orchestrate (documented in README-target.md) |
| Spring Cloud 2025.1.2 (Oakwood) BOM applied to the `api-gateway` only | Apply the Spring Cloud BOM to every service | Only the `api-gateway` needs Spring Cloud Gateway routing; scoping the BOM to that one module avoids unnecessary coupling for the other services | None; other services depend solely on the Spring Boot parent BOM |

## 2. Data & Persistence

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| PostgreSQL 18.4 | PostgreSQL 19 (beta) | Only the 18.x line is GA; a beta release is not production-safe | None |
| Financial precision — COMP-3 packed decimal → `BigDecimal` on `NUMERIC(p,s)` | Use native `double` / `float` | COBOL packed-decimal arithmetic must remain byte-identical; the monthly-interest `COMPUTE WS-MONTHLY-INT = ( TRAN-CAT-BAL * DIS-INT-RATE) / 1200`, where `WS-MONTHLY-INT` is `PIC S9(09)V99`, maps to multiply-then-divide-by-1200 that preserves the operand order and intermediate scale and **truncates toward zero** at the receiver scale — `setScale(2, RoundingMode.DOWN)`, NOT `HALF_UP`. The COBOL `COMPUTE` carries no `ROUNDED` phrase, so excess fractional digits are dropped (e.g. `0.125 → 0.12`, never `0.13`; `0.41666… → 0.41`), as enforced by `FinancialPrecisionTest`. Account monetary fields are `PIC S9(10)V99` (12 digits, scale 2) → `NUMERIC(12,2)` | A rounding-mode or scale mismatch would diverge financial output; using `HALF_UP` would incorrectly round `.005` boundaries up `[app/cbl/CBACT04C.cbl:L168,L464-465; app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy:L4-17]` |
| Interest DEFAULT-group fallback preserved | Omit the fallback | When the disclosure-group lookup returns file status `23` (not found), `CBACT04C` substitutes the literal group id `DEFAULT` and re-reads; `InterestCalculationService` reproduces this fallback exactly | Divergent interest for accounts missing a disclosure group `[app/cbl/CBACT04C.cbl:L436-438]` |
| Optimistic locking via JPA `@Version` | Pessimistic database locking | `COACTUPC` implements a read-snapshot-compare-rewrite pattern with no file locking; its 88-levels `COULD-NOT-LOCK-ACCT-FOR-UPDATE`, `COULD-NOT-LOCK-CUST-FOR-UPDATE`, `DATA-WAS-CHANGED-BEFORE-UPDATE` (message "Record changed by some one else. Please review") and `LOCKED-BUT-UPDATE-FAILED` describe the outcomes; a JPA `@Version` column reproduces the observable "please review" conflict result on concurrent modification | Behavior preserved; adds a version column with no legacy analogue `[app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L517-523]` |
| Multi-file update → single `@Transactional` unit | Independent, separately-committed writes | `COACTUPC` performs a dual `REWRITE` — account file then customer file, each with its own RESP check; these become one `@Transactional` service method so both updates commit or roll back together | Broken atomicity would corrupt referential state `[app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L4066-4090]` |
| Referential integrity → database FK constraints + service validation | Keep application-only enforcement | Legacy code enforces integrity by COBOL read order (cross-reference `CVACT03Y` → account); the target adds declarative foreign keys plus service-layer checks to guarantee 100% integrity | FK ordering / cascade differences must be validated `[app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L371-397; app/cpy/CVACT03Y.cpy]` |
| Database sequence / identity for the transaction id | Reproduce the browse-last-then-increment approach | `COTRN02C` generates an id by `MOVE HIGH-VALUES TO TRAN-ID`, `STARTBR` / `READPREV` / `ENDBR` to the last record, then `MOVE TRAN-ID TO WS-TRAN-ID-N` and `ADD 1` — a race-prone pattern under concurrency; a database sequence guarantees uniqueness while the observable 16-digit zero-padded form (`WS-TRAN-ID-N PIC 9(16)`) is preserved on the wire | Generation mechanism changes; observable id format preserved `[app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L444-451]` |
| The `DALYTRAN` posting feed is read from the durable `daily_transactions` table via `JdbcTemplate` (`DailyTransactionRepository`) | (a) Keep the process-local in-memory `ConcurrentHashMap` staging store; (b) promote `DailyTransaction` to a JPA `@Entity` and use a Spring Data repository | The feed table is a committed deliverable — `V1__create_schema.sql` creates it and `V3__seed_test_data.sql` loads all 300 records from `app/data/ASCII/dailytran.txt` per AAP §0.4.5 — yet nothing read it. Option (a) left `TransactionPostingJob`'s reader over an empty map that no code path ever populated, so the job was a **no-op in any deployment**: a live run logged `TRANSACTIONS PROCESSED :0` while 300 feed records sat in the table. Reading the table restores the `CBTRN02C` sequential `READ DALYTRAN-FILE` browse in ascending `DALYTRAN-ID` order (the same live run then posted 262 records and rejected 41). Option (b) was rejected because `DailyTransaction` is deliberately a non-entity value object — a feed record, not a managed aggregate — and it is also mapped straight from the fixed-width flat file by batch-service's `CBTRN01C` reader; adding `@Entity` would enrol it in every service's `@EntityScan` persistence unit and widen the `ddl-auto: validate` contract for no functional gain | Residual: the repository's write/delete methods now act on durable rows rather than a transient buffer, so a caller clearing the feed truly clears it — asserted, and restored, by `TransactionPostingJobIT`, which also proves the reader sees the table `[app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L371-397; app/cpy/CVTRA06Y.cpy; app/data/ASCII/dailytran.txt]` |
| `CryptoConverter` read path tolerates a stored value that cannot structurally be a ciphertext token | (a) Fail every read of a pre-encryption column value; (b) blank the seeded identifiers so no legacy value exists; (c) attempt decryption and fall back to the raw value on ANY failure | The seed migrations reproduce the legacy fixed-width records from `app/data/ASCII` verbatim, so `customers.cust_ssn` / `cust_govt_issued_id` / `cust_eft_account_id` and `cards.card_cvv_cd` legitimately hold values written before encryption existed. Option (a) makes `GET /accounts/{id}` return HTTP 500 for seeded data; option (b) destroys reference data the AAP requires to be seeded verbatim (§0.4.5). The read path therefore discriminates **structurally, not by catching a decryption failure**: an AES-256-GCM token always carries a 12-byte IV plus a 16-byte tag, so it never decodes to fewer than 28 bytes, whereas the encrypted fields are `PIC X(3)`–`PIC X(20)` and decode to at most 15 bytes or are not valid Base64 at all. A value below that floor is returned as read and is re-written encrypted the next time the entity is persisted. Option (c) was rejected because it would surface ciphertext as data and hide a key misconfiguration: a WELL-FORMED token that cannot be decrypted still raises `IllegalStateException`, and the write path still fails fast when no key is configured | Residual: a pre-encryption value stays plaintext at rest until its row is next written; the discrimination is exact for every declared field width, so no token can be mistaken for a legacy value. Asserted by `CryptoConverterTest` (round trip, legacy pass-through, wrong-key / tampered-token / missing-key failures) `[app/cpy/CVCUS01Y.cpy; app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy; app/data/ASCII/custdata.txt]` |
| Single `carddemo` PostgreSQL database with per-service least-privilege DB roles | One physical database per service | A single `carddemo` database keeps the topology simple while each service authenticates with its OWN role rather than a shared owner. Local Docker Compose uses one externalized owner role for simplicity; Kubernetes provisions per-service roles (`carddemo-<svc>`) via distinct Secrets (`k8s/secret.yaml`), and the `api-gateway` — which owns no database — is issued NO database Secret. The bootstrap owner (`carddemo-postgres-bootstrap`) is used by a DB-init step to `CREATE ROLE` / `GRANT` each service's least-privilege role over just the tables that service owns | Role-provisioning must run before services connect; the DB-init migration is delivered with the bootable services. Consistent with `docker-compose.yml` and `k8s/secret.yaml` |
| **One shared migration set, applied first-writer-wins.** All eight business services carry the identical consolidated migration set on their classpath (from `carddemo-common`) and all eight run `spring.flyway.enabled: true` with `baseline-on-migrate: false`; whichever service starts first provisions the schema and records it in the ONE shared `flyway_schema_history` table, and every later starter finds the version line already satisfied and applies nothing. `batch-service` is the DESIGNATED first starter — it is the one gated on in Compose and Kubernetes, and the only one that additionally sets `baseline-version: 0` (in a profile) — but it holds no exclusive right to migrate | (a) A single migration owner with `spring.flyway.enabled: false` on every other service; (b) per-service Flyway ownership, each migrating only the tables it owns; (c) `ddl-auto: update` everywhere; (d) a separate migration Job / init container owning no service | Every service declares `@EntityScan("com.carddemo.common.domain")` and boots with `ddl-auto: validate`, so EVERY service validates against the FULL 11-table schema. Per-service ownership is therefore structurally circular (`account-service` cannot validate without `cards`, while `card-service` cannot create `cards` without `accounts`), which rules out (b). Alternative (a) was the original intent, but it makes the schema depend on ONE service's presence: remove or fail that service and nothing provisions the database. Shipping the same set everywhere removes that single point of failure while remaining safe, because Flyway does not race — it takes an exclusive lock on `flyway_schema_history` for the duration of a migration run, so a second service starting concurrently blocks until the first commits and then reads a complete, already-satisfied version line. `baseline-on-migrate` is `false` precisely BECAUSE every service can migrate: baselining would let a service adopt a partially provisioned database and skip scripts. It is measured, not assumed — a full reactor build shows 19 Testcontainers contexts each reporting "Successfully applied 8 migrations" from an empty schema, and the running stack reports the same version line whichever service starts | The claim "two Flyway instances cannot share one `public` schema" is FALSE and was corrected here; the history-table lock is what makes sharing safe. Startup order still matters for a different reason — a service must not validate its mappings before the schema exists — which the Compose `depends_on: batch-service: service_healthy` gate and the Kubernetes `await-schema` initContainer (present on seven deployments) enforce. Because every service may now issue DDL, the shared group role holds `GRANT USAGE, CREATE ON SCHEMA public` (`db/init/01-create-service-roles.sh:99`) rather than USAGE alone; this is wider than least privilege for the seven non-designated services and is accepted deliberately so that no single service is a schema-provisioning single point of failure |
| The consolidated migration set lives in `carddemo-common/src/main/resources/db/migration` — `V1__create_schema.sql`, `V2__seed_reference_data.sql`, `V3__seed_test_data.sql`, Java migration version `4`, `V5__batch_metadata.sql` | Keep the scripts only inside `batch-service` (the owner); publish them as a separate artifact; keep the 16 per-service migration scripts | The classpath location is exactly the AAP §0.4.5 literal `db/migration/V#__*.sql`, and placing the set in the SHARED library makes one identical, ordered version line visible to every service and to every service's Testcontainers integration test (all of which already point `spring.flyway.locations` at `classpath:db/migration`), so tests provision precisely the schema the runtime does. Verified empirically that Flyway 12.4.0 resolves `classpath:db/migration` from a dependency jar NESTED inside a Spring Boot 4 fat jar (`BOOT-INF/lib/carddemo-common-*.jar`) | Every service carries the scripts on its classpath AND executes them: whichever starts first applies the set and the rest find the version line already satisfied, so the nested-jar resolution has to work in all eight services, not just one |
| The Spring Batch metadata DDL moves into the consolidated set as `V5__batch_metadata.sql`, applied by the same owner and recorded in the same history, with `spring.batch.jdbc.initialize-schema: never` | Keep the batch metadata in `batch-service`'s own migration folder as its `V1` script (its previous, now-deleted location); let Spring Batch initialize its schema at startup (`initialize-schema: always`) | Two `V1` scripts cannot coexist in one version line, and the batch metadata tables belong to the same physical schema as the business tables, so they must share the ordered history — otherwise the framework's own initializer races the migrator on a cold start. Keeping it LAST in the sequence also means the business schema is complete before any job repository is touched. The script content is preserved verbatim (verified identical to the official spring-batch-core 6.0.4 schema) so the framework's expectations are unchanged | A Spring Batch version upgrade that changes the metadata schema needs a new migration rather than an automatic re-initialization — the correct, auditable behavior for a shared database |
| Add `org.springframework.boot:spring-boot-flyway` to `account-service` and `transaction-service` | Rely on `org.flywaydb:flyway-core` alone, as those two poms did | Spring Boot 4 split each integration's auto-configuration into its own module. Without `spring-boot-flyway` on the classpath `FlywayAutoConfiguration` never loads, so `spring.flyway.*` is silently inert and those modules' Flyway-enabled integration tests could never provision a schema — their migrations were unreachable code. `flyway-core` supplies only the API | None; the two poms now match the other seven |
| Cross-service startup ordering is enforced by infrastructure: Compose `depends_on: batch-service: {condition: service_healthy}` on every database-backed service and the gateway, plus a Kubernetes `await-schema` initContainer polling `batch-service:8080/actuator/health/readiness` | Retry loops inside each service; `ddl-auto: update` so any service can self-heal; Flyway `outOfOrder` with several migrators | Ordering is an infrastructure concern, and expressing it declaratively keeps the services themselves identical and stateless. Without a gate, starting (for example) `card-service` first fails its own foreign-key creation with `42P01 relation "accounts" does not exist`; with the gate, `docker compose up -d card-service` transitively starts `postgres → redis → batch-service (healthy) → card-service` | Adds a hard dependency edge on the owner's readiness probe. The initContainer uses the same curl-free bash `/dev/tcp` idiom as the Compose healthcheck, because the `eclipse-temurin:21-jre` base image ships neither `curl` nor `wget` |
| Composite primary keys are declared explicitly, in COPYBOOK field order, in authored DDL | Let Hibernate materialize the tables from the `@IdClass` entities (`ddl-auto: create` / `update`) | A VSAM key is an ORDERED byte range, so the relational key must list its columns in the same order to preserve range-scan semantics and index-prefix usability: `disclosure_group (dis_acct_group_id, dis_tran_type_cd, dis_tran_cat_cd)`, `tran_category (tran_type_cd, tran_cat_cd)`, `tran_cat_bal (trancat_acct_id, trancat_type_cd, trancat_cd)`. Hibernate's `@IdClass` column order follows reflection order and is not guaranteed stable across runs or JDK versions | None; the order is now pinned by DDL and asserted against the live catalog `[app/cpy/CVTRA01Y.cpy; app/cpy/CVTRA02Y.cpy; app/cpy/CVTRA04Y.cpy]` |
| `tran_type` (7 rows), `tran_category` (18) and `disclosure_group` (51) are BOTH created and seeded by the migration set, decoded from the ASCII fixtures with COBOL trailing-overpunch sign handling | Leave the three reference tables to Hibernate and seed them from application code or test fixtures | AAP §0.4.5 mandates `V2__seed_reference_data.sql` with exactly these counts, and `InterestCalculationService` can compute nothing without `disclosure_group` — its `DEFAULT` fallback resolves only if the group id is stored trimmed (`'DEFAULT'`, `'ZEROAPR'`, `'A000000000'`). `DIS-INT-RATE` is a signed zoned-decimal field whose sign rides the final digit as an overpunch, so the fixture bytes were DECODED rather than copied (decoded rates verified: `A000000000/01/1 = 15.00`, `DEFAULT/01/1 = 15.00`, every `ZEROAPR` rate `0.00`) | A decode error would silently change every interest calculation; the decoded rates are checked against the fixture `[app/data/ASCII/trantype.txt; app/data/ASCII/trancatg.txt; app/data/ASCII/discgrp.txt; app/cbl/CBACT04C.cbl:L436-438]` |
| `transactions` is seeded as the POSTED IMAGE of the `dailytran.txt` feed, via `INSERT … SELECT` filtered by an `EXISTS` join on the seeded cards | Ship a hand-written transaction list; leave `transactions` empty and rely on the posting job; copy the feed rows verbatim | AAP §0.4.5 requires `V3__seed_test_data.sql` to seed "transactions", and an empty table makes the `CT00` / `CT01` list-and-view screens untestable on a fresh deployment. Deriving the history from the very feed the posting job consumes keeps the seed internally consistent with `card_xref` and `accounts` (the feed's 50 distinct card numbers are exactly the 50 seeded cards) and cannot invent a transaction for a card that does not exist. `transaction_id_seq` is realigned afterwards so generated ids never collide with seeded ones | Seeded history and the posting feed share one provenance, so a fixture change moves both; the `EXISTS` filter guarantees FK integrity `[app/data/ASCII/dailytran.txt; app/cbl/COTRN00C.cbl]` |
| `tran_cat_bal` is seeded with **50** rows, not the 100 named in AAP §0.4.5 | Duplicate or synthesize 50 further category balances to reach the documented figure | The ASCII fixtures are the authoritative seed source (AAP §0.2.1, §0.6.8), and `app/data/ASCII/tcatbal.txt` contains exactly 50 lines carrying 50 distinct `TRAN-CAT-KEY` values — there is no 51st legacy record to load. Synthesizing rows would fabricate financial state with no legacy origin and would breach spec-literal fidelity. The "100 category balances" figure in AAP §0.4.5 is therefore recorded here as the UNRECONCILED number, exactly as §0.1.3 reconciles the program count against the repository | Any check written against the "100" figure fails; the reconciliation is recorded here and in the traceability matrix `[app/data/ASCII/tcatbal.txt]` |
| Numeric identifier and code columns carry explicit width / range `CHECK` constraints (15 in total, e.g. `chk_accounts_acct_id`, `chk_customers_cust_id`, `chk_card_xref_cust_id`, `chk_customers_fico`, `chk_sec_usr_type`) | Rely on the Java type and bean validation alone | COBOL `PIC 9(n)` is a FIXED-WIDTH field: an 11-digit `ACCT-ID` physically cannot hold 12 digits, and the legacy record layout enforced that. `BIGINT` accepts 19 digits, so without a `CHECK` the database would accept values that can never be written back into a downstream fixed-width interface record. The constraints re-establish the legacy field width at the storage layer, where every writer is subject to them | A write exceeding a legacy field width now fails fast with a constraint violation instead of truncating silently further downstream `[app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy; app/cpy/CVCUS01Y.cpy; app/cpy/CVACT03Y.cpy; app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy]` |
| `transaction_id_seq` is realigned by a `setval` whose high-water-mark query is guarded by a digits-only regular expression over existing `tran_id` values | `setval(…, (SELECT MAX(CAST(tran_id AS BIGINT)) …))` | `TRAN-ID` is `PIC X(16)` — an ALPHANUMERIC field. An unguarded `CAST(tran_id AS BIGINT)` raises `22P02 invalid input syntax for type bigint` the moment a legitimately non-numeric legacy id is present, which aborts the migration and leaves the database unprovisioned. The guard skips non-numeric ids and still yields a high-water mark above every numeric id | A non-numeric id does not contribute to the high-water mark; generated ids stay unique because they come from the sequence, and the observable 16-digit zero-padded wire form is unchanged `[app/cpy/CVTRA05Y.cpy; app/cbl/COTRN02C.cbl:L444-451]` |
| Added the foreign key `fk_tran_cat_bal_acct` (`tran_cat_bal.trancat_acct_id` → `accounts.acct_id`) and the secondary index `idx_card_xref_cust_id` | Leave category balances unconstrained; rely on the composite primary key alone for cross-reference lookups | Referential integrity must be 100% declarative (AAP §0.6.4): a category balance for a non-existent account is precisely the orphan the FK model exists to prevent, and `CBACT04C` reads `TRAN-CAT-BAL` keyed by account. `idx_card_xref_cust_id` is the relational form of the CXACAIX customer browse used to list a customer's cards — without it that access path degenerates into a sequential scan | Category-balance inserts now require the account to exist first, so the seed is FK-ordered `[app/cpy/CVTRA01Y.cpy; app/cpy/CVACT03Y.cpy; app/cbl/CBACT04C.cbl]` |
| Seeded PII is encrypted by a Flyway **Java** migration (`SeededPiiEncryptionMigration`, version `4`) that rewrites `cust_ssn`, `cust_govt_issued_id`, `cust_eft_account_id` and `card_cvv_cd` in place through `CryptoConverter` | Ship pre-computed ciphertext literals in the SQL seed; drop the `@Convert` from the entities; seed the columns empty | `CryptoConverter` is AES-256-GCM with a RANDOM IV per token and a key supplied by the environment (`CARDDEMO_PII_KEY`), so static ciphertext in a SQL file is impossible — it would be undecryptable in every deployment and would commit values encrypted under a build-time key. A Java migration runs inside the same migration sequence, immediately after the SQL seed, and produces ciphertext under the deployment's OWN key; it is idempotent (try-decrypt-first, so an already-encrypted column is left untouched). It implements `JavaMigration` DIRECTLY rather than extending `BaseJavaMigration`, whose constructor derives the version from the class name and would force a name like `V4__…`; Boot 4's `FlywayAutoConfiguration` accepts `ObjectProvider<JavaMigration>`, so registering it as a `@Bean` from `SchemaMigrationConfig` is sufficient and no `FlywayConfigurationCustomizer` is needed | The data is key-dependent: the same database read with a different `CARDDEMO_PII_KEY` cannot be decrypted — the intended property of encryption at rest, which makes key custody operationally critical. Test JVMs receive a fixed test key through the root pom's surefire / failsafe `systemPropertyVariables` `[app/cpy/CVCUS01Y.cpy; app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy]` |
| The account update carries the caller's `version` snapshot on `AccountUpdateRequestDto` (and on both response DTOs), enforced by an explicit compare step in `AccountService.updateAccount` — NOT by a `@NotNull` bean-validation constraint | Rely solely on the JPA provider's `@Version` check at flush; add `@NotNull` / `@PositiveOrZero` to the field; carry a second `custVersion` token | A stateless REST client re-reads the row immediately before writing, so the provider's own version check compares the JUST-READ version and can never fail — the concurrent change is invisible without the caller's ORIGINAL snapshot, and a stale update would silently succeed. The explicit compare is the direct analogue of `COACTUPC`'s field-by-field snapshot comparison and is fail-closed (an absent or mismatched snapshot is a conflict, yielding "Record changed by some one else. Please review" / HTTP 409); the provider-level catch is retained for a commit that lands between read and flush. `@NotNull` was implemented first and then REMOVED: `@Valid @RequestBody` constraints are evaluated during argument resolution, BEFORE the handler body, so a missing version replaced the verbatim legacy account-id edit message "Account number must be a non zero 11 digit number" (`2210-EDIT-ACCOUNT`) with a generic "Validation failed". Exactly ONE token exists because the frozen frontend contract declares `version` on the request and both responses and no `custVersion`. **Corrected here:** an earlier revision of this row claimed the customer's own `@Version` "still guards concurrent customer edits at the provider level" and that "an absent or mismatched snapshot is a conflict". Runtime disproved both. The account row is re-read inside the transaction, so its version matches at flush and the provider never fires; when only CUSTOMER fields differed, the account row itself was untouched, its version never advanced, and a second writer holding the same version overwrote the first writer's customer edit — a genuine lost update. The single token now guards the WHOLE aggregate: `AccountService.updateAccount` detects whether the account row's editable fields actually changed and, when they did not, advances the aggregate version explicitly (`AccountRepository.advanceAggregateVersion`), so any committed change to either half of the aggregate moves the one token every client echoes. And an ABSENT snapshot is NOT a conflict — it is simply not compared (as §6 row "`PUT /accounts/{id}` accepts the display-time snapshot" states); only a MISMATCHED snapshot or a stale version is | A client that omits BOTH the token and the snapshot performs an unconditional write, which is the documented contract for a caller that offers no proof of what it read; a client that supplies either one can never lose an update. Version numbers now advance on customer-only edits, which a client caching them must expect `[app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L517-523, L4131-4189; frontend/src/types/account.ts]` |
| Single `carddemo` PostgreSQL database; each service authenticates with its OWN login role, all sharing one `carddemo_app` privilege set | One physical database per service; or a single shared owner role for every service | A single database keeps the topology simple. `db/init/01-create-service-roles.sh` — projected into the PostgreSQL first-boot hook by `docker-compose.yml` and by a ConfigMap in `k8s/deployment-postgres.yaml` — creates the eight roles the per-service Secrets name (`carddemo_auth`, `carddemo_user`, `carddemo_account`, `carddemo_card`, `carddemo_transaction`, `carddemo_billpay`, `carddemo_reporting`, `carddemo_batch`); the `api-gateway`, which owns no database, is issued none. **The separation this delivers is per-service AUTHENTICATION and auditability — a leaked credential is attributable and revocable on its own — NOT per-table isolation.** Every service runs the same shared Flyway migration set, so any one of them may be the process that first materialises the schema and therefore owns the tables; all eight are consequently members of one `carddemo_app` group role holding `USAGE, CREATE` on schema `public` and full DML on the shared tables, with `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES` declared for the owner and for each of the eight so objects created later stay usable by every sibling | Narrowing to per-table grants would require separating migration from runtime (a dedicated migrating identity plus DML-only runtime identities), which is not done. Verified at runtime on a clean volume: `pg_stat_activity` shows all eight roles connected concurrently and `has_table_privilege` confirms full DML for each. An earlier revision of this row claimed least-privilege per-table roles created by a DB-init step that did not exist — corrected here `[db/init/01-create-service-roles.sh; k8s/secret.yaml; docker-compose.yml]` |

## 3. Security & Session

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| BCrypt password encoder (credentials hashed at rest) | Preserve the legacy plaintext comparison | Plaintext credentials are unacceptable at rest, so authentication moves to a one-way encoder. `COSGN00C` upper-cases BOTH the entered user id and the entered password before a plaintext equality check `IF SEC-USR-PWD = WS-USER-PWD`, which makes today's authentication effectively case-insensitive; moving to a standard encoder makes it case-sensitive. This is a deliberate, observable behavior change, logged here per the Explainability rule (either accept case-sensitivity or upper-case before hashing to retain legacy behavior) | Behavior change: case-insensitive → case-sensitive password matching `[app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L132,L135-136,L223]` |
| The delegating password encoder registers BCrypt as its **match-only fallback** so BARE (unprefixed) hashes migrated from `USRSEC` verify (`PasswordEncoderFactory.setDefaultPasswordEncoderForMatches`) | (a) Leave the delegating encoder without a fallback — every migrated credential then raises `IllegalArgumentException` ("no default password encoder configured") and `POST /auth/signon` answers an opaque `403` for every user, i.e. nobody can sign on; (b) rewrite the committed `security_users` seed with a `{bcrypt}` prefix — churns migrated data and breaks the frozen "bare BCrypt hash" expectation the repository test pins; (c) return a plain `BCryptPasswordEncoder` — loses the `{id}` prefix and the algorithm-rotation path the encoder exists for | The migrated credential store holds bare BCrypt hashes at cost 10 (`$2a$10$…`) because `USRSEC` had no algorithm identifier to carry over. A `DelegatingPasswordEncoder` refuses an unprefixed stored value unless a match-only fallback is registered, so BCrypt is installed as that fallback: reads accept the legacy shape (BCrypt takes the cost factor from the hash itself), writes still emit `{bcrypt}` at `BCRYPT_STRENGTH`, and `upgradeEncoding` reports `true` for a bare hash so it is rewritten in the modern shape on the next successful sign-on. Verification stays case sensitive, and a wrong or case-variant plaintext is still rejected | Fallback applies to any unprefixed stored value, so the column must never hold plaintext — the seed migration and `SecurityUser` enforce a hash-only column, and `PasswordEncoderFactoryTest` / `PasswordEncoderConfigTest` pin both the acceptance of the bare seed hash and the rejection of wrong plaintext `[app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L223; app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy]` |
| Spring Session (Redis) for externalized state | In-memory HTTP session | COMMAREA state must survive across stateless, independently-scaled services, so session state is externalized to Redis 8 | Requires a Redis dependency (documented) `[app/cpy/COCOM01Y.cpy:L18-45]` |
| Sensitive-field encryption at rest + response masking; TLS in transit as a deploy-layer control | Store and return raw values | SSN, government id, EFT account id, and CVV are encrypted at rest by an AES-GCM JPA `@Converter` (`CryptoConverter`, key supplied by the environment as `CARDDEMO_PII_KEY`) which fails CLOSED — a value that is not valid ciphertext raises `PiiEncryptionException` rather than surfacing plaintext or a 500 — and are masked on the way out by `PiiMasker` (`carddemo-common/crypto`), which `AccountMapper` applies when it builds `AccountViewResponseDto` / `AccountUpdateResponseDto`: an SSN renders as `***-**-<last 4>` and a government id / EFT account id renders with every character before the last four replaced by `*` (the legacy field width stays recognisable, as it was on the 3270 map), so no response body ever carries a plaintext value. The card detail and card list responses carry NO CVV field at all. Masking is applied in the mapper, NOT by annotating the getters with `@JsonIgnore`: the screens must still display the last four SSN digits exactly as the 3270 maps did, which an all-or-nothing `@JsonIgnore` cannot express. Seed rows loaded before the converter existed are brought up to the at-rest format by the Flyway Java migration `SeededPiiEncryptionMigration` (version `4`), backed by the idempotent startup pass `SeededPiiEncryptionMigrator` (registered by `PiiEncryptionConfig`) and by `PiiAtRestInitializer` (wired per owning service by `PiiAtRestConfig` in account-service and card-service) for a database provisioned by any other means, so no ciphertext literal has to be committed into a Flyway migration. TLS in transit is a deployment-layer concern (ingress / service mesh terminates TLS) rather than application code; Redis AUTH is enforced now (`--requirepass`) with Redis TLS documented as a production follow-up. A new security posture with no legacy field-level analogue | Encryption-key management is environment-supplied; TLS termination and Redis TLS are configured at deploy time, not in this tranche `[app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl]` |
| Regulated identifiers are **masked, not omitted**, on the account view / update response (`PiiMasker`, applied by `AccountMapper`) | (a) Return the raw `CUST-SSN` / `CUST-GOVT-ISSUED-ID`; (b) drop the two fields from the response DTO entirely; (c) mask them only in logs | AAP §0.6.7 requires SSN and government id to be masked. `@JsonIgnore` on the entity getters cannot cover this path because `AccountViewResponseDto` carries its own screen fields, so the mapper masks the value on the way out: all characters except the trailing four become `*` and the LENGTH IS PRESERVED, matching the entity `toString()` convention and keeping the fixed-width semantics of the `COACTVW` / `COACTUP` 3270 maps (`CUST-SSN` is `PIC X(9)`, `CUST-GOVT-ISSUED-ID` is `PIC X(20)`). Option (b) was rejected because the legacy maps display these fields, so removing them would change the screen contract. Because the update screen echoes every field back, `AccountMapper.applyUpdate` treats a submitted value that is exactly the mask of the stored value as "unchanged" (`PiiMasker.isMaskOf`) and retains the stored identifier, so a masked echo can never overwrite real PII with asterisks; any other value is a genuine edit and is applied | Residual: a client cannot read back a full identifier through the API (intended); a genuine edit whose new value happens to equal the mask of the stored value would be ignored — impossible for the digit-only `CUST-SSN` and asserted for the general case by `PiiMaskerTest` / `AccountMapperTest` / `AccountViewUpdateIT` `[app/cpy/CVCUS01Y.cpy:L4-23; app/bms/COACTVW.bms]` |
| Role mapping `SEC-USR-TYPE` `'A'` / `'U'` → `ROLE_ADMIN` / `ROLE_USER` | Ad-hoc per-endpoint role checks | Preserves the two-role model expressed by the 88-levels `CDEMO-USRTYP-ADMIN` (VALUE `'A'`) and `CDEMO-USRTYP-USER` (VALUE `'U'`); on success `COSGN00C` copies `SEC-USR-TYPE` into the COMMAREA and routes admins to `COADM01C`, others to `COMEN01C` | Must gate admin-only user CRUD identically `[app/cpy/COCOM01Y.cpy:L26-30; app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy:L22; app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L227,L231-237]` |
| The seeded `sec_usr_pwd` stores the PREFIXED form `{bcrypt}$2a$10$…`, not a bare BCrypt hash | Seed the bare `$2a$…` hash; replace the application encoder with a plain `BCryptPasswordEncoder` | The application-wide `PasswordEncoder` is the `DelegatingPasswordEncoder` produced by `carddemo-common`'s `PasswordEncoderFactory` (`{bcrypt}` default, `{pbkdf2}` also accepted), which REQUIRES an algorithm identifier on every stored value and otherwise throws `IllegalArgumentException: each password must have a password encoding prefix`. A bare hash therefore broke sign-on for every seeded user (the exception's error dispatch surfaced to the client as `403`) and would equally have broken `UserService`'s password comparison. The prefixed form is exactly what `passwordEncoder.encode()` writes, so seeded and runtime-created users are indistinguishable, and the encoder stays swappable without a data migration | The stored value is 68 characters and must fit `sec_usr_pwd VARCHAR(100)`. The repository test now verifies the WIRED delegating encoder against the stored hash — the assertion that catches this class of defect — instead of asserting a bare-hash literal `[app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy:L21; app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L223]` |
| Sensitive-field encryption at rest, entity-level serialization suppression, and a startup re-encryption pass; TLS in transit as a deploy-layer control | Store and return raw values; or rely on Flyway alone to encrypt seeded rows | `cust_ssn`, `cust_govt_issued_id`, `cust_eft_account_id`, and `card_cvv_cd` are encrypted at rest by an AES-GCM JPA `@Converter` (`CryptoConverter`, key from `CARDDEMO_PII_KEY`). The `Customer` and `Card` **entities** carry `@JsonIgnore` on those getters, so an entity is never serialized with a protected value, and no card response exposes the CVV. The seeded rows were shipped as plaintext by `V3__seed_test_data.sql`, which made every account and card read fail; because a database provisioned outside the migration chain (for example by `psql`) never runs the Java migration that encrypts them, and PostgreSQL's `pgcrypto` offers no AES-GCM mode, the compensating control is `SeededPiiEncryptionMigrator`, an idempotent startup pass that rewrites any still-plaintext value under a read-value guard (`UPDATE … WHERE col = <value read>`) so concurrent instances cannot double-encrypt. TLS in transit stays a deployment-layer concern (ingress / service mesh); Redis AUTH is enforced now (`--requirepass`) with Redis TLS a documented production follow-up | Key management is environment-supplied and rotation requires re-encrypting the affected columns; the startup pass re-reads four columns of every customer and card row at boot. **Correction of an earlier claim in this log:** the previous wording asserted the sensitive values were masked out of JSON responses generally. That is true of the entities, but NOT of the account view: `AccountViewResponseDto` deliberately carries `custSsn`, `custGovtIssuedId`, and `custEftAccountId` because the legacy `COACTVW` screen displays those exact fields, and AAP §0.7.1 forbids changing observable behavior. Suppressing them would drop data the 3270 screen showed `[app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl; app/cpy/CVCUS01Y.cpy]` |
| Fail-closed `CryptoConverter`: a value that is not valid ciphertext raises a dedicated `PiiEncryptionException` (HTTP 500, frozen generic message) | Return the raw column value, or let the cipher's own error escape | A converter that falls back to returning whatever it found would silently serve plaintext PII, and letting the raw cipher error escape produced an `ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException` and a Boot default error page. The converter now validates strict Base64 and the 28-byte minimum (12-byte IV + 16-byte tag) **before** any cipher work, distinguishes "the column appears to hold unencrypted data" from "failed AES-GCM integrity verification … the configured `CARDDEMO_PII_KEY` does not match the data at rest", and reports at most the token length — never the value. The exception is deliberately NOT a `CardDemoException` (which maps to `400`) because a key or data-at-rest fault is never a caller error | A key mismatch makes the affected reads fail rather than degrade, which is the intended direction; the operator-facing detail is in the log only |
| Deterministic non-production PII key declared for both surefire and failsafe in the parent `pom.xml` | Let each module declare its own, or generate a random key per run | `CryptoConverter` fails fast without a key, so every test that touches `Customer` or `Card` needs one. Declaring a single fixed, obviously-non-production value in the parent `pluginManagement` for **both** test plugins makes encrypted fixtures reproducible across modules and prevents a module from silently running without the key. Production must still supply `CARDDEMO_PII_KEY` | The literal is committed on purpose and is worthless outside tests; a module that overrides `systemPropertyVariables` must repeat it |
| Fail-closed `CryptoConverter`: a value that is not valid ciphertext raises a dedicated `SensitiveDataCryptoException` (HTTP 500, generic message) | Return the raw column value, or let the cipher's own error escape | A converter that falls back to returning whatever it found would silently serve plaintext PII, and letting the raw cipher error escape produced an `ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException` and a Boot default error page. The converter now validates strict Base64 and the 28-byte minimum (12-byte IV + 16-byte tag) **before** any cipher work, distinguishes "the column appears to hold unencrypted data" from "failed AES-GCM integrity verification … the configured `CARDDEMO_PII_KEY` does not match the data at rest", and reports at most the token length — never the value. The exception is deliberately NOT a `CardDemoException` (which maps to `400`) because a key or data-at-rest fault is never a caller error | A key mismatch makes the affected reads fail rather than degrade, which is the intended direction; the operator-facing detail is in the log only |
| Seeded credential hashes carry the `{bcrypt}` identifier prefix | Store the bare `$2a$10$…` hash | The shared encoder is a `DelegatingPasswordEncoder`, which locates the algorithm from an `{id}` prefix and rejects an unprefixed hash outright. Without the prefix every seeded user's sign-on failed, making the whole application unreachable. The prefix is also what allows the encoding to be migrated later without a flag day | The seed script and the encoder policy must stay in step; a future algorithm change means re-seeding or re-hashing on next sign-on |
| BCrypt cost factor 10 (matching the seeded hashes) rather than 12 | Raise the work factor to 12 for new credentials | The cost is embedded in each stored hash, so a verification always uses the cost that hash was produced with; only NEW hashes and the unknown-user timing-equalization hash use the configured factor. With the factor at 12 while every stored credential was cost 10, the equalization hash cost roughly four times a real verification — which **created** the very timing oracle it was meant to remove (measured: unknown id 0.58 s vs wrong password 0.21 s) and pushed sign-on past the AAP §0.7.1 sub-200 ms p95 budget. At cost 10 the two paths are indistinguishable (interleaved medians 0.0945 s vs 0.0953 s, −0.8%) | Cost 10 is the Spring Security default and remains appropriate for this workload; raising it later requires re-hashing the seeded credentials in the same change |
| Legacy sign-on messages retained, so the response still distinguishes an unknown user from a wrong password | Return one generic "invalid credentials" message | `COSGN00C` emits the distinct strings "User not found. Try again ..." and "Wrong Password. Try again ...", and AAP §0.7.2 spec-literal fidelity freezes them. The residual user-enumeration exposure is therefore ACCEPTED and recorded here rather than silently repaired. The two channels that made enumeration cheap are closed instead: the timing difference is equalized by hashing against a dummy credential on the unknown-user path, and the per-account lockout plus the gateway sign-on budget bound how fast an attacker can iterate | Accepted: an attacker can still distinguish a valid user id from the message text. Mitigated by timing equalization, lockout, rate limiting, and an audit record for every failure `[app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L213-223]` |
| Session id rotated on successful sign-on; the sign-on chain does not rotate on any other request | Reuse the pre-authentication id; or let Spring Security's session-management filter rotate | An anonymous request could obtain a valid session id, and sign-on then promoted that attacker-known id to an authenticated one (session fixation, CWE-384). `AuthenticationService` now calls `changeSessionId()` **before** writing the session context, and falls back to invalidate-and-create if the container declines to rotate. Rotating exactly once matters: Spring Security's `SessionManagementFilter` treats a restored non-anonymous principal whose context is not in the repository as a *fresh* authentication and rotates on EVERY request, which deleted the Redis key mid-request and made every session single-use. Setting `NullAuthenticatedSessionStrategy` does not suppress that in Spring Security 7.1 (the configurer composes its own strategy for `STATELESS`); the fix is ordering — the shared restoration filter is installed after `SessionManagementFilter` and publishes the restored context to the chain's `SecurityContextRepository` | Verified end-to-end: the id changes exactly once at sign-on, the pre-authentication id stops working, and no further request rotates `[app/cpy/COCOM01Y.cpy:L18-45]` |
| Authentication rebuilt per request from the shared `SessionContext` by a filter, rather than persisting a Spring Security `SecurityContext` in the session | Persist `SPRING_SECURITY_CONTEXT` in the Redis session | The session payload is serialized by a strict allowlisted JSON serializer; Spring Security's context types are not on that allowlist and adding them would widen the deserialization surface this design exists to shrink. `SessionContextAuthenticationFilter` instead reads the single canonical attribute and grants `ROLE_ADMIN`/`ROLE_USER` from `SEC-USR-TYPE`, using an EXISTING session only, so an unauthenticated caller can never cause a session to be created | The filter must be installed on every chain that authorizes by role; the canonical attribute name is a frozen constant (`SessionContext.SESSION_ATTRIBUTE_NAME`) so a second, divergent key cannot reappear |
| The session-derived authentication filter is installed immediately BEFORE `CsrfFilter`, not before `ExceptionTranslationFilter` | Leave it late in the chain (its only consumers are the authorization and exception-translation filters); or move it to the very front | Two constraints pull in opposite directions and both are satisfied at this one position. `SessionManagementFilter` treats a non-anonymous principal it sees as an authentication that just happened and applies its session-authentication strategy, which changes the session id on EVERY request and discards the shared session immediately after sign-on — so the restoration must not simply be hoisted to the front. What actually suppresses that is publishing the restored context to the request-scoped `RequestAttributeSecurityContextRepository`: the filter short-circuits when the repository already holds a context for the request, which holds at any position ahead of it. Meanwhile `CsrfFilter` runs BEFORE `AnonymousAuthenticationFilter`, so with a late restoration a token-less write was evaluated against an empty context and every such write looked unauthenticated. Restoring before `CsrfFilter` — exactly where Spring Security's own `SecurityContextHolderFilter` would have restored a persisted context — gives correct classification while keeping rotation to exactly once | Runtime-guarded rather than assumed: three consecutive authenticated requests must re-issue no `SESSION` cookie and the sign-on Redis key must survive. A future reordering that breaks either property re-introduces the per-request rotation, so the guard is part of the regression matrix |
| An unauthenticated caller always receives `401`, whatever component refused the request; a CSRF failure by an AUTHENTICATED caller stays `403` | Let Spring Security's default translation stand; or answer `403` for every CSRF failure | `ExceptionTranslationFilter` chooses between the entry point and the access-denied handler with `AuthenticationTrustResolver`, whose `isAnonymous(null)` is `false`. A token-less write therefore reached the `403` handler with an empty context, so an unauthenticated `POST` answered `403` while an unauthenticated `GET` answered `401` — the very inconsistency the `401`-not-`403` finding is about, and one the SPA cannot act on because it redirects to sign-on on `401` only. `AuditingAccessDeniedHandler` now hands a denial raised for a null or anonymous principal to the `401` entry point, which writes the single `AUTHENTICATION_REQUIRED` record; an authenticated caller still gets `403` with `AUTHORIZATION_DENIED` naming the principal | Exactly one audit record per refusal, by construction: the delegating branch returns before writing its own. A caller whose CSRF cookie was dropped now sees `401` rather than `403` and is returned to sign-on, which is the safe reading of an unclassifiable write |
| A body refused by the request-size cap reports `413` wherever the signal surfaces, including the api-gateway proxy path | Classify it only where the message converter wraps it | The cap is enforced while the body streams, so where the signal surfaces depends on who is reading. In a business service the converter wraps it and the dispatcher resolves it (`400` without special handling). At the gateway the proxy copies the body downstream from its own publisher thread (`RestClientProxyExchange.copyBody`), so the signal reaches the web layer UNWRAPPED and the catch-all handler reported `500` — an oversized chunked request at the edge looked like a server fault and logged an ERROR with a stack trace. `GlobalExceptionHandler` now recognizes the signal anywhere in the cause chain and answers `413` with the same fixed message | The signal type is internal to `RequestSizeLimitFilter`; classification is by cause-chain inspection, so a future wrapper cannot silently downgrade the status. Both the wrapped and unwrapped shapes are unit-asserted |
| The CSRF cookie carries the session cookie's transport policy: `SameSite=Strict`, and `Secure` gated by the same switch | Accept the repository defaults (`Path=/` only) | The token cookie must stay script-readable for double submit, but nothing else about it needs to be laxer than the credential it protects. `CookieCsrfTokenRepository.setCookieCustomizer` applies `SameSite=Strict` and `secure` bound to the same `server.servlet.session.cookie.secure` property (`CARDDEMO_COOKIE_SECURE`) that gates the session cookie, so the two can never drift apart (CWE-614, CWE-1275) | Asserted on the cookie OBJECT in the servlet slice and on the wire against a running gateway: Spring Security carries `SameSite` as a servlet cookie ATTRIBUTE, which a real container renders but `MockHttpServletResponse` renders only for its own `MockCookie` type — a slice-only assertion would have been silently vacuous |
| One canonical session attribute name, published as a constant | Let each service name the attribute locally | The gateway wrote `sessionContext` while every other service read `carddemoSessionContext`, so a signed-on session authorized nothing through the gateway. The name now lives in one place and every producer and consumer imports it | A literal string reintroduced anywhere would silently break authorization again; a gateway test asserts the frozen key name |
| Live sessions revoked on user delete, on a `SEC-USR-TYPE` change, and on a credential change; a rename revokes nothing | Let a stale session expire naturally; or revoke on every update | On the 3270 the next transaction re-read `USRSEC`, so maintenance took effect immediately. A cached session context would otherwise keep a deleted user signed on, or a demoted administrator's authority alive, for the remainder of the timeout. Revocation deliberately runs while the unit of work is still open: if the commit then fails, an already-revoked user is merely asked to sign on again, whereas revoking after commit leaves a window in which stale authority is still honoured. A rename changes no authority and no credential, so it does not disturb a live session | Accepted: a same-instant race can revoke for a change that then rolls back — the safe direction. Revocation is best-effort by design; a Redis error is logged and never blocks user maintenance `[app/cbl/COUSR02C.cbl; app/cbl/COUSR03C.cbl]` |
| Principal→session index maintained explicitly in Redis (`<namespace>:principal:<userId>`) | Use Spring Session's `FindByIndexNameSessionRepository` | The indexed repository requires Redis keyspace-notification configuration (`CONFIG SET`) that a hardened or managed Redis commonly refuses, which would fail startup. The explicit index needs no server configuration, is written at sign-on, expires with the session timeout, and deletes revoked sessions through the session repository so the correct namespace and serializer are always used | Index entries can outlive a naturally expired session until their TTL elapses; deleting an already-gone session id is a harmless no-op |
| Account lockout after 5 consecutive failures for 15 minutes, keyed on the upper-cased `SEC-USR-ID`, held in memory per instance | An unbounded retry allowance; a shared Redis counter; exponential backoff | RACF revoked an id after N failures, and nothing replaced that control: 30 wrong passwords ran at full speed and the 31st correct attempt succeeded. The counter is in memory on purpose — it adds no infrastructure dependency and cannot fail *open* when an external store is unavailable — and the tracked-id map is bounded so it cannot become a memory-exhaustion vector. A locked id is refused even when the correct credential is finally presented, and the refusal carries the legacy "Unable to verify the User ..." text with **no** `Retry-After`: like the RACF revoke it replaces, the control does not advertise when guessing may resume | Per-instance counters mean N replicas tolerate N × 5 attempts before every replica locks; the gateway sign-on budget bounds the aggregate. A legitimate user who mistypes five times waits out the window (no administrator unlock exists, unlike RACF) `[app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl]` |
| Rate limiting applied ONLY at the api-gateway, per source address, with a global budget (600/min) and a tighter `/auth/` budget (60/min) | Apply the same per-address budget inside every service | Behind the gateway every request carries the *gateway's* address, so an address-keyed budget on an internal service throttles all users collectively and would breach the AAP 150-concurrent-user target. The gateway is the only place the client address is authentic, and forwarded headers are deliberately NOT trusted because they are attacker-controlled without a validated proxy chain. The topology-independent brute-force control is the per-account lockout above. Both budgets are stacked in one chain, so each filter instance scopes its own once-per-request marker by path prefix — sharing the default marker silently disabled the second budget entirely | Many users behind a single NAT address share one budget; both limits are configurable (`carddemo.rate-limit.gateway-requests-per-minute`, `.signon-requests-per-minute`). Counters are per instance, so a replicated gateway multiplies the effective budget |
| CSRF enforced once, at the api-gateway, using cookie double-submit; disabled on the internal services; `/auth/**` exempt | Enforce CSRF on every service; or disable it everywhere | The gateway is the only browser-facing surface, so it is the only place ambient-authority requests originate. Enforcing it again downstream would demand a token those services never issue — which is exactly what made every administrator write fail. The token is an always-issued `XSRF-TOKEN` cookie echoed as `X-XSRF-TOKEN` (the axios defaults, so the SPA needs no bespoke code), with a token endpoint for a cold start. Because CSRF is enabled, Spring Security's logout matcher accepts `POST /logout` only, closing the cross-site forced-logout vector. `/auth/**` is exempt because it is unauthenticated and carries explicit credentials rather than ambient authority: a fresh client cannot yet hold a token, sign-on rotates the session id so login CSRF cannot fix a session, and requiring a token there turned a genuine `405`/`415` into a misleading `403` | Internal services rely on the gateway for CSRF; anything that exposes them directly to a browser must re-enable it. The exemption is scoped to the sign-on prefix only |
| The SPA half of the CSRF chain is satisfied by the shipped same-origin topology, with no bespoke client code | Set `withXSRFToken: true` on the shared axios instance; or proxy nothing and accept a cross-origin SPA | The chain was verified link by link rather than assumed. The gateway issues the token through `CookieCsrfTokenRepository.withHttpOnlyFalse()` with cookie `XSRF-TOKEN` and header `X-XSRF-TOKEN` — both the axios defaults — so the value is script-readable and a `CsrfCookieMaterializingFilter` guarantees it is present on every response. Compose builds the SPA with `VITE_API_BASE_URL: /api`, a relative path that `frontend/nginx.conf` reverse-proxies from `location /api/` to `api-gateway:8080`, so every REST call is same-origin. axios 1.18.1 attaches the header when `withXSRFToken` is exactly `true`, or when `withXSRFToken` is unset and `isURLSameOrigin(url)` holds; the shared client sets `withCredentials: true` and leaves `withXSRFToken` unset, so the same-origin branch applies and the header is attached automatically. Adding `withXSRFToken: true` was rejected because it would send the token to whatever absolute origin a build happened to point at | Dev-only caveat: running the Vite dev server on `:5173` against an ABSOLUTE gateway URL (the `frontend/Dockerfile` fallback `ARG VITE_API_BASE_URL=http://localhost:8080`) is cross-origin, so axios attaches no token and writes return `403`. Point the dev server at `/api` behind a proxy, or set `withXSRFToken` for that profile only. The shipped Compose and Kubernetes topologies are unaffected |
| Session cookie: `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict`, `Path=/`, and `Secure` in every non-local profile | `SameSite=Lax` without `Secure` | `Lax` still sends the cookie on top-level cross-site GET navigations; `Strict` does not, and CardDemo has no cross-site entry point that needs it. `Secure` is profile-gated so plain-HTTP local development still works while any deployed profile refuses to send the cookie over cleartext | A deployment that terminates TLS elsewhere must keep the deployed profile active for `Secure` to apply |
| No session is created for an unauthenticated request | Let the container create one per request | Every anonymous request minted and persisted a Redis session, an unbounded-growth channel for an unauthenticated caller. All chains are `STATELESS`, the saved-request cache is disabled (a REST API never replays a saved request), and the restoration filter uses an existing session only | A flow that genuinely needs pre-authentication state would have to introduce it deliberately |
| Service-level authentication enforced on EVERY service port, including a direct port reached by bypassing the gateway; network topology is never the control | Rely on the compose/Kubernetes network so only the gateway is reachable | Six services shipped with no `spring-boot-starter-security` and no filter chain at all, and a direct-port (gateway-bypass) probe proved the consequence: an unauthenticated `POST /billpay` zeroed a real account balance (194.00 → 0.00) and an unauthenticated `POST /transactions` inserted a financial row, both reachable from the host over the compose bridge even though only the gateway publishes a host port. Topology is now treated as defence in depth only: each of the nine services carries the shared session-derived chain, so an anonymous request to any port is refused before business logic runs — asserted per service by a filters-on `SecurityContractIT` that proves the service collaborator is never touched | Accepted residual: the services still listen on a routable interface in a local compose run (only the gateway publishes a host port). Kubernetes adds a default-deny `NetworkPolicy`, but it must be APPLIED to have effect, and it is not a substitute for the service-level control above |
| Object-level authorization intentionally not scoped to the caller: any signed-on user may read any account, card, or transaction | Restrict each read to the caller's own customer records | `USRSEC` (`CSUSR01Y`) carries only id, name, password, and type — there is NO user→customer linkage anywhere in the legacy data model, and the 3270 screens accepted any account or card number from any signed-on operator. Introducing caller scoping would invent a relationship the source does not have and would change observable behavior, which AAP §0.7.1/§0.7.6 forbid | Accepted and recorded: enumeration of account, card, and transaction identifiers by an authenticated user is legacy-faithful. Authentication is now mandatory on every read, every access is audited, and adding scoping would require a new user→customer mapping as an explicit product decision `[app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy; app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl; app/cbl/COCRDSLC.cbl]` |
| Full card number returned in responses; masked in every log path | Mask the PAN in responses too; or leave logs unmasked | AAP §0.6.7 names SSN, government id, and CVV as the fields to mask — the PAN is deliberately not in that list, because `COCRDSL` and `COTRN01` display all sixteen digits and the DTO field contract is frozen. A log file, by contrast, has no legacy analogue and must not retain a PAN, so `GlobalExceptionHandler` masks both the logged path and the logged message (`SensitiveDataMasker.maskPan`) while the response still reports the path the caller asked for | Responses carrying a PAN rely on TLS, the `no-store` cache directive, and mandatory authentication. The log-side guarantee is structural rather than asserted: `GlobalExceptionHandler` exposes the request URI to a logging statement only through `loggedPath`, which is `SensitiveDataMasker.maskPan(extractPath(request))`, and a backend message only through `loggedMessage`; `extractPath` has exactly one caller, `loggedPath` itself. A future logging statement that reaches for the raw path would therefore have to reintroduce that call deliberately `[carddemo-common/src/main/java/com/carddemo/common/config/GlobalExceptionHandler.java; app/cbl/COCRDSLC.cbl; app/cbl/COTRN01C.cbl]` |
| All ten seeded users share the password `PASSWORD` — an accepted, demo-only risk | Seed distinct random passwords; or ship no users at all | The legacy `USRSEC` fixture is the reference data the 17 UI workflows are exercised against, and the AAP treats the seeded users as test data for a local/demo stack. The credential is now stored only as a BCrypt hash (never plaintext), but a single shared password across five administrators and five users is a real weakness and is recorded here rather than left implicit — it contradicted an earlier claim in §6 that no convenient defaults are committed | ACCEPTED for local and demo profiles ONLY. Any shared, staging, or production environment must rotate or disable these accounts before exposure; the seed script is the single place to change `[app/jcl/DUSRSECJ.jcl; app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy]` |

## 4. Behavioral Fidelity & Presentation

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Reject codes 100–103 reproduced exactly (`TransactionPostingJob`) | Simplify or renumber the reject codes | `CBTRN02C` validates in a fixed order — cross-reference then account, short-circuiting when the earlier lookup fails — and emits `100` "INVALID CARD NUMBER FOUND", `101` "ACCOUNT RECORD NOT FOUND", `102` over-limit (computed as `WS-TEMP-BAL = ACCT-CURR-CYC-CREDIT - ACCT-CURR-CYC-DEBIT + DALYTRAN-AMT` compared to `ACCT-CREDIT-LIMIT`, NOT against the current balance), and `103` account-expired (`ACCT-EXPIRAION-DATE >= DALYTRAN-ORIG-TS (1:10)`); the posting job preserves the identical code-to-condition mapping and the 430-byte reject record (350-byte transaction image + 80-byte trailer: `PIC 9(04)` reason + `PIC X(76)` description) | Downstream reject consumers depend on the exact codes and byte layout `[app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L176-182,L371-397,L380-421]` |
| Pseudo-conversational COMMAREA → stateless REST + externalized `SessionContext` | Sticky server-side HTTP sessions | CICS carried cross-screen state in the COMMAREA (`COCOM01Y`) and transferred control with `XCTL`; the target uses stateless controllers with a `SessionContext` held in Spring Session (Redis) / JWT claims, and replaces `XCTL` program transfer with a gateway route or HTTP redirect plus a role check. The context 88-levels `CDEMO-PGM-ENTER` (VALUE `0`) / `CDEMO-PGM-REENTER` (VALUE `1`) and `CDEMO-LAST-MAP PIC X(7)` are preserved as `SessionContext` state | Session-store dependency; observable navigation behavior preserved `[app/cpy/COCOM01Y.cpy:L18-45; app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L231-237]` |
| Date validation `CEEDAYS` / Lillian → `java.time` STRICT parsing (`DateUtil`) | Lenient date parsing | `CSUTLDTC` validates dates by calling the Language Environment `CEEDAYS` service and converting to a Lillian day count (`OUTPUT-LILLIAN PIC S9(9) BINARY`); `DateUtil` uses `LocalDate.parse` with a `DateTimeFormatter` set to `ResolverStyle.STRICT`, treats severity zero as valid, and maps each feedback category (`FC-INVALID-DATE`, `FC-INVALID-ERA`, `FC-INVALID-MONTH`, `FC-BAD-PIC-STRING`) to an equivalent validation result. To match the Lillian calendar range it rejects dates before the Gregorian epoch `1582-10-15` and rejects year zero / zero-padded-out-of-range input; it returns the result in the 15-character contract width and takes an injectable `java.time.Clock` so "today"-relative checks are deterministic in tests | Edge-case date semantics (pre-1582, year zero, padding, clock) must match `[app/cbl/CSUTLDTC.cbl:L2,L60-77]` |
| `ReportService` computes the Monthly/Yearly range from an injectable `java.time.Clock` | Keep the two direct `LocalDate.now()` calls and let tests recompute the expected range | `CORPT00C` derives the Monthly range as the first/last day of the current month and the Yearly range as the current calendar year. Reading "today" from an injectable clock leaves runtime behavior byte-identical — the Spring constructor delegates to `Clock.systemDefaultZone()`, which is exactly what `LocalDate.now()` uses — while allowing the tests to freeze time on a month-end, leap-February and year-end instant and assert literal dates. Without it a test can only re-run the service's own algorithm (a tautology that also races midnight). Mirrors the same pattern already used by `BillPaymentMapper` and `DateUtil` | Public constructor surface widened by one overload; the container constructor is marked `@Autowired` so bean creation stays unambiguous `[app/cbl/CORPT00C.cbl]` |
| `ReportPage` publishes the line-24 legend as `ENTER=Continue` + **`F3=Back`**, taken from the mapset rather than the `F3=Exit` wording of the task brief's illustrative `setChrome` snippet | Use `F3=Exit` as the snippet shows; or normalise every screen to one shared legend | `app/bms/CORPT00.bms:L222-226` declares `INITIAL='ENTER=Continue  F3=Back'` (`LENGTH=23`, which the two captions plus the two-space gap match exactly), and the same brief twice orders "confirm exact captions against BMS FKEYS" / "Match exact PF captions from `CORPT00.bms`". The 17 mapsets carry 14 DIFFERENT legends (`ENTER=Process F3=Exit`, `ENTER=Sign-on  F3=Exit`, `ENTER=Fetch  F3=Save&&Exit  F4=Clear  F5=Save`, …), so per-screen wording — not a shared legend — is the legacy contract, and the spec-literal fidelity rule makes the mapset the frozen source | A caller expecting the snippet's `F3=Exit` string sees `F3=Back`; the key ACTION is identical (PF3 → main menu, `XCTL PROGRAM('COMEN01C')`) `[app/bms/CORPT00.bms; app/cbl/CORPT00C.cbl:L187-189]` |
| An informational line-23 message renders GREEN (`.errorBanner[role="status"]`), while errors stay RED | Leave both branches red as `.errorBanner` did; or colour the message from a page-supplied prop | Eight online programs move `DFHGREEN` into `ERRMSGC` before sending a success message (`COADM01C`, `COBIL00C`, `COMEN01C`, `CORPT00C`, `COTRN02C`, `COUSR01C`–`COUSR03C`), so GREEN is the legacy attribute for that line and red for it was a rendering defect: `ReportPage`'s `Monthly report submitted for printing ...` displayed in error red. Keying the rule off the `role="status"` branch that `ErrorBanner` already emits keeps the decision in the stylesheet, needs no component API change, and cannot affect the `role="alert"` branch | Applies to every screen's informational line at once — which is the intent, since all eight programs share the convention; verified in the browser (info `rgb(51,255,102)`, error `rgb(255,59,48)`) `[app/cbl/CORPT00C.cbl:L448; app/cbl/COUSR01C.cbl:L254]` |
| `ReportPage` guards the report-job launch with a synchronous `useRef` in-flight flag, not only with the render-derived `PFKeyDef.enabled` | Rely on `enabled: !submitting` and the button's `disabled` attribute alone; debounce the handler; or accept duplicates as the backend's problem | Runtime testing proved the render-derived guard insufficient: `PFKeyBar`'s document `keydown` listener closes over the previous render's key array, so TWO ENTER activations inside ONE JavaScript task both reached the handler and both issued `POST /reports` — two batch jobs from one confirmation. The ref is set before the `await` and cleared in `finally`, so the second activation returns immediately; re-testing showed 11 ENTER activations producing exactly 4 POSTs, one per confirmation, including a three-clicks-in-one-task variant | A guard with no legacy analogue (CICS locked the keyboard for the duration of the transaction, which is the behaviour being restored); the flag is per-screen state, so it cannot wedge across navigations `[app/cbl/CORPT00C.cbl:L462-510; app/cpy/CSSTRPFY.cpy]` |
| `frontend/src/setupTests.ts` polyfills `TextEncoder`/`TextDecoder` from `node:util` behind a `typeof` guard | Add `testEnvironmentOptions.customExportConditions` to the Jest config; polyfill per test file; or switch the test environment | `jest-environment-jsdom` omits both globals, so `react-router-dom` threw `ReferenceError: TextEncoder is not defined` at import time and EVERY routed page test failed to load — not just the report screen. The shared setup file already runs before each suite and is the narrowest place that fixes all 17 page suites at once; the `typeof` guard makes it inert wherever the globals exist, so no existing behaviour changes (the 68 pre-existing tests still pass) | Test-infrastructure only, never bundled into the application; a future jsdom that ships the globals simply skips the assignment `[frontend/package.json]` |
| BMS 3270 mapsets → plain React 19 + CSS (no component library) | Adopt a named component library / design system | The prompt names no component library or design system, so each 24×80 mapset becomes a React function component using plain React with CSS, preserving field labels, tab order, password masking, and PF-key actions (ENTER submit, PF3 exit, PF7 / PF8 paging) | Visual parity is hand-maintained `[app/bms/]` |
| `AccountUpdatePage` binds each date to ONE `YYYY-MM-DD` entry field with a visible `(YYYY-MM-DD)` hint, instead of the mapset's separate YEAR(4) / MON(2) / DAY(2) sub-fields | Reproduce the three `OPNYEAR` / `OPNMON` / `OPNDAY`-style sub-fields per date (four dates, twelve inputs) and recompose them before submission | `COACTUP.bms` splits Opened, Expiry, Reissue and Date of birth into three `UNPROT` sub-fields each purely because a 3270 field cannot carry an input mask; the wire contract is already a single `X(10)` `YYYY-MM-DD` string on every one of them (`acctOpenDate`, `acctExpiraionDate`, `acctReissueDate`, `custDobYyyyMmDd`). One field per date submits the identical payload, keeps the four TURQUOISE captions byte-exact, and removes the recomposition step that could emit a malformed date; the `(YYYY-MM-DD)` hint replaces the visual `-` separators the mapset drew between the sub-fields. `SSN:` and `Phone 1:` / `Phone 2:` follow the same rule — one field bound to the `X(9)` / `X(15)` DTO string, with the positional `(aaa)ppp-llll` phone form preserved so the server's area-code and line-number edits see the same slices | Presentation-only: no label, message, or wire value changes. A user must type the separators that the sub-fields supplied implicitly, so the client reproduces the legacy blank / invalid edits (`… must be supplied.`, `… is not valid`) against the whole value rather than per sub-field; the Year / Month / Day sub-field messages of `CSUTLDPY` are unreachable from this screen and remain the server's `[app/bms/COACTUP.bms; app/cpy-bms/COACTUP.CPY; app/cpy/CSUTLDPY.cpy; frontend/src/types/account.ts]` |
| `AccountUpdatePage` lays out its fields with page-scoped BEM class names that carry no CSS of their own, and reproduces each 3270 field's column span with the HTML `size` attribute (equal to the field's `maxLength`) | Add page-scoped rules to the shared `frontend/src/index.css`; ship a per-page stylesheet next to the component; give every field its own row | `index.css` is the single global stylesheet the whole SPA shares and all 17 screens were authored in parallel, so per-page rules appended to it collide on reconciliation; a per-page stylesheet contradicts that single-stylesheet design. The precedent is already set — `Layout`'s own `screenBody` class has no rule either. `size` closes most of the gap without any CSS at all: a text input defaults to ~20 characters regardless of its `maxLength`, which is what pushed rows past the 80-column frame; binding `size` to the legacy width makes each box exactly as wide as its BMS `LENGTH` (measured: `size=1` → 27px, `size=15` → 167px, `size=50` → 517px) and brings 12 of the 14 field rows onto a single line | Two rows still reflow onto a second line — the three 25-character name fields (1168px of content) and the `Phone 2:` / `EFT Account Id:` / `Primary Card Holder Y/N:` row (812px) against 749px of usable width. Verified in Chrome: zero horizontal overflow (`scrollWidth == innerWidth == 1280`), zero elements past the viewport edge, no clipped or overlapping content, and every caption still associated with its input. The BMS row grouping is kept in the DOM rather than re-cut to dodge the unstyled classes, because the mapset row structure is the authoritative source `[app/bms/COACTUP.bms; frontend/src/index.css]` |
| The account-update function keys published into the shared `Layout` chrome dispatch through a handler ref rather than being re-published on every keystroke | Publish the state-dependent handlers directly and let the chrome effect re-run whenever any entry field changes | `ENTER` / `F3` / `F5` / `F12` live in the shell's `pfKeys`, one React state level ABOVE the page, so a handler that closes over entry state only reaches the key bar after a second render round. A key activated between those rounds ran against the pre-load state — observably, `F5` immediately after the account read reported `Account number not provided` because the published closure still held `version === null`. Dispatching through a ref makes the four `onActivate` callbacks identity-stable and always current, and collapses one chrome publish per keystroke into one per message change | The ref is written in an effect, so a key activated before the first commit would use the initial handler set — unreachable, since the key bar is only rendered after that commit `[app/bms/COACTUP.bms; frontend/src/components/Layout.tsx]` |
| `frontend/src/setupTests.ts` installs the `TextEncoder` / `TextDecoder` globals | Polyfill them per test file; pin an older `react-router`; move the routed pages behind a mocked router in every test | `jsdom` exposes neither global, and `react-router` 7 dereferences `TextEncoder` while resolving route data, so ANY test that renders a routed page failed the whole suite with `ReferenceError: TextEncoder is not defined` — every one of the 17 screens is routed. The shared setup file already referenced by `setupFilesAfterEnv` is the one place the fix applies to all of them; the globals are assigned only when absent | Test-environment only: no production code path reads these globals, and a future `jsdom` that supplies them natively is left untouched `[frontend/package.json; frontend/src/pages/]` |

## 5. Source-Inventory Reconciliation

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Program count reconciled to 28 `.cbl` / `.CBL` programs (17 online + 10 batch + 1 utility) | Accept the prompt's "33 programs (17 online + 16 batch)" | Repository evidence is authoritative: 17 online `CO*` + 10 batch `CB*` + 1 utility (`CSUTLDTC`) = 28; the "16 batch" figure most plausibly refers to batch JCL job streams (29 present), not batch COBOL programs | Mismap risk if the stale count were used `[app/cbl/; app/jcl/]` |
| BMS map names use 7-character base names with no trailing `M` (e.g. `COSGN00.bms`) | The prompt's `COSGN00M` / `COMEN01M` names | The actual mapset files are 7 characters; corroborated by `CDEMO-LAST-MAP PIC X(7)`, which reserves exactly seven characters for a map name | Wrong identifiers would break DTO / route wiring `[app/bms/; app/cpy/COCOM01Y.cpy:L43]` |
| Preserve source misspellings `ACCT-EXPIRAION-DATE` and `CARD-EXPIRAION-DATE` | Silently "correct" the spelling to `ACCT-EXPIRATION-DATE` / `CARD-EXPIRATION-DATE` | Spec-literal fidelity: frozen-contract identifiers are preserved character-for-character; both fields are spelled without the second `T` throughout the source and program logic | Any "fix" breaks byte-identical field contracts `[app/cpy/CVACT01Y.cpy:L11; app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy:L9; app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L414]` |
