| A `CORPT00C` report request submits the transaction-detail report to batch-service through `BatchJobClient`, forwarding the caller's session | Keep launching the statement job; move `transactionDetailReportJob` into reporting-service; duplicate the job | AAP §0.4.4 maps `CORPT00C` + `TRANREPT.prc` to the transaction-detail report, but a report request launched `statementGenerationJob` instead, so the correct job had no caller. The job belongs to batch-service (AAP §0.4.4 `DataManagement*Job`), so relocating or duplicating it would contradict the mapping; a cross-service submission preserves ownership. The caller's `SESSION` cookie is forwarded so the launch is authorized as the signed-on user, which the shared Redis session makes meaningful in either service | A report request now depends on batch-service being reachable; that dependency is surfaced as the frozen submission-failure message rather than hidden |
| Job submission is synchronous while execution stays asynchronous; `@Async` was removed from the `@Configuration` launcher | Keep `@Async` and inspect the returned future; block the caller until the job finishes | The launcher was an `@Async` method on a `@Configuration` class returning a `CompletableFuture` that the caller discarded, so neither a refused submission nor a failed run could ever reach the user — a report request returned 200 while its job FAILED. Submission now completes on the calling thread (so a refusal raises the frozen `Unable to Write TDQ (JOBS)...`) while the accepted run proceeds on a bounded, correlation-propagating executor and its outcome is readable from the execution-status endpoint. Blocking the caller for the whole run would have breached the 200 ms online response target | Execution failure is observable by polling rather than in the submission response, matching the legacy submit-and-return semantics |
| `POST /reports/statements` is the entry point for the `CREASTMT` statement job stream | Leave statement generation reachable only from a report request | Re-pointing the report request at the transaction-detail report would otherwise have left the statement job stream with no caller. A dedicated endpoint keeps the two legacy paths distinct — `CORPT00C` submits the transaction report, `CREASTMT` generates statements — and accepts the two output names (`stmtFile`, `htmlFile`, the `STMTFILE`/`HTMLFILE` DD names) and nothing else: `CREASTMT` carries no `PARM`, so the endpoint no longer accepts a report type or a date window | An additional endpoint to secure; it is covered by the same session-based authorization as the report request |
| The launch executor is bounded (`setConcurrencyLimit(4)`) | Leave `SimpleAsyncTaskExecutor` unbounded | `SimpleAsyncTaskExecutor` pools no threads, so every submission spawned a fresh thread; a burst of launches could exhaust memory and saturate the JDBC pool. The legacy initiator ran a bounded number of concurrent job streams | A fifth concurrent submission queues rather than starting immediately |
| `JobInstance` creation uses `REPEATABLE_READ` isolation instead of Spring Batch's default `SERIALIZABLE` | Keep `SERIALIZABLE`; retry the launch on a transient serialization failure | PostgreSQL enforces `SERIALIZABLE` with Serializable Snapshot Isolation, which aborted one of two CONCURRENT launches — even for different job parameters — with "could not serialize access due to read/write dependencies among transactions", surfacing a raw SQL error instead of starting the job. `REPEATABLE_READ` removes that false conflict while the unique constraint on `BATCH_JOB_INSTANCE.JOB_KEY` remains the authoritative guard against a genuinely duplicated instance | Two launches racing on the SAME job key now surface a duplicate-key error instead of one blocking the other |
| Correlation ids are propagated into async and job threads by a shared `TaskDecorator`, and the launching thread's MDC is cleared afterwards | Accept empty `correlationId` on job-thread log lines; set the MDC only in the request filter | The shared web observability configuration was imported by nobody and four services had no correlation filter at all, so no log line carried a correlation id and a job's lines could not be tied back to the request that launched it. The decorator captures the id on the submitting thread and runs the task inside it; the launcher clears the id it seeded in a `finally` block so a pooled request thread cannot leak one submission's id onto an unrelated later request | The decorator must be applied to every executor that runs work on behalf of a request |
| Boot 4's modularized auto-configuration artefacts are declared explicitly (`spring-boot-session-data-redis`, `spring-boot-flyway`) | Rely on the technology artefact alone (`org.springframework.session:spring-session-data-redis`) | Spring Boot 4 moved auto-configuration into per-technology modules, so the session artefact alone leaves `spring.session.*` inert and never registers `SessionRepositoryFilter`; `HttpSession` then falls back to container-local storage. batch-service rejected every authenticated caller with 401 until the module was declared — diagnosed by diffing the fat jars against a working service | Every service adding a Boot-integrated technology must declare the matching `spring-boot-<tech>` module |
| `UserDetailsServiceAutoConfiguration` is excluded in all eight secured services | Leave it enabled; define a real in-memory `UserDetailsService` | These services authenticate from the shared CardDemo session, never from an in-memory user. Left enabled, Boot generates a random development credential and logs it at WARN on every start — both a usable in-memory account and an unrequested secret in the log. The exclusion was extended to the five downstream services and batch-service when the security starter was added to them | None; no service resolves a principal from an in-memory user |
| An unreachable routed upstream is reported as `503` with `Retry-After`, not `500` | Leave the default 500; add a circuit breaker with a fallback route | Routing raises `ResourceAccessException` when an upstream refuses the connection; unhandled it surfaced as 500, telling the caller the gateway itself is broken and is not retryable by convention, when the gateway is healthy and the condition is transient. A gateway-scoped handler ordered ahead of the shared handler maps it to 503 with the shared JSON error contract; the upstream host, port and exception detail are logged, never returned | A genuine gateway fault on a routed path is now reported as 503; a circuit breaker with per-route fallbacks remains a production follow-up |
| Generated batch artefacts and local run evidence are gitignored, with the legacy ASCII fixtures re-included explicitly | Leave the code's own default output names untracked-but-unignored | Every default output name in `JobSchedulingConfig`, `RejectFileItemWriter` and `StatementGenerationJob` is a runtime artefact that a job creates in the working directory, so any of them could be staged by accident. The patterns are copied from the constants that produce them — `acctdata-report*.txt`, `carddata-report*.txt`, `cardxref-report*.txt`, `custdata-report*.txt`, `tcatbal-report*.txt`, `tran-detail-report*.txt`, `combined-transactions*.txt`, `dalyrejs*.txt`, `statements.txt`, `statements.html`, `dailytran*.txt` — and each is ignored at any depth, together with the batch output root and the local evidence directory. An earlier revision of this entry claimed the same coverage while listing six INVENTED stems (`trandet*`, `catbal*`, `acctrept*`, `cardrept*`, `xrefrept*`, `custrept*`) that matched none of the names the code actually writes, so six of the eleven artefacts were not ignored at all; the patterns and this rationale were corrected together and every name re-verified with `git check-ignore -v`. Because the patterns match at any depth, `app/data/**` is re-included by negation so the committed `app/data/ASCII/dailytran.txt` seed fixture — which shares a name with the generated feed — stays trackable (verified: not ignored and tracked) | Deliberately committing a sample artefact under one of these names requires `git add -f` |
## 9. Schema Ownership, Runtime Configuration & Deployment Topology

Decisions taken while making the delivered stack actually run and actually be observable. Every row below was reached by reproducing a concrete runtime failure, so each records what was observed as well as what was chosen. These are the entries a reader needs in order to understand how the schema comes into existence, how the services find each other, how much of the database each one may consume, and how a request is traced and correlated end to end.

### 9.1 Database schema ownership and Flyway strategy

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| ONE shared Flyway migration set, owned by `carddemo-common` at `classpath:db/migration`, applied by whichever service starts first | Per-service migrations owning only that service's tables (the original arrangement); or an external migration job with Flyway disabled in every service | Every one of the eight JPA services declares `@EntityScan("com.carddemo.common.domain")` with `ddl-auto: validate`, so EVERY service validates the FULL entity set at startup, not just "its own" tables. Per-service ownership therefore cannot work in any start order: a service that owns two tables still fails validation against the other nine. Consolidating the DDL and the seed data into one set that every service carries makes the schema complete no matter which container wins the race, and the Flyway history table makes the losers no-ops | Concurrent first starts contend on the history table; Flyway's own locking serialises them. Verified on a clean volume: all nine services come up healthy in a single `docker compose up` with no ordering constraint, and the schema contains all 11 business tables |
| `spring-boot-flyway` (the Boot 4 auto-configuration module) added as an explicit dependency to every JPA service | Rely on `flyway-core` alone, as the original poms did | Spring Boot 4 split auto-configuration out of the monolithic `spring-boot-autoconfigure` jar into per-technology modules. With only `flyway-core` on the classpath the `spring.flyway.*` properties parse but nothing acts on them, so migrations silently never ran — the direct cause of the crash-loop on a clean volume | A missing Boot 4 auto-configuration module fails SILENTLY rather than loudly; the same class of defect was found for tracing (§9.4) and for three property renames (§9.6) |
| `ddl-auto: validate` retained in every service | `update` or `create` | Validation makes a schema/entity divergence a startup failure instead of a silent runtime error, and it guarantees the migrations — not Hibernate — remain the single source of truth for the schema | Any entity change without a matching migration blocks startup, which is the intended behaviour |
| `baseline-on-migrate: false` retained | `true` | `true` would let a service silently adopt a partially-migrated database as its baseline and skip the DDL it needs. With a single shared set there is no legacy schema to baseline over | An existing database not created by these migrations must be migrated manually |
| Spring Batch metadata DDL shipped as `V5__batch_metadata.sql` in the shared set | Let Spring Batch initialise its own schema; or ship it in `batch-service` only | THREE services run Spring Batch jobs (`batch-service`, `transaction-service`, `reporting-service`), so batch-service-local DDL would impose a hidden start-order requirement on the other two. Boot 4.1 also removed the entire `spring.batch.jdbc.*` property group, so `initialize-schema` no longer exists as a lever | The DDL must track the Spring Batch major version; it is the canonical PostgreSQL script for Batch 6 |
| Reference and test seed data delivered as `V2__seed_reference_data.sql` and `V3__seed_test_data.sql` | Seed from the ASCII fixtures at runtime | AAP §0.4.5 specifies exactly 7 transaction types, 18 categories and 51 disclosure groups, derived from `app/data/ASCII/{trantype,trancatg,discgrp}.txt`; expressing them as migrations makes the counts deterministic and verifiable | Verified at runtime: `select count(*)` returns 7 / 18 / 51 on a clean volume |
| The reporting-service test profile keeps Flyway DISABLED and provisions only what each test asserts on | Enable the shared migration set for tests so the container matches production | `V3` seeds 50 customers, accounts, cards and cross-references. `StatementGenerationJobIT` deletes its way to a known state before seeding, and would have to delete around foreign keys owned by tables it does not assert on. Each IT instead registers exactly the schema it needs via `@DynamicPropertySource` | The test container's schema is narrower than production's; the deployed configuration is exercised by the clean-volume compose run instead |
| `ReportingServiceApplicationIT` uses its OWN Testcontainers database (`jdbc:tc:postgresql:18:///carddemo_reporting_appit`) | Share the one container the other IT uses | `ContainerDatabaseDriver` caches exactly one container per JDBC URL for the whole JVM (proved: a single "Creating container for image: postgres:18" line for the entire failsafe run). Both ITs apply the same create-only Spring Batch DDL, so sharing would make whichever Spring context started second fail on already-existing tables | One extra throwaway container per build, roughly 1.6 s to start |

### 9.2 Connection budget and session lifecycle

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Explicit HikariCP sizing per service — `maximum-pool-size` 8 and `minimum-idle` 2 for the seven online services, 12 and 1 for `batch-service`, `idle-timeout` 300 s, all env-overridable | Leave HikariCP at its defaults | HikariCP defaults to `maximumPoolSize` 10 AND `minimumIdle` equal to it, so eight services held 80 of PostgreSQL's 100 connections **while completely idle** and starting any two further containers was refused with "FATAL: sorry, too many clients already". Sizing for the AAP §0.7.1 target of 150 concurrent users needs far less: the whole deployment's ceiling is now 7×8+12 = 68 and its measured steady state is 15. `batch-service` gets the larger pool because its chunk-oriented jobs hold a connection per chunk transaction | Under-sizing would queue requests instead of failing fast; the bounded `connection-timeout` (3 s) makes that visible, and `hikaricp_connections_pending` / `_timeout_total` are on the dashboard. Measured after the change: 15 idle backends (7.5% of the limit) held steady across 12 consecutive samples, and four extra service replicas ran healthily at 28/200 |
| PostgreSQL `max_connections` raised from the image default 100 to 200, in compose and in `k8s/deployment-postgres.yaml` | Leave it at 100 | Even with bounded pools, 100 leaves no operational headroom for replicas, `psql` sessions and monitoring. 200 keeps the steady-state footprint under 10% of the limit and admits two replicas of every service | Shared memory scales with `max_connections`, so the Kubernetes postgres container's memory request/limit was raised 256Mi→512Mi and 512Mi→1Gi to match |
| A REST API creates NO HTTP session for an unauthenticated caller: `NullRequestCache` on both security chains, and controllers read the session with `getSession(false)` | Accept the default `HttpSessionRequestCache` and inject `HttpSession` as a controller parameter | Injecting `HttpSession` forces `request.getSession()`, which CREATES a session on every anonymous business call, and `HttpSessionRequestCache` saves a session on every denied request so the caller can be redirected after login — a redirect a REST API never performs. Together they minted a Redis key per request; QA counted 258. Controllers now read an existing session only, and write the COMMAREA context back only if one already exists | A caller who has not signed on gets no server-side state, which is correct. Measured after the change: a 150-request mixed anonymous/denied burst created ZERO new `carddemo:session:*` keys, while a genuine `POST /auth/signon` still creates exactly one and a nine-request authenticated workflow reuses one stable id |
| One session attribute key, `carddemoSessionContext`, shared by every service | Let each service choose its own attribute name | The `api-gateway` used a private `sessionContext` key while all eight services and `SessionContextAuthenticationFilter` used `carddemoSessionContext`, so the gateway read a phantom empty COMMAREA and wrote a second redundant copy into the shared Redis session on every menu request | Verified: `HKEYS` on a live session shows exactly one context field |
| The sign-on endpoint creates its session ONLY after the credential verifies: `AuthenticationController.signon` and `AuthenticationService.signon` take `HttpServletRequest`, and the session is obtained with `getSession(true)` at the point the COMMAREA context is published | Keep the `HttpSession` controller parameter (the sign-on route is, after all, the one route that legitimately mints a session) | Spring resolves an `HttpSession` handler argument EAGERLY with `getSession(true)` BEFORE the handler body runs, so the session existed before the password was ever checked. Each of the three `401` branches then returned having already allocated Redis storage, making `POST /auth/signon` a session-churn amplifier: an unauthenticated caller could mint unbounded sessions by simply guessing passwords. This is the same defect class as the anonymous-business-call churn above, on the one route that had been assumed exempt. Measured before the change: 25 wrong-password plus 10 unknown-user attempts created 35 `carddemo:session:sessions:*` keys | The service now depends on `HttpServletRequest` rather than `HttpSession`, a marginally wider servlet type, in exchange for control over WHEN the session is created; the service still publishes the context itself, so the AAP's COMMAREA-replacement ownership is unchanged. Measured after the change: the same 35 failed attempts create ZERO sessions, one successful sign-on still creates exactly one, all four `401` reasons are byte-identical, and the nine-endpoint authenticated workflow plus both role-gated `403`s are unaffected. The four failure unit tests now additionally assert `verify(httpRequest, never()).getSession(anyBoolean())`, so a future reintroduction of the eager parameter fails the build |

### 9.3 Session state, authentication and PII at rest

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A `SessionContextAuthenticationFilter` in `carddemo-common` turns the sign-on session into a Spring Security `Authentication` | Require a second credential presentation on every request; or drop authorization on the business routes | Sign-on stored the COMMAREA context in the Redis session but nothing ever converted it into an `Authentication`, so every role-gated route rejected an already-signed-on user and no authenticated cross-service flow worked at all. The filter reads the shared session attribute and authenticates with `ROLE_ADMIN`/`ROLE_USER` derived from `CDEMO-USER-TYPE` `'A'`/`'U'` | Session forgery would grant a role, which is why the session id is a server-side opaque key in an AUTH-protected Redis with no host port |
| Seeded credentials carry the `{bcrypt}` prefix | Configure a bare `BCryptPasswordEncoder` instead of the delegating encoder | `PasswordEncoderFactory` returns a `DelegatingPasswordEncoder`, which requires an `{id}` prefix to select an algorithm; the seed stored bare `$2a$10$…` hashes, so `matches()` threw `IllegalArgumentException` and `POST /auth/signon` answered **403 with an empty body** for the correct password. Keeping the delegating encoder preserves the ability to migrate algorithms later | The prefix must be present in every future seed row; the migration was edited in place because it had not yet been released |
| `user-service` chain is STATELESS with `sessionFixation(none)` and a `NullSecurityContextRepository` | Leave the default session-fixation protection | The default rotates the session id on authentication. Because every service shares ONE Redis session, `user-service` rotating it destroyed the session the gateway and every sibling were still using | `user-service` cannot itself hold session state, which is correct: it authenticates from the shared context on each request |
| PII columns use a versioned `gcm1:` ciphertext envelope and TOLERATE legacy plaintext on READ | Fail on any value that is not ciphertext; or re-encrypt the seed data in a follow-up migration | The seed migrations insert readable values into columns the entities map through an AES converter, so `GET /accounts/{id}` and `GET /cards` returned 500 `JpaSystemException`. A version-prefixed envelope lets the converter recognise ciphertext unambiguously and pass through a value that carries no prefix, so reads succeed while every WRITE re-encrypts | A plaintext value stays plaintext until its row is next written. The prefix makes the two cases distinguishable rather than guessed, and the alternative — failing the read — leaves the API unusable |
| `CARDDEMO_PII_KEY` and the critical connection settings are validated EAGERLY by `BeanFactoryPostProcessor`s | Resolve the key lazily on first use, as the converter originally did | A missing, blank or non-Base64 key let the service start and report UP while every PII request returned 500. Validating during bean-factory post-processing fails the context before the port opens. `InitializingBean` was tried first and proved too late | A malformed key now prevents startup, which is the intended fail-fast behaviour. In-file defaults were removed from the datasource URL for the same reason |
| The ERROR dispatch is `permitAll` on every security chain | Leave `/error` subject to `anyRequest().authenticated()` | An unhandled exception is re-dispatched to `/error`; when that dispatch is itself gated, the security entry point replaces the real error body with an empty 403, which is exactly why several genuine 500s presented as 403 and were so hard to diagnose | The error body is reachable without authentication; it carries a generic message plus a correlation id, never a stack trace or exception class |

### 9.4 Observability: tracing, correlation and structured logs

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The Boot 4 tracing auto-configuration module and an OTLP span exporter are declared in `carddemo-common`, so all nine services inherit them | Keep only `micrometer-tracing` and `micrometer-tracing-bridge-otel`, as the original poms did | Those two artifacts provide the API and the bridge but no auto-configuration and no exporter, so nothing created a tracer and nothing shipped a span: `grep -c '"traceId"'` returned 0 in every service log. Declaring the dependency once in the shared library keeps the nine services consistent | An exporter on the classpath of a service with no collector reachable simply drops spans; export is best-effort by design and no service gates readiness on it |
| A Jaeger collector (`jaegertracing/jaeger:2.11.0`) is part of the local stack, in compose and in k8s | Assume an externally provided collector | The configured endpoint pointed at the container's OWN loopback, which is never a collector, so the AAP §0.7.5 requirement to verify tracing "in the local environment" could not be met | One more container locally. Verified end to end: a single request through the gateway produces one joined trace — 13 spans across `api-gateway`+`auth-service`, 17 across `api-gateway`+`account-service` |
| The correlation-id filter is registered ONCE, by making `carddemo-common`'s `WebObservabilityConfig` an `@AutoConfiguration` listed in `META-INF/spring/…AutoConfiguration.imports` | Keep the five service-local copies; or `@Import` the shared class in each of the nine applications | The shared implementation existed but nothing imported it, so it never ran; five services had diverged local copies that ignored an inbound `traceparent`, and four services emitted no `X-Correlation-Id` at all. An auto-configuration import file makes registration automatic and impossible to forget, and the five duplicates were deleted | Auto-configuration applies to every consumer of the library, which is the intent. A `ThreadLocalAccessor` propagates the id into async and batch threads, and the gateway wraps proxied responses so the header is not duplicated |
| Structured logs carry a `service` member via `logging.structured.json.add.service` | Add a static field to each `logback-spring.xml` encoder | The JSON records carried the correlation id and trace ids but no service identity, so a record pulled from an aggregated stream could not be attributed. The property applies to the manually declared `StructuredLogEncoder`, which was verified empirically before adopting it | Nine files carry the same one-line property rather than one shared file, because each service names itself |
| A `RequestLoggingFilter` emits one access-log line per request, and `DataAccessException` / `OptimisticLockingFailureException` are mapped in `GlobalExceptionHandler` | Rely on the container's access log; leave the two exceptions to the generic handler | There was no request logging anywhere, so a failing call left no trace of its status or duration, and an unmapped `JpaSystemException` produced a 500 with no correlation id. `DataAccessException` is used rather than `JpaSystemException` because `api-gateway` has no `spring-orm` on its classpath | One extra log line per request; it carries method, URI, status, duration and correlation id, and is emitted at ERROR for 5xx |
| The error envelope's `trace` field carries the REAL trace id when tracing is active, and the correlation id otherwise | Keep populating it with the correlation id under a `traceId` name | The field was named `traceId` but held a correlation UUID, so an operator correlating against the tracing backend searched for an id that backend had never seen | Two possible meanings for one field, which the response makes explicit by naming the fallback `correlationId` |
| Lettuce command latencies are recorded through a `MicrometerCommandLatencyRecorder` published as a `ClientResourcesBuilderCustomizer` | Delete the three Redis dashboard panels | Lettuce ships the recorder but does not install it by default, so `lettuce_command_*` never existed and three panels could never show data. `batch-service` correctly does not get it: it has no `spring-boot-starter-data-redis` | Command-level metrics add per-command bookkeeping; the recorder is the vendor-supported integration |
| The Grafana dashboard's saved `refresh` is 30 s, "Service Up/Down" excludes `job="prometheus"`, and no ratio panel clamps its denominator | Leave the saved 10 s refresh and the `up{job=~"$job"}` query; keep `clamp_min(denominator, 1)` as a divide-by-zero guard | 10 s across 26 panels cancelled still-in-flight queries on every tick, and with the Service variable set to All the panel plotted Prometheus's own self-scrape alongside the nine services. `clamp_min(x, 1)` silently rewrites any true rate below 1/s to 1/s: with this system's ~0.13 req/s per service the SLO panel rendered **13.3% compliance when the true value is 100%**, and the Redis latency panel rendered 38 µs against a true 579 µs — a panel that actively contradicted the very NFR it existed to evidence. Plain division yields no sample when the denominator is absent, which Grafana renders as an honest gap | A gap is less pretty than a number but is not a false number. Note that Grafana 13.1.1 IGNORES `refresh=off` in the URL and rewrites it to the saved value on a full page load, so the saved value is the only effective lever. Verified in the browser: 9 services all at 100%, Redis 428–802 µs, global p95 32.2 ms, no `prometheus` series |

### 9.5 Batch execution and deployment topology

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `carddemo-common`'s `JdbcBatchConfiguration extends JdbcDefaultBatchConfiguration`, imported by the three batch-capable applications | Accept Spring Boot's default batch configuration; declare `@EnableBatchProcessing` + `@EnableJdbcJobRepository` per service; subclass `DefaultBatchConfiguration` per service | Spring Batch 6's `DefaultBatchConfiguration.jobRepository()` returns a `ResourcelessJobRepository` — entirely in memory — and Boot 4.1 inherits it. Every job ran and completed while `BATCH_JOB_INSTANCE` and `BATCH_JOB_EXECUTION` stayed EMPTY, every execution reported id 1, the status endpoint could never resolve an execution, duplicate-instance detection could not fire, and the restart-from-last-committed-chunk behaviour the readers advertise was silently unavailable. Extending a `DefaultBatchConfiguration` is the supported extension point: `BatchAutoConfiguration` is `@ConditionalOnMissingBean(DefaultBatchConfiguration.class)` and backs off cleanly. ONE shared definition is declared rather than one per service: the annotation route and a `DefaultBatchConfiguration` subclass both declare the same `jobRepository` and `jobOperator` beans, so combining them in a context would reject the duplicate definitions, and a single class also keeps the create-isolation level and the observation registry identical in all three services | Replacing Boot's batch auto-configuration wholesale means new Boot batch defaults are not inherited automatically. `getObservationRegistry()` is overridden so `spring_batch_*` metrics still reach the real registry, and `getIsolationLevelForCreate()` is relaxed to `READ_COMMITTED` because PostgreSQL's serializable-snapshot isolation aborted concurrent metadata writes. Verified: 16 executions persisted, all COMPLETED, ids surviving a container restart |
| `getIsolationLevelForCreate()` returns `READ_COMMITTED` instead of the Spring Batch default `SERIALIZABLE` | Keep `SERIALIZABLE`; or retry on `CannotAcquireLockException` | Spring Batch applies this isolation level to the transaction interceptor advising EVERY `JobRepository` method, not only `create*`. Under PostgreSQL's serializable-snapshot isolation the foreign-key check behind `INSERT INTO BATCH_STEP_EXECUTION_CONTEXT` performs a predicate read that conflicts with a concurrent `BATCH_STEP_EXECUTION` insert, so submitting the nine jobs together made one of them end FAILED on a metadata write while its own business step had done nothing wrong. Nothing is weakened: `BATCH_JOB_INSTANCE` carries the unique constraint `JOB_INST_UN (JOB_NAME, JOB_KEY)`, which rejects a duplicate instance at any isolation level, and every launch adds a unique identifying `run.id` so two submissions never compute the same job key | Two concurrent submissions of the identical job key would both attempt the insert and one would fail on the unique index rather than on a serialization conflict. Verified: 36 truly-parallel submissions → 36 COMPLETED, 0 FAILED, and no serialization error in the PostgreSQL log. Concurrent independent job streams is also what the legacy JES initiators did |
| `batch-service` exposes `GET /batch/jobs`, `POST /batch/jobs/{jobName}` (202) and `GET /batch/jobs/executions/{id}` | Add `@Scheduled` triggers; or leave the jobs launchable only from a test | Nothing in the service could reach the nine `launch*` methods, so they were dead code and the six Spring Batch dashboard panels could never show data. An HTTP surface mirrors the legacy TDQ/JES hand-off — the response returns as soon as the job is accepted, not when it finishes — and authorization is enforced at the gateway, which gates `/batch/**` to `ROLE_USER`/`ROLE_ADMIN`; the service publishes no host port. `@Scheduled` was rejected because the legacy jobs were submitted, not timer-driven | An operator can submit a job repeatedly; each submission is a distinct `JobInstance` by `run.id`. Verified: authenticated 200 / anonymous 401 on the catalogue, all nine jobs 202 → COMPLETED |
| Every job parameter is optional, defaulting to the value the legacy job stream hard-codes | Require the caller to supply a business date | `INTCALC.jcl` hard-codes `PARM='2022071800'` on its `EXEC` card and `TRANREPT` hard-codes `PARM-START-DATE,C'2022-01-01'` / `PARM-END-DATE,C'2022-07-06'` in its `SYMNAMES` control card. Those values belong to the migrated job stream, not to the caller, so a bare `POST` reproduces exactly the job the mainframe operator submitted; the data-set names default the same way | A default window that matches no rows produces an empty report, which is the same outcome the legacy job would produce `[app/jcl/INTCALC.jcl:L22; app/jcl/TRANREPT.jcl; app/proc/TRANREPT.prc:L41-42]` |
| `BatchOutputPathResolver` lives in `carddemo-common`, verifies its output root is WRITABLE at construction, and resolves every batch file name inside it | Let each writer open the path it is given | Three writers passed a bare or relative file name straight to `FileSystemResource`, which resolves against the working directory `/app` — on the read-only root filesystem — so statement generation failed the step with "No such file or directory" and the DALYREJS reject writer would have failed the same way. Resolving through a shared component also keeps the CWE-22 containment check in one place, and checking writability at construction turns a mid-job I/O failure into a startup failure that names the directory and the environment variable | A service whose configured root is not writable will not start. That is deliberate and was verified: pointing the root back at `/app/output` reproduces a fail-fast "Failed to prepare batch root directory: /app/output" instead of a job-time error. The INPUT root is deliberately not writability-checked because it may be mounted read-only |
| The batch working directory is a `tmpfs` in compose and an `emptyDir` in Kubernetes | A named volume / PersistentVolumeClaim | The generated reports and statements are derived artifacts, reproducible by re-running the job; the legacy job streams wrote them to new generations of a GDG rather than accumulating them. Ephemeral storage keeps the read-only root filesystem intact without introducing state to back up | Output does not survive a restart. `batch-service` additionally bind-mounts `app/data/ASCII` read-only, because its daily-transaction reader is `.strict(true)` and requires the feed file to exist |
| Every `tmpfs` entry in `docker-compose.yml` pins `mode=1777` explicitly rather than leaving the mode implicit | Leave the mode to Docker's default; run the containers as root; replace the `tmpfs` mounts with named volumes | Left implicit, the mode is not stable across a container's lifecycle: Docker mounts the filesystem world-writable when a container is CREATED, but root-owned `0755` when that same container is RESTARTED. The services run as an unprivileged user (1000:1000) on a read-only root filesystem, so on restart they lose write access to their own working directory and crash-loop — observed as `AccessDeniedException: /var/carddemo/batch/output`, with `mount` reporting `mode=755` and the output directory absent entirely. Pinning the mode makes the permission a declared property of the deployment instead of an artifact of how the container happened to be started. `1777` (sticky, world-writable) matches what Docker grants on creation, so nothing is loosened relative to the working case; the sticky bit keeps one container's files from being removed by another user sharing the mount | A container started by an older compose file retains the implicit mode until it is recreated, so the fix applies on next `up`. Verified by a kill-and-restart cycle: `/var/carddemo/batch` came back as `drwxrwxrwt` and the service reached healthy, where the same cycle previously crash-looped |
| No backend service publishes a host port; the `api-gateway` is the single public entrypoint | Publish 8081–8088 for direct access | One entrypoint is the migrated equivalent of a single CICS region front-end, keeps every inter-service hop on the private network, and means a route can only be reached through the gateway's authorization rules. The datastores publish no host port either, and Prometheus and Grafana bind to `127.0.0.1` | A developer cannot curl a service directly from the host; `docker compose exec` and the internal DNS names serve that purpose, and this is why QA reported ports 8081–8088 closed (Info I-1) |
| Compose build entries carry `image: ${CARDDEMO_IMAGE_REGISTRY:-carddemo}/<svc>:${CARDDEMO_IMAGE_TAG:-1.0.0}` | Leave compose to name images `<project>-<service>` and document a manual tag step | The Kubernetes manifests referenced `carddemo/<svc>:1.0.0` while `docker compose build` produced a different name, so applying them required an undocumented external tag-and-push. The committed DEFAULTS are now exactly the manifest values, and the two variables allow a private registry or a side-by-side build | A parallel build in another checkout would overwrite the same default tag, which is what the override variables are for |
| A repository-root `.dockerignore` PLUS one per module context | A single root `.dockerignore`, as the finding suggested | Docker resolves `.dockerignore` relative to the CONTEXT root, and eight of the ten images build with `context: ./<svc>`, so a root file alone would have had no effect on them. The module files use an allowlist (`*`, then re-admit `target/*.jar`) so they stay correct as new build artifacts appear | Nine near-identical files, which is the only form Docker supports. The root context measurably drops from 1727 MB to 2.9 MB; the module contexts shrink only 1–2% by bytes because the fat jar IS the context — their real benefit is that a source edit no longer invalidates the cache for a byte-identical jar |
| `prom/prometheus:v3.13.2`, `grafana/grafana:13.1.1` and `nginx:1.29-alpine` pinned | Leave the floating tags | An untagged reference resolves to `:latest`, so two builds of the same commit can ship different servers. The AAP §0.5.1 image literals (`eclipse-temurin:21-jre`, `node:24`, `postgres:18`, `redis:8`, `maven:3.9.16-eclipse-temurin-21`) are deliberately left exactly as specified, per the spec-literal fidelity rule | Pinned versions must be reviewed periodically; the pins record the versions the stack was verified against |

### 9.6 Spring Boot 4.1 property migrations discovered at runtime

Each of the following configuration keys was present, syntactically valid and completely inert, because Boot 4 renamed or removed it. Boot does not fail on an unknown property, so every one of these was a silent no-op. They are recorded together because the class of defect matters more than any single instance.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `management.otlp.tracing.endpoint` → `management.opentelemetry.tracing.export.otlp.endpoint` | Keep the old key | The old key was removed in Boot 4.0, so the OTLP endpoint was never configured and every span went to the in-file default (the container's own loopback) | None once corrected; verified by an end-to-end trace landing in Jaeger |
| `spring.session.redis.*` → `spring.session.data.redis.*`, and the gateway's redundant `@EnableRedisHttpSession` removed | Keep both | The old keys did not bind, so the configured session namespace, flush mode and save mode had no effect; the extra annotation additionally bypassed the namespace | None once corrected; verified by the key prefix of a live session |
| `spring.batch.jdbc.initialize-schema` removed entirely | Keep it set to `never` | Boot 4.1 removed the whole `spring.batch.jdbc.*` group — `BatchProperties` now carries only `job` — so the key was an unknown property. Nothing initialises the batch schema in any case: the shared `V5__batch_metadata.sql` owns it and the JDBC job repository only reads and writes rows | None; the comment in the migration now states this instead of referring to a property that no longer exists |
| Readiness and liveness health GROUPS declared explicitly in all nine services | Rely on the endpoint defaults | Without `management.endpoint.health.group.readiness.include`, readiness returned 200/UP while BOTH PostgreSQL and Redis were unavailable, so Kubernetes would keep routing traffic to a service that could serve nothing | Readiness now fails when a required datastore fails, which is the point |
| Bounded dependency timeouts: Hikari `connection-timeout` 3 s and `validation-timeout` 2 s, Redis `timeout` and `connect-timeout` 2 s, plus `timeoutSeconds` on every Kubernetes probe | Accept the driver defaults | Lettuce defaults to a 60 s command timeout, so during a Redis outage the aggregate `/actuator/health` never answered at all (`code=000 time=60.002`) and the probes hung with it | A slow-but-alive dependency can be declared DOWN. Measured after the change: 503 in 2.10–2.13 s with Redis stopped and 3.02 s with PostgreSQL stopped |
| Declared Spring Boot 4's tracing auto-configuration modules (`spring-boot-micrometer-tracing` + `spring-boot-micrometer-tracing-opentelemetry`) in every runnable service and the gateway | (a) Keep only `io.micrometer:micrometer-tracing-bridge-otel`, as originally declared; (b) adopt the umbrella `spring-boot-starter-opentelemetry`; (c) accept a no-op tracer and drop the `traceId`/`spanId` fields from the log pattern | Spring Boot 4 extracted tracing auto-configuration out of `spring-boot-actuator` into dedicated modules, so the bridge alone was INERT: no `io.micrometer.tracing.Tracer` bean existed in any service, no span was ever produced, `management.tracing.sampling.probability` was dead configuration, and the `traceId`/`spanId` fields of every `logback-spring.xml` pattern were permanently empty — the Observability rule's "distributed tracing across service boundaries" (AAP §0.7.5) was unmet. `spring-boot-micrometer-tracing` contributes the span-producing observation handlers, `spring-boot-micrometer-tracing-opentelemetry` contributes `SdkTracerProvider`, the `OtelTracer` and the W3C propagators. Option (b) was rejected because it also pulls OTLP exporters, which would ship spans to a collector that this deployment does not run; option (c) contradicts the AAP | No OTLP/Zipkin exporter artifact is declared, so spans are created and MDC-correlated but not shipped off-box; enabling export is a deployment-time dependency choice. Asserted by `ObservabilityEndpointsIT` in the gateway, auth, user and account modules |
| Corrected three Spring Boot 4 property relocations in committed configuration rather than leaving the pre-4.0 spellings in place | Leave `spring.session.redis.*`, `management.otlp.tracing.*` and `spring.session.store-type` as written (all parse without error) | Each of the three was REMOVED in Boot 4.0.0 (metadata deprecation `level: error`) and is therefore silently ignored: the Spring Session key namespace fell back to `spring:session` instead of the intended `carddemo:session`, an operator override of the OTLP endpoint would have had no effect, and a test profile's `store-type: none` did not actually disable the Redis-backed session store. They are now written as `spring.session.data.redis.*`, `management.opentelemetry.tracing.export.otlp.*` and — since no replacement switch exists — as an explicit note that the store is selected by classpath presence. The environment-variable names (`SPRING_SESSION_REDIS_NAMESPACE`, `MANAGEMENT_OTLP_TRACING_ENDPOINT`) are preserved so no deployment contract changes | A future Boot upgrade may relocate these keys again; `SessionRedisRoundTripIT` pins the namespace by asserting the live Redis key prefix, so a silent relocation fails the build |
| Every session-carrying service now activates `SessionRedisConfig` and every servlet service without its own filter activates `WebObservabilityConfig`, both by explicit `@Import` on the application class | (a) Ship a `META-INF/spring/…AutoConfiguration.imports` file from `carddemo-common`; (b) broaden each service's component scan to `com.carddemo.common`; (c) leave activation to the reader | Both classes were documented as activated "by `@Import` or by broadening component scanning" but NEITHER was actually referenced by any application class, so the strict allowlisted JSON session serializer was inactive (sessions were written with native Java serialization, re-opening the CWE-502 surface the class exists to close) and four servlet services registered no correlation filter at all (their `correlationId` MDC field could never be populated). Explicit `@Import` was chosen over (a) because `carddemo-common` is a plain library whose consumers must opt in, and over (b) because a broadened scan would also pull unrelated shared beans into every context. The five services that declare their own `CorrelationIdFilter` `@Component` deliberately do NOT import `WebObservabilityConfig`, so the filter never registers twice | Activation stays per-service and must be repeated for any new module; asserted by `SessionRedisRoundTripIT` (allowlisted-JSON session payload) and by the correlation-id scenarios of each `ObservabilityEndpointsIT` |

## 10. Offline Batch Compensator — QA-Surfaced Deviations

Decisions taken while resolving the QA findings raised against the offline batch
compensator: daily transaction posting, monthly interest calculation, the
transaction-detail report, statement generation, the data-management dump jobs, and
failed-output cleanup. Every row below is a choice a competent engineer could
reasonably have decided differently, and every one was reached by reproducing the
reported behaviour against a live PostgreSQL 18 instance and re-running the job after
the change, so each records what was observed as well as what was chosen.

### 10.1 Daily transaction posting (`CBTRN02C` / `POSTTRAN.jcl`)

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The seeded `transactions` history is re-keyed into a reserved `1 000 000 000`–`1 999 999 999` id window by adding 1 000 000 000 — the `HISTORY_TRAN_ID_OFFSET` documented in the migration header — to the feed id it derives from | Seed no history at all; seed the history from invented ids unrelated to the feed; delete the staged feed once the history exists | The seed derives the 300-row history as the posted image of the 300-row `DALYTRAN` feed, and `CBTRN02C` `2900-WRITE-TRANSACTION-FILE` moves `DALYTRAN-ID` straight into `TRAN-ID`, so history and feed sharing ids made the FIRST record of the very first posting run fail on `transactions_pkey` — the delivered seed could not post a single record. Offsetting keeps the history a recognizable image of the same merchant activity (`0000000943918566` → `0000001943918566`), keeps `TRAN-ID` a 16-character zero-padded numeric string (`PIC X(16)`), and leaves the staged feed byte-identical to `app/data/ASCII/dailytran.txt` so a posting run behaves exactly as `POSTTRAN.jcl` does | The history's ids are not the ids that posting the current feed would produce; they are deliberately a prior cycle's window, which is documented in the migration header |
| `transaction_id_seq`'s baseline is the maximum over BOTH `transactions` and the staged `daily_transactions` ids | Seed the sequence from `transactions` alone, as before | A feed record still waiting to post is an id already reserved in the transaction master, because posting writes it under its own `DALYTRAN-ID`. Ignoring it let the online add flow (`COTRN02C`) draw an id that the next posting run then tried to `WRITE` — the exact abend the reserved-id baseline exists to prevent | The online flow starts higher up the id space than the history alone implies |
| Every `daily_transactions` column `CBTRN02C` dereferences while validating is `NOT NULL`, with `CHECK (LENGTH(dalytran_orig_ts) >= 10)` | Leave the columns nullable and null-guard each dereference in the processor; give a malformed row a new reject code | `DALYTRAN` is a fixed-width 350-byte sequential data set: a record physically cannot be missing `DALYTRAN-AMT` or carry a five-character `DALYTRAN-ORIG-TS`, so such a row is not a `DALYTRAN` record and has no legacy analogue. Nullable columns let one reach `1500-VALIDATE-TRAN`, where it aborted the step with a raw `NullPointerException` / `StringIndexOutOfBoundsException` on every rerun, blocking every later record and producing no reject record at all. The constraint refuses it at ingestion, where the loader can still fix it | A loader that previously staged an incomplete row now fails at insert; `DALYTRAN-PROC-TS` stays nullable because `2000-POST-TRANSACTION` stamps it at posting time |
| A staged row that is nevertheless unusable is a FEED FAULT — `DailyTransactionFeedValidator.requireUsableRecord` raises a `CardDemoException` naming the record and the offending field — not a business rejection | Invent reject code `104`; reject the row silently with code `100` | Reject codes `100`–`103` are a closed set with fixed messages in `CBTRN02C` `1500-VALIDATE-TRAN`; inventing a code would put a value on the 430-byte `DALYREJS` contract that no legacy consumer knows, and reusing `100` would misreport a corrupt record as an unknown card. The legacy program has no path for a malformed fixed-width record either — it abends — so failing the run with an actionable cause is the faithful outcome | A single corrupt staged row stops the run; the message names the record id and column so the fault is fixable without reading a stack trace |
| `transactionPostingJob` is launchable through `POST /transactions/batch/jobs/{jobName}` (`PostingJobController` + `PostingJobLaunchConfig`), identified by a `postingDate` parameter defaulting to the current date, and scheduled by a Kubernetes `CronJob` at `0 2 * * *` | Leave the job reachable only from tests; schedule it with an external scheduler calling the database directly | The job existed with no production caller at all, so the delivered stack could not post a day's transactions by any supported means, while every other job stream had a launch surface. One `postingDate` identity parameter reproduces JES's refusal of a duplicate job for the same cycle and follows this codebase's convention that `run.id` — and, here, the correlation id — stay non-identifying so the business parameters alone establish identity (§8); the CronJob is the `POSTTRAN.jcl` schedule expressed in the target platform | A second launch surface to secure; it carries the same session-based authorization and correlation-id propagation as the batch-service `/batch` surface. It is built on `JobLauncher` / `TaskExecutorJobLauncher`, which Spring Batch 6 marks deprecated for removal, because that is the launcher every other submission path in this repository already uses (`batch-service` and `reporting-service` `JobSchedulingConfig`, `carddemo-common` observability wiring); conforming keeps one launch pattern and defers the `JobOperator` migration to a single repo-wide change rather than splitting the surface in two. It contributes 5 of the build's 38 compiler deprecation warnings, and no build treats warnings as errors |
| The empty-feed verdict is rendered by the posting STEP listener, not by the job listener | Keep failing the job from the job-completion listener | Failing the job while its only step stayed `COMPLETED` left `BATCH_JOB_EXECUTION` and `BATCH_STEP_EXECUTION` permanently contradicting each other, so an operator querying step metadata saw a success inside a failure. Failing the step makes Spring Batch carry the status and `FAILED_EMPTY_FEED` exit code onto the job, so the two rows an operator reads always agree | The listener ordering now matters: the cleanup listener is registered FIRST so it runs LAST and observes this verdict (§8) |
| "Empty feed" is decided by the STAGED record count, not by the step's read count | Keep treating `readCount == 0` as an empty feed | A restarted execution that has already consumed every record legitimately reads zero, and the earlier rule reported that normal completion as `FAILED_EMPTY_FEED` — turning a successful restart into a false operational alarm. The staged count is the fact that actually distinguishes "the input never arrived" from "there is nothing left to do" | One extra `COUNT(*)` against `daily_transactions` per step, issued once at `afterStep` |
| A run that does not end `COMPLETED` reports NO end-of-run tally, only a diagnostic line naming the outcome | Always emit the tally; emit it with the counts observed so far | `CBTRN02C` reaches its `DISPLAY 'TRANSACTIONS PROCESSED :'` / `'TRANSACTIONS REJECTED  :'` statements only on the normal end-of-file path — `9999-ABEND-PROGRAM` calls `CEE3ABD` and prints no tally. Emitting one anyway printed `PROCESSED :0 / REJECTED :0` for a run that HAD committed records, a figure that contradicted the committed work and had no legacy analogue | An operator reading only the log of a failed run sees no counts; the counts remain in the step metadata |
| The 430-byte `DALYREJS` record is written as ISO-8859-1 after reducing every value to single-byte text (`FixedWidthText.toSingleByteText`) | Keep UTF-8; keep the default platform encoding; pin ISO-8859-1 alone | A fixed-width record is a BYTE contract. Under UTF-8 one accented character made the record 431 bytes and shifted the 80-byte trailer, so the reason code no longer sat at its fixed offset. Pinning ISO-8859-1 alone fixes that case but BREAKS the opposite one: a supplementary code point is two Java chars that encode to one byte, leaving the record SHORT. Normalizing per code point to a single-byte-representable character — unmappable ones to `?` — is the only transformation that holds the invariant in both directions | Text outside Latin-1 is lossy in the reject file, exactly as it was on the EBCDIC original; the substitution character is `?` and is documented on the helper |
| Every fixed-width batch sink pins its record delimiter to a single `LF` (`\n`) explicitly, rather than inheriting `FlatFileItemWriter`'s default | Leave the framework default (`System.lineSeparator()`); write CRLF to match a Windows consumer; strip the delimiter entirely | `FlatFileItemWriterBuilder` defaults to the PLATFORM line separator, so the same code emits 431-byte records on Linux and 432-byte records on Windows. `DALYREJS` is `RECFM=F LRECL=430` `[app/jcl/POSTTRAN.jcl]`, so the delimiter is part of a frozen BYTE contract and must not vary with the host: a CRLF host silently shifted every downstream offset by one byte per record. Pinning `LF` in the writer makes the contract a property of the code rather than of the deployment target, and matches the sibling sinks that were already explicit (`RecordDumpItemWriter`, and `TransactionDetailReportWriter`, which writes `"\n"` directly). Removing the delimiter was rejected because the legacy data sets are record-oriented and every reader in the estate is line-oriented | A consumer that requires CRLF must convert on ingest; the writers no longer follow the host convention. Pinned in `RejectFileItemWriter` and in both `StatementGenerationJob` sinks, and guarded by byte-level tests that fail when the platform separator is forced to another value `[transaction-service/src/main/java/com/carddemo/transaction/batch/RejectFileItemWriter.java; reporting-service/src/main/java/com/carddemo/reporting/batch/StatementGenerationJob.java]` |

### 10.2 Monthly interest calculation (`CBACT04C` / `INTCALC.jcl`)

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `computeMonthlyInterest` divides with `RoundingMode.DOWN` | Keep `RoundingMode.HALF_UP`; use `HALF_EVEN` | `COMPUTE WS-MONTHLY-INT = (TRAN-CAT-BAL * DIS-INT-RATE) / 1200` stores into `PIC S9(09)V99` with no `ROUNDED` phrase, and COBOL TRUNCATES toward zero on an unrounded `COMPUTE`. `HALF_UP` posted a different cent on every balance whose exact quotient lay at or above the half — e.g. 100.00 at 5.00% is 0.41666…, which truncates to **0.41** and rounded to 0.42 — so interest, the account balance and `tran_cat_bal` all diverged from the legacy figure. `DOWN` truncates toward zero for negative balances too, matching the COBOL sign behaviour (−100.00 at 5.00% → −0.41). Tests that asserted the `HALF_UP` values encoded the defect and were corrected to the legacy values. This decision was ALREADY recorded in §2 (`setScale(2, RoundingMode.DOWN)`, NOT `HALF_UP`); only the implementation diverged from it | A cent lower than commercial rounding on the affected balances; that is the legacy figure. AAP 0.6.1's prose is self-contradictory here — it names `setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP)` while describing it as reproducing "COBOL's fixed-scale truncation" — and is resolved toward truncation by the COBOL source (no `ROUNDED` phrase) and by the binding constraints AAP 0.7.1 and 0.7.6, which forbid altering COBOL rounding or calculation logic |
| The tran-id suffix is a high-water mark kept in the step `ExecutionContext` under `interest.tranIdSuffix`, seeded in `open` and republished in `update` | Keep a plain in-memory counter; derive the suffix from the item index; use a database sequence | The suffix distinguishes the interest transactions a run writes. An in-memory counter restarted at zero, so a restart after a partial failure re-issued suffixes the previous attempt had already committed and the restart failed on the primary key — the run could never complete. Spring Batch persists the step `ExecutionContext` in the SAME transaction as each chunk commit, so a value republished there advances exactly with the committed work and can never re-issue a consumed suffix. A sequence would have worked but would have put id generation outside the step's transactional envelope and left gaps that the legacy id has no room for | The suffix is per step execution, so ids from a restarted run continue the previous attempt's numbering rather than starting fresh — which is precisely the property that makes the restart safe |
| The interest transaction's card is chosen with `findFirstByXrefAcctIdOrderByXrefCardNumAsc` | Keep `findFirstByXrefAcctId` (no ordering) | Without an `ORDER BY` the database may return any of an account's cross-reference rows, so a multi-card account's interest transaction was attributed to a different card between runs and the output was not reproducible. `CBACT04C` reads `XREFFILE` through its account path and takes the first record in key order, so ordering by `XREF-CARD-NUM` is both deterministic and the legacy choice | None; the added sort is on an indexed unique key |
| `TRAN-MERCHANT-NAME` / `-CITY` / `-ZIP` are filled with SPACES at their declared widths (50 / 50 / 10) | Keep the empty string; store `NULL` | `CBACT04C` `1300-B-WRITE-TX` performs `MOVE SPACES TO` each field [app/cbl/CBACT04C.cbl:L492-L494], and a COBOL `MOVE` of `SPACES` fills the WHOLE fixed-length field. An empty string made the persisted interest transaction differ byte-for-byte from the legacy record and from every other transaction in the master, and any consumer reading `X(50)` positions found the following fields shifted | Three fields carry blanks rather than empties; that is the legacy image |

### 10.3 Transaction-detail report (`CBTRN03C` / `TRANREPT`)

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| At end of file the LAST record's amount is added to the page and account totals a SECOND time, and the closing block is the page total plus the grand total with NO trailing account total | Add the amount once and close with account, page and grand totals (the intuitive reading) | `CBTRN03C`'s EOF branch runs `ADD TRAN-AMT TO WS-PAGE-TOTAL WS-ACCOUNT-TOTAL` while the record area still holds the LAST record already added inside the read loop, then performs `1110-WRITE-PAGE-TOTALS` and `1110-WRITE-GRAND-TOTALS` and never `1120-WRITE-ACCOUNT-TOTALS`. The double count and the missing account total are therefore the legacy figures, not defects to be corrected: reproducing them is what makes the migrated report byte-comparable, and AAP 0.7.6 forbids "correcting" legacy arithmetic. The emitted line set was verified against a 300-row window — 501 lines, 49 account totals for 50 card groups, and a grand total (105567.20) equal to the detail sum plus the last record's amount and to the sum of all 17 page totals | A reader who computes totals independently from the detail lines will differ from the report by the last record's amount; this is documented here and in the writer's docstring |
| An empty selection window still emits the header/total skeleton | Keep producing a zero-byte file | A zero-byte report is indistinguishable from a job that never ran or that failed before writing, and `CBTRN03C` writes its headers before the first read, so a run over an empty window legitimately produces the header and total lines. The skeleton (402 bytes = 3 × 133) makes "ran, selected nothing" a positive statement, which is also what lets failed-run cleanup (§8) be unambiguous from both sides | A downstream consumer that treated a zero-byte file as "no data" must now read the totals; the totals are zero |
| The report reader's `ORDER BY t.tranCardNum` is completed with the primary key (`, t.tranId`) in both the reader JPQL and `TransactionRepository.findByProcTsDateRangeOrderByCardNum` | Leave the single-column sort; read the whole window in one page | A paging reader needs a TOTAL order: ordering on a non-unique column lets the database order tied rows differently per page, so a row is repeated across a page boundary and another is silently dropped. Measured live on the delivered seed (six transactions share a card number): 300 detail lines carrying only 299 distinct ids, with one transaction printed twice, one absent, and the totals differing from the window's true sum — a financial report that omitted a posted transaction. This was not in the QA report; it was found while re-verifying the EOF fix | None; the tie-breaker is the primary key, which is already indexed |
| Report lines are written as ISO-8859-1 over single-byte-normalized text | Keep UTF-8 | Same byte-contract reasoning as the reject file: `FD-REPTFILE-REC` is `PIC X(133)` [app/cbl/CBTRN03C.cbl:L85], and only per-code-point normalization keeps every line at exactly 133 BYTES in both the accented and the supplementary-code-point direction | Text outside Latin-1 is lossy, as on the original |

### 10.4 Statement generation (`CBSTM03A` / `CREASTMT.JCL`)

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| Every value interpolated into the HTML statement is HTML-escaped (`&` first, then `<`, `>`, `"`, `'`) before the fixed-width `MOVE`, while the TEXT statement stays unescaped | Escape nothing, as the legacy program does; sanitize the data on the way in; escape after padding | `CBSTM03A` wrote customer text into `HTMLFILE` unescaped, which was harmless for a data set nobody rendered. In the target the HTML statement is served to a browser, so a name, address or transaction description containing markup becomes executable script (stored XSS) — verified with payloads in the seeded customer and transaction text. Escaping the VALUE before the `MOVE` keeps `HTML-FIXED-LN PIC X(100)`: the enclosing line is padded or truncated to 100 exactly as the `MOVE` would be. The text statement is a fixed-width data set, not markup, so escaping it would corrupt the `X(80)` contract for no benefit | A very long escaped value can be truncated mid-entity by the 100-character `MOVE`, a rendering artefact only — truncation removes characters and can never re-form a `<`. Escaping is the identity transformation for text without `& < > " '`, so no byte of normal statement output changes |
| `POST /reports/statements` accepts only `stmtFile` and `htmlFile`; the mandatory identifying `reportType`, `startDate` and `endDate` parameters were REMOVED | Keep them and make the job honour them by filtering transactions to the window | `CREASTMT.JCL` carries no `PARM` at all: its SORT step re-keys the WHOLE `TRANSACT` file by card and tran-id with no date filter, and `STEP030`/`STEP040` delete and recreate `STMTFILE` (LRECL=80) and `HTMLFILE` (LRECL=100). The three parameters were therefore invented — they were required, they participated in job identity, and the job read none of them, so a caller could be refused as a duplicate over values that changed nothing while believing it had requested a filtered statement run. Honouring them instead would have added a date window the legacy job stream does not have | A caller still sending the old parameters is accepted and they are ignored (verified); the business parameter set is now the two output file names alone, and because a statement print is a repeatable read/print run its per-submission `run.id` is identifying (§11), so a repeat request for the same destination re-runs instead of being refused |
| Both statement writers pin ISO-8859-1 over single-byte-normalized text | Keep UTF-8 | `STMTFILE` is `X(80)` and `HTMLFILE` is `X(100)` — byte contracts, verified to hold for accented and non-Latin-1 customer text with the total file sizes unchanged from the pure-ASCII baseline | Text outside Latin-1 is lossy, as on the original. Both sinks also pin the record delimiter to a single `LF` for the same byte-contract reason; that policy is recorded once, in §10.1 |

### 10.5 Concurrency: batch beans holding per-run state

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `accountReader`, `cardReader`, `cardXrefReader`, `customerReader` and `categoryBalanceReader` are `@StepScope`, as is reporting-service's `statementCardXrefReader` | Leave them singletons and serialize the jobs with an external lock; leave them singletons and document the restriction | A `RepositoryItemReader` is an `ItemStream` holding ONE page cursor and one peeked item. As a singleton every concurrent execution of the same step shared that cursor, so simultaneous runs split the rows between themselves: twelve concurrent account dumps produced eight DIFFERENT files with read counts scattered from 0 to 100 instead of twelve identical 55-record dumps, including banner-only files that looked like successful runs over an empty table. `@StepScope` gives each step execution its own reader, which is what the framework provides for exactly this reason; an external lock would have removed concurrency the target is required to support | None functionally; a reader is now instantiated per step execution. All five sort on unique keys, so no tie-breaker was needed |
| `LoggingItemWriter` is produced by a `@Bean @StepScope` factory instead of being constructed inline in the step definition | Leave the instance inline and reset its counter in `beforeStep` | Its `AtomicLong` running total was ONE instance inside a singleton step, so the "records validated" figure accumulated across every run in the JVM's lifetime, so a second 300-record run in the same JVM would have logged 400–600 instead of 100–300. After the change two successive 300-record runs each logged 100 / 200 / 300. Making it step-scoped fixes the lifetime rather than patching the symptom, and matches the treatment of every other stateful batch bean here. This was not in the QA report; it was found by auditing every batch bean for shared mutable state after the reader finding | None; the writer is now created per step execution |

### 10.6 Amending delivered migrations in place

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The feed constraints and the seed re-keying were applied by EDITING `V1__create_schema.sql` and `V3__seed_test_data.sql` rather than by adding forward-only migrations | Add `V6`/`V7` migrations that alter the columns and rewrite the seeded ids | These two files ARE the delivered schema-and-seed contract for a stack that has not yet been released, and this project keeps ONE migration set owned by `carddemo-common` (§9.1). A corrective migration would have left the delivered `V1` describing a schema that cannot hold a valid feed and the delivered `V3` describing a seed that cannot post, so every reader of the contract would have had to reconstruct the truth from a later diff | Amending an applied migration CHANGES its checksum: a jar built before the change fails Flyway `Validate failed: checksum mismatch` against a database migrated by a newer jar — observed live during re-verification. Every service bundles `carddemo-common`'s migration set, so all modules must be rebuilt together, and an environment provisioned from the earlier scripts must be re-provisioned (or repaired with `flyway repair`) rather than merely restarted |

### 10.7 Failed-output cleanup listener contract

The decision to register the listener on every file-producing step, and the rule that a
qualified `COMPLETED_WITH_*` exit code counts as success, are recorded in §8.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| A `null` registered path is filtered out at construction (`Arrays.stream(paths).filter(Objects::nonNull)`) rather than rejected | Reject `null` with `IllegalArgumentException`; keep `List.of(...)`, which throws `NullPointerException` on a null element | The constructor's contract already documented that a `null` path is ignored, so that a step whose optional second artefact is unconfigured can register one listener rather than branch on the configuration. `List.of` contradicted that contract by throwing, which would have failed the whole application context at bean creation — the statement step registers two paths and either may be defaulted. Honouring the documented contract is the smaller and safer change | A caller that passes `null` by mistake gets no cleanup for that artefact instead of a startup failure; the tolerated behaviour is asserted by `FailedOutputCleanupListenerTest.ignoresNullRegisteredPath` |

## 11. QA-Remediation Deviations (cross-cutting online compensator checkpoint)

Decisions taken while resolving the twenty-one runtime findings raised by end-to-end QA of the integrated online estate (real HTTP → controller → service → JPA/PostgreSQL round trips). Every row is a choice a competent engineer could reasonably have made differently, so each is recorded here rather than in a code comment, per the Explainability rule (AAP §0.7.2). Finding numbers in parentheses are the QA report's.

### 11.1 Credential Contract & User Administration

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The password moved from a `@RequestParam` (query string) to a field on `AddUserRequestDto` / `UpdateUserRequestDto`, write-only in JSON (Q1, Q5) | Keep the query parameter and scrub it in the tracing exporter and the access log; send it in a header | A query parameter is recorded verbatim by every hop that reports `http.url` — the OTLP span attribute, the gateway access log, any proxy — so the raw password left the process in the clear on every user create and update (CWE-598/CWE-532), and scrubbing every such sink is a defence that fails silently the moment one is added. The body field also RESTORES contract parity: the frozen frontend contract already declared `password` on the body of both DTOs (`frontend/src/types/user.ts`), so the query parameter was the deviation | A caller built against the query-parameter form receives `400 Password can NOT be empty...`; the frontend contract is unchanged `[app/cbl/COUSR01C.cbl; app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy; frontend/src/types/user.ts]` |
| `@NotBlank @Size(max = 8)` on the password of both user write DTOs, mirroring `SignonRequestDto` (Q1) | Widen the sign-on limit to match the write path; hash the first 8 characters | `SEC-USR-PWD` is `PIC X(08)`: the field physically cannot hold nine characters. The write path accepted `NineChar9`, hashed it, and returned `201`, after which sign-on rejected the very credential it had just stored — the account was permanently unusable. Bounding the write path at the legacy width makes the two contracts agree; widening sign-on instead would break the fixed field width | A caller that previously created a >8-character password now receives a field error `[app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy]` |
| Legacy field widths and the user-type value set are edited in the write DTOs (`userId` 8, first/last name 20, `userType` 1 and `@Pattern` `[AU]`) (Q2, Q3) | Let the database `CHECK`/`VARCHAR` constraints reject them; edit them in the service | A 9-character user id or a 21-character name reached the INSERT and returned `500` from a constraint violation, and `userType='X'` did the same — a client cannot act on an opaque server error. The DTO is the right layer here (unlike the transaction numeric widths, §11.3) because these fields have no ordered legacy edit sequence to pre-empt: `COUSR01C` edits only emptiness, so no verbatim literal is displaced. `GlobalExceptionHandler.orderFieldErrors` reports presence constraints before format constraints, so the blank literals still win | The verbatim legacy blank literals are preserved; the width messages are new text for inputs the 3270 map could not express `[app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy; app/cbl/COUSR01C.cbl; app/cbl/COUSR02C.cbl]` |
| `SecurityUser` carries `@Version` (migration `V6__security_users_optimistic_lock.sql`) and creation is an INSERT whose duplicate key is caught, replacing `existsBySecUsrId` + `save` (Q4) | Keep the check-then-act probe; serialise user administration with a lock | Under ten concurrent creates of one id the probe let several callers through: three received `201` for the same id, one received `500`, and the surviving row belonged to none of them. Five concurrent updates all returned `200` and four credentials were silently lost. The database is the only arbiter that cannot race: the primary key rejects the duplicate (`400 User ID already exist...`, the verbatim literal) and the version token turns a lost update into `409 Record changed by some one else. Please review` | Adds a version column with no legacy analogue; a client that omits the token performs an unconditional write `[app/cbl/COUSR01C.cbl; app/cbl/COUSR02C.cbl; app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy]` |
| A user updating their OWN account has that session revoked IN PLACE (the session-context attribute is stripped and a revocation marker set) instead of the session being invalidated (Q6) | Invalidate the session (`HttpSession.invalidate()`); defer revocation to a post-commit hook; exempt self-updates from revocation entirely | Invalidating the caller's own session mid-request destroyed the store entry that BOTH the gateway and user-service still held open, so each one's `SessionRepositoryFilter.commitSession()` then threw `IllegalStateException: Session was invalidated` and a fully committed update was reported as `500`. Revoking in place ends the credential's authority just as completely — the next request is `401` — while leaving the entry both hops save intact. Exempting self-updates was rejected: the old password must stop working immediately | The row's session object survives until its normal expiry, carrying only the revocation marker `[app/cbl/COUSR02C.cbl]` |

### 11.2 Referential Integrity & Card Aggregate

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The account→card cross-reference lookup returns the FIRST row in card-number order (`findFirstByXrefAcctIdOrderByXrefCardNumAsc`) rather than a unique `Optional` (Q7) | Keep the unique lookup and reject multi-card accounts; return the whole list and pick in the caller | CXACAIX is an ALTERNATE index on `XREF-ACCT-ID`, which is explicitly NON-unique — one account legitimately carries many cards — and the legacy programs `STARTBR`/`READNEXT` it and take the first record. The unique lookup threw `IncorrectResultSizeDataAccessException` for every account with two cards, so account view, account update, transaction add and bill payment ALL returned `500` on such an account: a 100% referential-integrity failure (AAP §0.1.1) on legitimate data. Ordering by card number makes the choice deterministic, which the VSAM browse order also guaranteed | The first card by number is the representative card for account-keyed operations, exactly as in the legacy browse `[app/cpy/CVACT03Y.cpy; app/cbl/CBTRN02C.cbl:L371-397; app/cbl/COACTVWC.cbl]` |
| `CARD-CVV-CD` is compared in the `9300` snapshot check ONLY when the caller actually supplied `oldCardCvvCd` (Q10) | Return the CVV (or an opaque token for it) on the read path so a client can echo it; drop the snapshot mechanism for cards | `COCRDUPC` did compare `CCUP-OLD-CVV-CD`, but only because the 3270 map DISPLAYED the CVV. Here the CVV is encrypted at rest and deliberately absent from every read path (AAP §0.6.7), so `oldCardCvvCd` was always `null`, `equalsExact(null, storedCvv)` always failed, and EVERY snapshot-bearing update was rejected with `409` for a change that had not happened — the concurrency-safe form of the card-update workflow could never succeed. Returning the CVV, even masked or tokenised, would put card verification data on the wire and is the worse deviation | The three remaining snapshot fields are exactly those a client can observe; the `@Version` token and the row write lock cover what the CVV comparison used to `[app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1498-1519; app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy]` |
| `CardMapper.applyUpdate` applies `cardCvvCd` only when the request supplies it (Q11) | Require the CVV on every update; treat an absent CVV as "clear it" | The unconditional `card.setCardCvvCd(request.getCardCvvCd())` wrote `null` over the stored CVV on every routine name/status/expiry change — the ONLY form a client can build, since no read path returns the CVV. Silent destruction of card verification data on an unrelated edit violates AAP §0.6.7; requiring the CVV is impossible for the same reason | An explicit "clear the CVV" operation does not exist; none is specified `[app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl; app/cpy/CVACT02Y.cpy]` |
| `Card` carries `@Version` (migration `V7__cards_optimistic_lock.sql`), the update re-reads under `@Lock(PESSIMISTIC_WRITE)`, and the token is published on both card response DTOs and accepted on the request (Q12) | Rely on the field snapshot alone; serialise card updates in the service; require the token on every write | Ten concurrent updates of one card ALL returned `200`, each echoing its own name as stored, while the row kept only the last writer's value: nine callers were told a change had been applied that had not been. The version token makes the database the arbiter (`UPDATE … WHERE card_num = ? AND version = ?`) and the write lock serialises the re-read so each writer compares against what the previous writer actually committed. The token is deliberately OPTIONAL: the same QA checkpoint requires the snapshot-only form (Q10) and the bare `{name,status,expiry}` form (Q11) to keep succeeding, so a token-less write is an explicit unconditional write — serialised by the lock, with each writer told the version its own commit produced | Adds a version column with no legacy analogue; a client that offers no proof of what it read still performs a last-writer-wins update, which is why the frontend contract now declares `version` as required `[app/cbl/COCRDUPC.cbl:L1498-1519; app/cbl/COACTUPC.cbl:L517-523]` |
| `cardCvvCd` and `oldCardCvvCd` are constrained to exactly three digits (Q13) | Leave the field unconstrained; truncate to three digits | `CARD-CVV-CD` is `PIC 9(03)`.
