It passed only when invoked as a bare `npx jest <file>` without the flag, a CJS-style path no project command uses. `unstable_mockModule` + dynamic import is the pattern the sibling suite `src/hooks/useSession.test.ts` already established, which the instruction's own "same MANDATORY pattern" clause points at. Monkey-patching cannot work: ES module bindings are immutable and `useApi` resolves `ApiError` at its own import time | Jest marks the API unstable, and no static import of the mocked graph is possible — only type-only imports from `../types` stay static; a future Jest that supports `jest.mock` under ESM would allow the literal form `[frontend/package.json; frontend/src/hooks/useSession.test.ts:L34-38]` |
| The page is exercised inside the REAL shell — route element `<Layout><BillPayPage /></Layout>` under a `MemoryRouter` — rather than rendered standalone or with `useScreenChrome` stubbed | Render `BillPayPage` alone and assert only its own body; mock `../components/Layout` with a chrome-capturing fake; export `ScreenChromeContext` so the suite can build a test-only provider | Every verbatim message and every PF caption the instruction requires is rendered by `ErrorBanner` (line 23, `role="alert"` / `role="status"`) and `PFKeyBar` (line 24 buttons) INSIDE `Layout`; the page only publishes them through `useScreenChrome`, whose detached default is deliberately inert (§13), so a standalone render silently discards the chrome and could assert none of it. `ScreenChromeContext` is intentionally not exported, leaving `Layout` as the only harness, and using the real shell verifies the publish path end-to-end instead of a fake's echo | The suite now depends on `Layout`/`Header`/`ErrorBanner`/`PFKeyBar` as well as the page, so a shell regression can surface here; each of those ships its own suite, which localizes the cause `[frontend/src/components/Layout.tsx; frontend/src/components/ErrorBanner.tsx; frontend/src/components/PFKeyBar.tsx]` |
| The legend assertion expects `ENTER=Continue`, `F3=Back`, `F4=Clear` — the mapset wording the page publishes — not the `F3=Exit` of the suite's instruction; the F3 EXIT ACTION is asserted separately by clicking the key and by the physical `F3` keydown, both expected to land on `/menu` | Assert `F3=Exit` and relabel `BillPayPage.tsx` to match; assert the action only and skip the captions | `COBIL00.bms` declares `INITIAL='ENTER=Continue  F3=Back  F4=Clear'` at `LENGTH=33`, which is exactly those three captions plus their two-space gaps, and `BillPayPage.tsx` publishes the three verbatim. Spec-literal fidelity makes the mapset the frozen source, and its precedence clause keeps an EXISTING spelling over a statement's spelling of it; §4 already settled this identical discrepancy the same way for `ReportPage`. Relabelling the page would break the mapset contract and the "match exact captions" clause at once | A reader comparing suite against instruction sees `F3=Back`; the key's behaviour is identical — PF3 leaves for the main menu, as `COBIL00C` does with `XCTL PROGRAM('COMEN01C')` `[app/bms/COBIL00.bms:L131-135; frontend/src/pages/BillPayPage.tsx:L246; app/cbl/COBIL00C.cbl:L126-131]` |
| The success banner is asserted as raw `textContent` equality plus `startsWith('Payment successful. ')`, not with `toHaveTextContent` or a `*ByText` query | `expect(banner).toHaveTextContent('Payment successful.')`; a regular-expression matcher; `getByText` with a custom normalizer | jest-dom's `toHaveTextContent` and every Testing Library text query run the default normalizer, which trims and COLLAPSES runs of whitespace — precisely the bytes under test, because `COBIL00C` concatenates `'Payment successful. '` with `' Your Transaction ID is '` and the rendered banner therefore carries two consecutive spaces, which `billpay-service` reproduces in its own `String.format`. Raw `textContent` equality pins the trailing space byte-for-byte; a mutation run that deleted that space failed the suite, so the assertion is load-bearing rather than decorative | None: the comparison is strictly tighter than the normalized one `[app/cbl/COBIL00C.cbl:L527-530; billpay-service/src/main/java/com/carddemo/billpay/service/BillPaymentService.java:L253]` |
| `Account ID NOT found...` and `Tran ID already exist...` are driven as REJECTED `ApiError`s carrying the standardized backend body message, while `Acct ID can NOT be empty...`, `Invalid value. Valid values are (Y/N)...` and `Confirm to make a bill payment...` are driven through the page's own paths | Resolve `payBill` with a response whose `message` field carries the text; assert the page hard-codes all six literals | The page owns only the three client-side texts; the not-found and duplicate outcomes are produced by `billpay-service` (`RecordNotFoundException` → HTTP 404, `CardDemoException` → HTTP 400) and reach line 23 through `ApiError.body.message`. Driving them as rejections exercises the real chain — `useApi`'s `err instanceof ApiError` normalization and the page's body-message preference — and leaves each frozen literal asserted where it is actually produced. It is also what makes the mocked `ApiError` a genuine passthrough: the suite constructs its rejections with the very constructor the mocked module hands to `useApi` | The rejection fixtures must track the backend status/body contract; they are typed as `ApiErrorResponse`, so a contract change breaks `tsc` instead of passing silently `[billpay-service/src/main/java/com/carddemo/billpay/service/BillPaymentService.java:L70-L82; carddemo-common GlobalExceptionHandler; frontend/src/api/client.ts]` |
| The suite adds three cases beyond the instruction's enumerated list: an all-blank account id, a lower-case `y` confirmation, and a lower-case `n` cancellation | Cover only the enumerated cases | `COBIL00C` tests `ACTIDINI OF COBIL0AI = SPACES OR LOW-VALUES` and its confirm `EVALUATE` names `WHEN 'Y' WHEN 'y'` and `WHEN 'N' WHEN 'n'`, so all three are legacy behaviour a functional-equivalence suite has to pin — and `BillPayPage` already implements them. The enumerate-every-member and no-unrequested-output clauses constrain what the PROGRAM emits; extra assertions over behaviour that already exists emit nothing and change no contract | Three additional cases to maintain, each anchored to a cited COBOL line `[app/cbl/COBIL00C.cbl:L157-L189; frontend/src/pages/BillPayPage.tsx:L187-L200]` |
## 31. Report-Request Screen Test Suite (`ReportPage.test.tsx`, mapset `CORPT00` / `CR00`)

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The report endpoint is mocked with `jest.unstable_mockModule('../api', …)` and the mocked graph (`../api`, `../components/Layout`, `./ReportPage`, `../hooks`) is pulled in with top-level `await import`, instead of the `jest.mock('../api')` the suite's own instruction names | Keep `jest.mock` as written and accept the failure; drop the module mock and let the real axios client run; move this one suite onto the CJS transform | The module runs Jest 30 in NATIVE ESM (`"type": "module"`, preset `ts-jest/presets/default-esm`, `extensionsToTreatAsEsm`, `transform` with `useESM`), where every static import is linked and evaluated before the first statement of the test body, so `jest.mock` is silently ignored. Probed both ways: with `jest.mock` the suite's `requestReport` was the REAL function (`TypeError: requestReportMock.mockReset is not a function`, 48 failures), `frontend/src/api/config.ts` evaluated `import.meta.env` — which the same instruction forbids — and a live axios instance was created. `unstable_mockModule` is Jest's documented ESM form and registers before the dynamic imports, so the page, `useApi` and the suite all resolve the SAME mocked module. Switching the transform was rejected because it would alter shared test infrastructure for one file | The API carries Jest's `unstable_` prefix, so a future major may rename it; it is used in exactly one place, and the suite's first assertion (`jest.isMockFunction(requestReport)`) fails loudly if the mock ever stops applying instead of letting the suite issue live HTTP `[frontend/package.json jest.preset; frontend/src/api/config.ts]` |
| The `../api` factory DECLARES a working `ApiError` class (plus a `signon` export) rather than passing the real module's class through | `jest.requireActual('../api')` or `await import('../api/client')` inside the factory to reuse the real class; mock only `../api/reports` and `../api/config` so the real barrel still loads | `ApiError` is declared in `frontend/src/api/client.ts`, which creates the axios instance and calls `getApiBaseUrl()` → `import.meta.env`, so reusing the real class evaluates exactly what the instruction forbids. Because every consumer resolves `../api` to the one mocked module, the factory's class IS the class `useApi` narrows with `instanceof ApiError`, so the real constructor shape (`status`, `message`, `body?: ApiErrorResponse`, `isOptimisticLockConflict`) is reproduced field-for-field and both failure paths — service message and transport-message fallback — behave as they do against the real client. `signon` is exported because `../hooks/useSession` imports it and ESM linking fails outright on a missing named export | The mocked class must follow `client.ts` if that constructor changes; `tsc --noEmit` catches the drift, because the suite constructs `ApiError` against the real declaration's types `[frontend/src/api/client.ts; frontend/src/hooks/useApi.ts:L24; frontend/src/hooks/useSession.ts:L26]` |
| The mocked launch acknowledgement uses the frozen `ReportResponseDto` shape (`{}` or `{ errorMessage }`), NOT the `jobId` / `status` envelope the instruction's parenthetical names | Add `jobId` / `status` to `ReportResponseDto`; cast the resolved value to bypass strict excess-property checking | `frontend/src/types/report.ts` mirrors `com.carddemo.common.dto.ReportResponseDto` field-for-field and records that the contract exposes no job-id/status envelope: the acknowledgement rides on the `X(78)` `ERRMSGO` status line, which is precisely what `CORPT00C` re-displays after writing its JCL to TDQ `'JOBS'`. Widening a frozen wire type from a test, or casting around the excess-property check, would put the suite ahead of the backend contract. The asynchronous launch is therefore verified by the call itself plus the verbatim `<Name> report submitted for printing ...` line | If the reporting service later returns a job id, the DTO and this suite must be extended together `[app/cpy-bms/CORPT00.CPY; app/cbl/CORPT00C.cbl:L449-L452; frontend/src/types/report.ts]` |
| The page is rendered inside `Layout` — a fifth module beyond the suite's four dependency entries — and every caption and message is asserted against raw `textContent` equality rather than `toHaveTextContent`; the line-24 legend is asserted as `ENTER=Continue` + `F3=Back` | Render the page bare and capture the chrome by mocking `../components/Layout`; assert with `toHaveTextContent`; assert the brief's `F3=Exit` | `errorMessage` / `infoMessage` / `pfKeys` are published through the `useScreenChrome` context that `Layout` owns, and a page rendered outside it discards them into the inert detached context, so the mandated verbatim-message assertions would have nowhere to land; `Layout.test.tsx` already sets the precedent of importing the shell directly, and mocking it would assert a captured object instead of the rendered line-23 region. `toHaveTextContent` normalizes whitespace, which would hide exactly the drift these assertions exist to catch — `'  End Date :'`, `'Please confirm to print the '` and the two-line BMS confirm caption all carry significant spaces — so raw `textContent` equality is used, with an identity `normalizer` for the caption queries. The legend follows the mapset decision already recorded in §4 | The suite couples to the shell, so a `Layout` regression fails it too — intended, since the screen is only ever reachable inside the shell `[app/bms/CORPT00.bms:L200-226; frontend/src/components/Layout.tsx:L85-L106]` |
## 32. User-List Screen Suite (`UserListPage.test.tsx` checkpoint)

Decisions taken while delivering the colocated Jest / React Testing Library suite for the
administrator user-list screen `COUSR00` (CICS `CU00`, program `app/cbl/COUSR00C.cbl`). Every
deviation below is from a literal reading of the suite's own build instruction, so each is recorded
here per the Explainability rule.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The `../api` double is registered with `jest.unstable_mockModule('../api', …)` and the screen plus the hook barrel are then loaded with `await import(…)` in `beforeAll`, instead of the `jest.mock('../api')` the instruction names literally | Reproduce `jest.mock('../api')` unchanged and accept that the real module is used; switch this suite to the CJS transform so `jest.mock` hoisting applies; inject `listUsers` as a prop | This project runs Jest 30 in NATIVE ESM (`"type": "module"`, preset `ts-jest/presets/default-esm`, `extensionsToTreatAsEsm`, `transform` with `useESM`), where the module registry cannot intercept a static ESM import. A probe confirmed it directly: with `jest.mock('../api', factory)` in place, `jest.isMockFunction(listUsers)` returned `false` and the REAL `listUsers` was bound, which would issue an axios request from jsdom and defeat the suite's own required outcome. `jest.unstable_mockModule` is the documented ESM equivalent and is already the established pattern in this repository — `frontend/src/hooks/useSession.test.ts` mocks the same `../api` module exactly this way — so the change reuses a convention rather than introducing one. Changing the transform for one file was rejected because it would fork the module's test infrastructure; prop injection was rejected because it would alter the delivered `UserListPage` signature | The suite depends on an `unstable_`-prefixed Jest API that a future major could rename; it is used by a sibling suite already, so any rename is a single coordinated edit. The `../api` double must also export `signon`, because the `../hooks` barrel re-exports `useSession`, which imports it — omitting it fails the ESM link with "does not provide an export named 'signon'" `[frontend/src/hooks/useSession.test.ts; frontend/package.json jest]` |
| `../components/Layout` is replaced by a double whose `useScreenChrome()` returns an identity-stable `setChrome` spy, and the line-24 legend and line-23 message are asserted from the captured `ScreenChrome` payload | Render `<Layout>` around the screen and assert the real `PFKeyBar` buttons and `ErrorBanner` text in the DOM; drop the legend and message assertions altogether | `useScreenChrome()` outside a `Layout` deliberately returns an INERT context (Section 13), so a screen rendered on its own has its chrome silently discarded — the `F3=Exit` / `F7=Backward` / `F8=Forward` legend, the enabled flags and the `Invalid selection. Valid values are U and D` message would all be unobservable, and three of the suite's required scenarios could not be asserted at all. Capturing the payload asserts precisely what the instruction asks for — that the handlers are "wired" and that the verbatim message is published — while keeping the suite's imports inside the four declared dependencies of the file, none of which is the shell. Dropping the assertions was not an option; wrapping in the real shell would have added a fifth, undeclared dependency and turned a screen unit test into a shell integration test the shell's own suite already covers | The suite does not prove that the captured chrome reaches the rendered frame; `Layout.test.tsx` covers that path for every screen, so the two suites are complementary rather than overlapping `[frontend/src/components/Layout.tsx; frontend/src/components/Layout.test.tsx]` |
| The real `ApiError` class is passed through the `../api` double (`import { ApiError } from '../api'` in the suite, re-exported by the factory) | Hand-roll a `FakeApiError` stand-in as `useSession.test.ts` does; omit `ApiError` from the factory | `useApi` uses `ApiError` as a VALUE — `err instanceof ApiError` decides whether a rejection is surfaced as-is or renormalized to `status` `0` — so omitting it breaks the link, and substituting a look-alike would exercise the stand-in's identity instead of the production classification. Passing the real class through is also what the instruction's "Passthrough `ApiError`" asks for, and it lets the failed-browse cases construct genuine `ApiError(404, …, body)` and `ApiError(0, 'Network Error')` values and assert the real `error.body?.message` / `error.message` precedence. The sibling hook suite `useApi.test.ts` already imports the real `ApiError` from `../api` for the same reason | Importing `../api` for the class transitively evaluates `api/config.ts`, whose `import.meta.env?.VITE_API_BASE_URL` read is the one thing the instruction cautions against. It is inert here — the optional chain resolves to the `http://localhost:8080` fallback, no request is ever issued through the real client because the screen's graph is mocked, and the pre-existing `useApi.test.ts` proves the same import is safe in this runner. The suite itself contains no `import.meta` `[frontend/src/api/config.ts; frontend/src/hooks/useApi.test.ts]` |
| Navigation is asserted with a route-recorder element mounted at `/users/update`, `/users/delete` and `/admin` that reports `useLocation()` into a spy, leaving `useNavigate` as the real router implementation | Mock `react-router-dom` and replace `useNavigate` with a spy; assert only that the destination element rendered | Partially mocking `react-router-dom` under native ESM requires resolving the actual module from inside the mock factory, which re-enters the registry, and it would have to re-export the whole router surface the suite and the screen both use (`MemoryRouter`, `Routes`, `Route`, `useLocation`). The recorder needs neither: because it reports the location it was REACHED with, it asserts the pathname AND the `state` payload the screen attached — `{ userId: 'USER0003' }` — which is exactly the row identity that `COUSR00C` carried in the COMMAREA before `XCTL` to `COUSR02C` / `COUSR03C`. Asserting only that an element rendered would not prove the user id travelled | The spy observes the destination render rather than the `navigate` call itself, so a redundant navigation to the same location would not be distinguished; no scenario depends on the call count `[app/cbl/COUSR00C.cbl:L189-L210]` |
| Async `@testing-library/user-event` interactions are NOT wrapped in `act`; `act` is retained only around `render` and around a directly invoked PF-key `onActivate` | Wrap every interaction in `await act(async () => …)` for symmetry; suppress the resulting console output | The first implementation wrapped the ENTER click and produced eight React `The current testing environment is not configured to support act(...)` console errors. Root cause: Testing Library's own `asyncWrapper` (`@testing-library/react/dist/pure.js`) sets `IS_REACT_ACT_ENVIRONMENT` to `false` for the duration of an async Testing Library call and drains the microtask queue before restoring it, so an enclosing `act` leaves react-dom with a non-null act queue and a false flag — the exact condition that emits the warning. user-event already routes its own updates through Testing Library's `eventWrapper`, so the wrapper was redundant as well as harmful. Suppressing the output was rejected outright: a green suite that prints React errors is not a passing suite | None observable — the suite now runs with zero `console.error` across all 28 cases, and the state assertions that follow each interaction are unchanged `[frontend/node_modules/@testing-library/react/dist/pure.js asyncWrapper]` |
## 33. Add-User Screen Suite (`UserAddPage.test.tsx` checkpoint)

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `UserAddPage.test.tsx` registers its `../api` double with `jest.unstable_mockModule('../api', …)` and imports the screen, the shell and `../hooks` dynamically in `beforeAll`, rather than the `jest.mock('../api')` its build instruction names | Reproduce `jest.mock('../api')` verbatim and accept the outcome; move this one suite to the CJS transform; hand-roll a module stub | The module runs Jest 30 in NATIVE ESM (`"type": "module"`, preset `ts-jest/presets/default-esm`, `extensionsToTreatAsEsm`, `transform` with `useESM`), where `jest.mock` is neither hoisted nor applied to statically imported ES modules: the screen would bind to the REAL `../api`, and `api/config.ts` would evaluate `import.meta.env` — precisely what the instruction forbids. `unstable_mockModule` is the pattern the sibling suite `frontend/src/hooks/useSession.test.ts` already establishes for the same specifier | Every module that reaches `../api` must be imported dynamically after registration; a static import added later at the top of the file would silently bypass the double `[frontend/package.json; frontend/src/hooks/useSession.test.ts:L34-L63]` |
| The mocked `../api` exports a module-scope `ApiError` stand-in carrying `status` / `body` / `isOptimisticLockConflict` instead of re-exporting the real class | Re-export `ApiError` from `api/client.ts`; assert only on `error.message` | `useApi` narrows a failure with `err instanceof ApiError`, so the rejection and the hook must share ONE class identity — otherwise the error is re-wrapped with `status` `0`, the backend `body.message` is lost and the screen shows the `Unable to Add User...` fallback, making the duplicate case vacuous. Importing the real class loads `api/client.ts` → `api/config.ts`, which reads `import.meta` | If `ApiError` gains a member the screens read, the stand-in must gain it too; the duplicate case fails loudly if it does not `[frontend/src/api/client.ts:L73-L89; frontend/src/hooks/useApi.ts:L117-L123]` |
| The request assertion pins the DTO member `password`, not the `secUsrPwd` spelling used in the instruction | Rename the member to `secUsrPwd` across `types/user.ts`, `api/users.ts` and the four user screens | `UserAddRequestDto.password` exists at the base commit and is documented there as the `SEC-USR-PWD` carrier mirroring the backend `AddUserRequestDto` JSON contract; `secUsrPwd` is the COBOL field spelling, not an identifier in this codebase. Renaming a frozen wire contract to match prose would break the backend binding and four screens | Reverse traceability to `SEC-USR-PWD` rests on the DTO docstring and the matrix row rather than on the identifier `[frontend/src/types/user.ts:L57-L73; app/cbl/COUSR01C.cbl:L155]` |
| The line-24 assertions use the delivered legend literals `ENTER=Add User`, `F3=Back`, `F4=Clear` while asserting the actions the instruction names (add on ENTER, exit to `/admin` on F3, clear on F4) | Assert the instruction's shorthand `ENTER=Add` / `F3=Exit`; relabel the screen's legend to the shorthand | `COUSR01.bms` line 24 declares `ENTER=Add User  F3=Back  F4=Clear  F12=Exit` and `UserAddPage` reproduces the first three verbatim; the shorthand names each key's function, not its caption, so asserting it would fail against the BMS-verbatim contract, and relabelling the screen would break that contract | None: all three handlers, including the `/admin` exit, are asserted directly `[app/bms/COUSR01.bms:L155-L159]` |
| The screen is rendered inside `Layout` (and a `MemoryRouter` location probe) rather than on its own | Render `<UserAddPage />` alone; export the chrome context so the suite can supply a bespoke provider | `useScreenChrome()` outside `Layout` returns the inert detached context whose `setChrome` is a no-op, so the line-23 message region and the line-24 legend — where every verbatim `COUSR01C` message and every PF key is observed — would not be rendered at all | The suite exercises the screen through the real shell, so a shell regression can also fail it; that is the intended coverage for a UI workflow `[frontend/src/components/Layout.tsx:L80-L106]` |
## 34. Update-User Screen Test Suite (`UserUpdatePage.test.tsx` checkpoint)

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The suite registers its `../api` double with `jest.unstable_mockModule('../api', …)` at module scope and then imports the page, the `Layout` shell and the `../testing/sessionHarness` seeding helpers (§37) dynamically inside `beforeAll`, rather than the `jest.mock('../api')` form named in the file's own build instruction | Reproduce `jest.mock('../api')` literally and accept that the real barrel loads; switch this one suite to the CJS transform so `jest.mock` hoists; spy on the imported namespace object | This project runs Jest 30 in NATIVE ESM (`"type": "module"`, preset `ts-jest/presets/default-esm`, `extensionsToTreatAsEsm`, `NODE_OPTIONS=--experimental-vm-modules`), where `jest.mock` neither hoists nor intercepts an ES-module import. Left literal it would silently not mock: the real `../api` barrel would load `api/client.ts`, construct the axios instance and evaluate `import.meta.env` through `api/config.ts` — the exact outcome the same instruction forbids ("Never evaluate `import.meta`") — and `getUser` / `updateUser` would attempt real HTTP. Static imports are hoisted and evaluated before any statement in the test body, so the consumers must be imported dynamically after registration; no module-registry reset is used, so React and Testing Library stay one instance. The mocked specifier `'../api'` is reproduced verbatim and the sibling suite `frontend/src/hooks/useSession.test.ts` already establishes this exact pattern. Spying was rejected because ES-module namespace objects are sealed | Depends on an `unstable_`-prefixed Jest API that could be renamed in a future major; the repository already relies on it elsewhere, so a rename is a one-line, repo-wide change `[frontend/package.json; frontend/src/hooks/useSession.test.ts:L34-L37]` |
| `ApiError` is published through the mock factory as a locally declared `Error` subclass reproducing the client's public shape (`status`, `body`, `isOptimisticLockConflict`, `name = 'ApiError'`) instead of re-exporting the real class from `../api/client` | Import the real `ApiError` from `../api/client` inside the factory; additionally mock `../api/config` so the real client can load without `import.meta`; drop `ApiError` from the factory | `useApi` imports `ApiError` from `../api` and both narrows with `instanceof` and constructs `new ApiError(0, …)`, so an ES-module link error occurs if the factory omits the name and the narrowing fails if it is a bare `jest.fn`. Reaching the real class means loading `api/client.ts`, which evaluates `import.meta.env` at module initialization. Declaring the class in the suite keeps every import inside the file's declared dependency set, guarantees `import.meta` is never evaluated, and keeps the narrowing exact because the factory and the assertions share one class object | A future change to the real `ApiError` shape would not be observed by this suite; the shape is three readonly fields fixed by `ApiErrorResponse` and is itself covered by `frontend/src/hooks/useApi.test.ts`, which constructs the real class `[frontend/src/api/client.ts:L73-L90; frontend/src/hooks/useApi.ts:L24, L118-L120]` |
| Every case renders `UserUpdatePage` inside the shared `Layout` shell, which adds `frontend/src/components/Layout.tsx` to the suite's imports beyond the four dependencies the file's instruction lists | Render the page bare and assert only on its own form controls; export the screen-chrome provider from `Layout` for tests; hand-roll a provider stand-in in the suite | The page publishes its line-23 message region and its line-24 function-key legend into the shell through `useScreenChrome`, and outside a `Layout` that context is deliberately inert — `setChrome` is a no-op, so the chrome is discarded. Rendered bare, none of the verbatim message literals and none of the ENTER / F3 / F4 / F5 behaviours the instruction mandates are observable at all. `Layout` already exists on disk, is an AAP 0.4.3 in-scope target, and is imported by the page under test, so exercising the real shell also verifies the publication path rather than a stand-in | Couples the suite to the shell: a `Layout` regression fails these 18 cases as well as `Layout.test.tsx`. Accepted — that coupling is the CICS `SEND MAP` relationship being reproduced `[frontend/src/components/Layout.tsx:L85-L105; frontend/src/pages/UserUpdatePage.tsx:L20, L365-L389]` |
| The fetched record is asserted to seed `FNAME`, `LNAME` and `USRTYPE` only, with `PASSWD` asserted as masked, empty and editable, although the instruction lists the password among the fields that "seed into editable fields" | Assert a seeded password value and change the response contract to carry one; seed the field with a placeholder; skip the password assertions | `UserDto` is password-free by frozen contract — the raw password exists only on the request bodies and is encoded server-side — so `GET /users/{id}` returns nothing to seed and the page cannot populate the field. Legacy `COUSR02C` could seed it because it read the plaintext `SEC-USR-PWD` straight out of `USRSEC`; that read disappeared with the BCrypt encoder decision already recorded in this log. The masking, emptiness and editability assertions preserve everything about the field that remains observable | The screen requires the password to be re-entered before every save, which the legacy screen did not; the `Password can NOT be empty...` case pins that behaviour `[frontend/src/types/user.ts:L28-L33, L84-L89; app/cbl/COUSR02C.cbl:L167-L172]` |
| The no-change guard is verified by rejecting `updateUser` with a `400` whose body message is `Please modify to update ...`, not by a client-side comparison | Re-read the record in the page and compare field by field before sending; hold the fetched snapshot in component state and short-circuit the save | `COUSR02C` performed its comparison against the record it had just re-read from `USRSEC`, so the authority for "nothing changed" is the datastore, and `user-service` reproduces it inside the same transaction that would rewrite the row, returning `400` with the verbatim literal. A client-side comparison would duplicate the rule in a second place and could disagree with the service after a concurrent update. The suite therefore asserts the literal is surfaced unchanged and that the request carried the unmodified values | The guard is only reachable with the service running; the backend side is covered by `user-service` `UserServiceTest` / `UserCrudLifecycleTest` `[app/cbl/COUSR02C.cbl:L219-L242; user-service/src/main/java/com/carddemo/user/service/UserService.java:L116]` |
| The suite settles each render by awaiting the line-23 informational literal rather than a seeded field value | Await a field value and assert the message immediately afterwards; insert a fixed delay; wrap the assertions in `act` by hand | The page seeds its fields in one commit and only then publishes chrome from a `useEffect`, so the shell's message region is one render behind the field values. Awaiting the field value first left the message region empty and made the first case fail; the informational literal is published last, so awaiting it settles the whole screen and every remaining assertion can be synchronous. A fixed delay was rejected as flakiness by construction | None observed: the full suite passes on repeated runs, including randomized order in band `[frontend/src/pages/UserUpdatePage.tsx:L219-L226, L365-L389]` |

## 35. SPA Route Table & Role-Gated Guards (`App.tsx` checkpoint)

Non-literal choices made while delivering `frontend/src/App.tsx` — the client-side route table
that re-expresses the CICS menu navigation and `XCTL` transfers as declarative React routes.
Recorded here per the Explainability rule so the route table stays auditable against
`app/cpy/COCOM01Y.cpy` and against the option -> route table in Section 14.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| The account and transaction features declare BOTH a keyed ENTRY route and a deep-linked IDENTIFIER route — `/accounts` + `/accounts/:accountId`, `/accounts/update` + `/accounts/:accountId/update`, `/transactions/view` + `/transactions/:transactionId` — while the card screens declare the entry forms `/cards/view` and `/cards/update` alone | Declare only the parameterised forms the file's own build instruction enumerates; declare only the entry forms Section 14 fixes; or make the identifier a query parameter | A card number is a PAN, so it travels in the router location state and never in a path segment, keeping it out of the address bar and the session history; the account id and the transaction id carry no such constraint and keep their deep-linkable form. The instruction lists only the parameterised forms, but the delivered menu navigates to the ENTRY forms (`MAIN_MENU_TARGETS`, `ADMIN_OPTION_ROUTES`), and those values are already ratified in Section 14. With the entry forms absent, five of the seventeen workflows — account view, account update, card view, card update, transaction view — would have fallen through the catch-all to the caller's home screen. The entry form is also the legacy-faithful shape: `COACTVWC`, `COCRDSLC` and `COTRN01C` each prompt for their own key on their own screen, so the menu `XCTL` carried no identifier. Every param page already treats an absent param as the blank-key screen (`accountId ?? ''`, an early-return effect), so one component serves both routes with no branch | Two paths reach one screen for accounts and transactions, so a future rename must move both; the card screens cannot be deep-linked at all. Pinned by `App.test.tsx` |
| `/users/update` and `/users/delete` are STATIC paths | `/users/:userId/update` and `/users/:userId/delete`, which the instruction offers as an equally acceptable form | `UserListPage` dispatches the row selection as `navigate(UPDATE_USER_ROUTE, { state: { userId } })` — the id travels in router state, not in the path — and Section 14's admin option table already fixes the bare `/users/update` and `/users/delete` values | A user id cannot be deep-linked or bookmarked on these two screens, exactly as the 3270 screens could not be entered with a pre-selected row |
| `RequireAdmin` repeats the authentication check rather than nesting inside `RequireAuth` | Nest the admin routes inside the `RequireAuth` group so the authentication branch is inherited | The instruction requires `RequireAdmin` itself to send an unauthenticated visitor to the sign-on screen and a signed-in non-administrator to `/menu`; nesting would make the first branch unreachable, because the outer guard would always redirect first. Duplicating one boolean test keeps each guard independently correct and independently testable | Two guards read `isAuthenticated`; a future change to the unauthenticated target must be applied in both. Both branches are asserted by `App.test.tsx` |
| Literal path segments are declared AHEAD of their parameterised siblings in source order | Rely solely on React Router 7's static-over-dynamic branch ranking | Ranking alone is correct, but source order makes the intent legible and survives a future switch to an order-sensitive matcher. The instruction asks for exactly this for `/transactions/add` | None. Proven behaviourally rather than assumed: at `/cards/view` the card filter is empty and NO request is issued, while `/cards/<pan>` seeds the filter and fetches — so `view` is never captured as `:cardNumber` |
| The shared shell is ONE pathless parent route, `element={<Layout><Outlet /></Layout>}`, wrapping every route including `/signon` | `<Layout><Routes>...</Routes></Layout>` outside the route tree; or a minimal shell variant for the sign-on screen | Every one of the seventeen pages publishes its transaction id, program name, titles, message and PF keys through `useScreenChrome`, which only exists inside `Layout` — `SignonPage` included — so a screen rendered outside the shell would silently lose its header and key bar. `Layout` exposes only `children`, so no minimal variant exists to select | The sign-on screen carries the same frame as every other screen, which is what the 3270 mapsets did (`COSGN00.bms` declares the identical header rows) |
| The entry route is declared as `path="/"` on a child of the pathless shell | An `index` route | The pathless parent contributes no path segment, so `/` remains a valid absolute child path and React Router raises no "Absolute route path nested under" condition. The explicit path also matches the instruction's `"/"` literal | An `index` route would behave identically; the choice is legibility |
| The catch-all `*` redirects to `ENTRY_ROUTE` (`/`), which `HomeRedirect` re-resolves to the caller's home screen | Render a NotFound screen inside the shell, which the instruction offers as an alternative | A 3270 terminal had no not-found state: an unrecognised transaction id returned the operator to a menu. Redirecting through `/` reuses `HomeRedirect`, so an unknown deep link resolves by ROLE — `homeRouteForRole` returns `/admin` for `'A'`, `/menu` for `'U'` and `/signon` for a caller the server reports as signed out — and invents no new screen or copy | The visitor is not told the path was unknown. Both hops pass `replace`, so no intermediate `/` is left in history; `App.test.tsx` asserts the resolved route per role and asserts `useNavigationType()` is `REPLACE` |
| Only `App` is exported; `RequireAuth`, `RequireAdmin`, `HomeRedirect` and `homeRouteForRole` stay module-private | Export the guards so sibling tests can mount them in isolation | The instruction mandates a single default export, and every guard branch is reachable — and asserted — through `App` itself, so exporting them would widen the public surface for no coverage gain | A future file needing the guards must export them at that point |
| Internal imports name the exact modules `./types/session` and `./hooks/useSession` rather than the `./types` / `./hooks` barrels the page components use | Import from the barrels, matching the seventeen pages | These two modules are the declared dependencies of this file, and `Layout.tsx` already sets the direct-module precedent with `import { useSession } from '../hooks/useSession'`. Naming them directly keeps the dependency edge explicit | This file's import style differs from the pages'; both resolve to the same modules, so behaviour is identical |
| `/signon` is deliberately NOT guarded against an already-authenticated visitor | Redirect a signed-in caller from `/signon` to their home screen | The instruction declares `/signon` the one public route and enumerates only the two guards; adding a third redirect would be observable behaviour the contract does not ask for. It would also be wrong: CICS transaction `CC00` was re-enterable at any time, and both `MainMenuPage` (PF3 `RETURN-TO-SIGNON-SCREEN`) and `SignonPage` (`F3=Exit`) call `signOut()` and then navigate to `/signon` | Visiting `/signon` while signed in is the path that exposes the known `POST /auth/signon` 500-on-re-authentication defect recorded in Section 14; the defect belongs to auth-service, not to the route table |
| `App.test.tsx` replaces ONLY the transport — `jest.unstable_mockModule('./api/client', …)` — and establishes a session by having the stubbed `GET /session` publish an identity that the PRODUCTION probe reads through `resolveSessionFromServer()` | Mock the whole `../api` barrel as `useSession.test.ts` does; keep a `__setSession` store mutator exported from `hooks/useSession.ts`; or let the real axios client run against the live gateway | Mounting `App` loads all seventeen pages, so a barrel mock would have to re-declare every request function or leave pages calling `undefined`; replacing the axios instance one level lower keeps every real api wrapper — including `getSessionIdentity` — in the graph while guaranteeing no network, no axios and no `import.meta` evaluation. A store mutator was REMOVED rather than kept (see §37): with one, the suite would still pass if the SPA's session bootstrap or its route guards were deleted, so it could not fail for the reason it exists. Screen identity is asserted from the `pgm-name` the shell renders, which is data-independent, so the suite pins routing rather than any page's fetch | The suite does not exercise real payload shapes; that is each page's own suite's job. Its 74 cases cover the 21 named routes, both guards, the role-resolved entry route and the catch-all |
| **RESOLVED.** The one-based screen counter is converted to the route's zero-based `page` in ONE place, `toWirePage` in `frontend/src/api/users.ts` (`page > 1 ? page - 1 : 0`); `UserController.listUsers` keeps `defaultValue = "0"` and `UserService.listUsers` keeps feeding the value into `PageRequest.of(pageNumber, PAGE_SIZE)` unchanged | Change `UserListPage` to hold a zero-based counter; or change `user-service` to treat `page` as one-based | The screen counter is a legacy artefact — `COUSR00` paints a one-based page number and PF7 / PF8 step it — while `PageRequest` is zero-based by definition, so the two indices are genuinely different contracts and the conversion belongs at the wire boundary between them. Converting in the api module keeps every page component on the counter the mapset displays and leaves the Java service's `Pageable` semantics untouched; the earlier form of this row recorded the unconverted state, in which `GET /users?page=1` asked for rows 11-20 and the grid rendered empty | The conversion is invisible to callers, so a future caller that bypasses `frontend/src/api/users.ts` and issues its own request must apply it. Pinned by `UserListPage.test.tsx`, which asserts the browse request for the first page and for every PF7 / PF8 hop `[frontend/src/api/users.ts; frontend/src/pages/UserListPage.tsx; user-service UserService.listUsers]` |
## 36. Delete-User Screen Suite (`UserDeletePage.test.tsx`, mapset `COUSR03`)

Decisions taken while delivering the colocated Jest / React Testing Library suite for the
administrator delete-user screen (`COUSR03` / `CU03` / `app/cbl/COUSR03C.cbl`).

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| `UserDeletePage.test.tsx` mocks `../api` with `jest.unstable_mockModule('../api', …)` and imports the screen, the shell and the session seam inside `beforeAll`, rather than with the `jest.mock('../api')` its build instruction names | Reproduce `jest.mock('../api')` verbatim and accept whatever it does; switch this one suite to the CJS transform so `jest.mock` is hoisted; inject `getUser` / `deleteUser` into the page as props or context so no module mock is needed | This project runs Jest 30 in NATIVE ESM (`"type": "module"`, preset `ts-jest/presets/default-esm`, `extensionsToTreatAsEsm`, `transform` with `useESM`), where `jest.mock` is NOT hoisted above the ES-module import graph: a static `import UserDeletePage from './UserDeletePage'` binds the real `../api` before any statement in the test body runs, so the factory would be registered too late and the suite would issue real axios calls for the read and the delete — the delete-only-after-confirm outcome it exists to prove would not be observable. `unstable_mockModule` paired with a post-registration dynamic `import()` is the form Jest documents for native ESM, and the sibling `useSession.test.ts` already mocks the very same specifier this way, so this reuses an established repository pattern rather than inventing one. Forking the transform for a single file would change the whole module's test infrastructure; injecting the calls would alter the page's frozen contract | The suite depends on a Jest API still carrying the `unstable_` prefix, and the page and shell must be loaded in `beforeAll` instead of statically — both already true of the sibling suite, which passes unchanged `[frontend/src/hooks/useSession.test.ts; frontend/package.json jest.preset]` |
| The mocked module publishes an `ApiError` class DECLARED IN THE SUITE, reproducing `client.ts`'s `(status, message, body?, isOptimisticLockConflict = false)` constructor, its three members and `name = 'ApiError'` | Re-export the real class from an async factory (`const actual = await import('../api/client')`); reject with a plain `Error`; reject with an object literal carrying a `status` field | The page narrows failures with `error instanceof ApiError` and `useApi` normalizes with `new ApiError(0, …)`, and both import that symbol from `../api` — so the mock must publish a real class, and it must be the same class the suite constructs its rejections from, or the `404` → `User ID NOT found...` mapping silently falls through to `Unable to lookup User...` and the miss cases would pass for the wrong reason. Loading the real class would evaluate `../api/config`, which reads Vite's `import.meta.env`, and would build an axios instance with request/response interceptors inside a unit suite; the instruction for this file forbids evaluating `import.meta`. A plain `Error` or an object literal fails the `instanceof` narrowing that is itself under test | A future change to `ApiError`'s constructor or members must be mirrored here; the stand-in pins the four-parameter shape, so a divergence surfaces as a `tsc --noEmit` error rather than a silent drift `[frontend/src/api/client.ts; frontend/src/api/config.ts; frontend/src/hooks/useApi.ts]` |
| Every case renders the screen INSIDE the real `Layout` shell, and each act on a record waits for the SETTLED shell — the read-only field value, the line-23 prompt, and a re-enabled `F5=Delete` — before a function key is pressed | Render `UserDeletePage` standalone (its `useScreenChrome` default is deliberately inert); mock `Layout`; wait only for the body field to carry the record | Line 23 and line 24 belong to the shell, so the verbatim messages (`User ID can NOT be empty...`, `User ID NOT found...`, `User <id> has been deleted ...`) and the `ENTER=Fetch  F3=Back  F4=Clear  F5=Delete` legend are observable only through it — a standalone render discards the published chrome and none of the required outcomes could be asserted. Because the page publishes chrome from an effect into the shell's own state, the legend lands ONE render behind the body: waiting only for the field left `PFKeyBar` still holding the in-flight legend with `enabled: false`, so an F4 / F5 keypress was filtered out and the case failed for a reason that had nothing to do with the screen's logic (observed, then fixed). Requiring the prompt AND the re-enabled key is the condition that proves the whole 24-row frame has settled | The wait helper is coupled to the confirmation prompt, so a screen that stopped publishing it would time out rather than fail on the specific assertion `[app/bms/COUSR03.bms:L140-L148; frontend/src/components/Layout.tsx; frontend/src/components/PFKeyBar.tsx]` |
| The suite pins the legend to EXACTLY the four line-24 keys, in order, and never exercises PF12 even though `COUSR03C` also branches on `DFHPF12` | Assert a fifth `F12` key and its transfer to the administrator menu; assert only that the four keys are present, without pinning their count or order | `COUSR03.bms` legends exactly `ENTER=Fetch  F3=Back  F4=Clear  F5=Delete` on line 24 and the page declares those four keys; a statement that enumerates its members is satisfied only by implementing every member and no more, so asserting a fifth key would require unrequested observable behavior from the screen. Pinning the count and the order makes the assertion fail when a key is added or reordered, instead of tolerating it silently | The legacy `DFHPF12 → COADM01C` shortcut has no covering case on this screen; F3 reaches the same route and is covered `[app/bms/COUSR03.bms:L144-L148; app/cbl/COUSR03C.cbl:L119-L125]` |
| Fixtures are the rows the seed migration really loads — `ADMIN001` as the signed-on administrator (`'A'`) and `USER0002` / `AJITH` / `KUMAR` (`'U'`) as the delete target | Invent placeholder ids and names; generate them per run | Reusing the seeded `security_users` rows keeps the ids the suite asserts identical to the ones a running deployment serves for this workflow, and keeps the eight-character `SEC-USR-ID` width honest rather than accidentally testing a shorter key | A renamed seed row means updating this suite; the ids are matched only against the mock, so no database is involved `[carddemo-common/src/main/resources/db/migration/V3__seed_test_data.sql:L48,L54; app/cpy/CSUSR01Y.cpy]` |

## 37. Code-Review Remediation — Fail-Closed Session Resolution, Live Security Controls, Production-Wired Frontend Coverage

Decisions taken while resolving the end-to-end security-gate code review. Every row here records a
choice where the review named a defect but not a unique remedy, or where the remedy reversed a
decision recorded earlier in this log. Rows that supersede an earlier decision name the section they
replace, and the superseded row carries a forward pointer, so the log reads as a history rather than
as a set of contradictions.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| **SUPERSEDES §13.** The `__setSession(user, role)` store mutator is REMOVED from `frontend/src/hooks/useSession.ts`, and the session is seeded instead by `frontend/src/testing/sessionHarness.ts`, a test-only module whose `seedSignedOnSession(userId, role)` drives the PRODUCTION `GET /session` probe through the public `useSession().refresh` | Keep the mutator and merely add composition coverage alongside it; move the mutator to a test-only module but keep it writing the private `setState`; gate it behind `import.meta.env.MODE !== 'production'`; mock `../hooks/useSession` wholesale per suite | With the mutator in place every one of the eighteen frontend suites took its identity from a function that writes the store directly, so **not one of them could fail if the SPA's session bootstrap or its route guards were deleted** — the suites asserted the store's derived state, never the code that populates it. Adding composition coverage without removing the mutator would have left that hole in eighteen suites and closed it in one. Relocating the mutator without changing what it writes moves the file but keeps the defect, because the seam would still bypass `probeIdentityOnce`. An environment gate is a build-mode check, not a coverage property: the suites would still be blind. Mocking the hook is worse still — it replaces the very `isAuthenticated` derivation under test. Routing the seam through `refresh` inverts the dependency: the harness programs the suite's own `getSessionIdentity` double and then lets the production code path read it, so seeding EXERCISES the bootstrap instead of substituting for it | Seeding became asynchronous, because `act()`'s synchronous form does not flush the microtask queue that `refresh` awaits; all eighteen suites moved to `async beforeEach`/`afterEach`, and eighty-two seeding call sites were migrated. The harness must also RESET the identity double to a rejecting stub after seeding: `probeIdentityOnce` memoizes into module-level state that `signOut`/`abandonSession` null, so a post-logout remount would otherwise re-probe and silently sign the caller back in. Test count rose 642 → 660 with zero skips `[frontend/src/hooks/useSession.ts; frontend/src/testing/sessionHarness.ts]` |
| A new `frontend/src/AppComposition.test.tsx` mounts the REAL `App` with nothing under `src` mocked — only `apiClient.defaults.adapter` is replaced — and its stub MODELS THE SERVER, so a successful `POST /logout` clears the identity and the next `GET /session` answers 401 | Assert the same paths inside the existing page suites; drive a real browser through the flows; mock `../api` at the barrel as `useSession.test.ts` does | The control C‑05 named is the wiring BETWEEN the session module, the axios interceptor and the route guards, so any double placed inside `src` removes the thing under test. Mounting `App` loads all seventeen pages, so a barrel mock would have to re-declare every request function — and each stub would be another place the wiring could be faked. Replacing only the transport adapter leaves the interceptor chain, the probe, the guards and the shell all genuine. Making the stub stateful is what makes revocation observable: a logout that did not actually clear server-side identity would still let the next probe succeed, and the test would pass for the wrong reason | A suite that passes proves nothing about whether it CAN fail, so each of the four controls was deleted in turn and the suite re-run: removing `void probeIdentityOnce()` fails 1 case, forcing `RequireAuth` to always render `<Outlet />` fails 3, removing the `sessionExpiryHandlers` fan-out from `client.ts` fails 1, and removing `await signOut()` from `MainMenuPage`'s F3 fails 2. Every mutation was reverted after observation. The stub parks the session probe behind an explicit `sessionProbeGate` released in `afterEach`, because a first implementation left a request parked forever whenever an assertion failed, converting a clean failure into a timeout `[frontend/src/AppComposition.test.tsx; frontend/src/api/client.ts; frontend/src/App.tsx]` |
| **SUPERSEDES the two negative assertions at `AccountUpdatePage.test.tsx:785‑808`.** The assertions that froze the ABSENCE of the 401 → `/signon` redirect are deleted along with the suite's `/signon` probe route, the first case renamed to describe what it does prove, the second made to assert positively that the update call was issued exactly once, and a module-level `:note:` points at the suite where the transition IS asserted | Keep the negative assertions and annotate them; make this suite mount the real client so the redirect happens here; delete the two cases outright | An assertion that a mocked-away control does not fire encodes the mock's limitation as expected behaviour: had the production interceptor been deleted, those two cases would have gone green. Mounting the real client inside a page suite duplicates the composition layer and re-introduces `import.meta` evaluation this suite deliberately avoids. Deleting the cases loses the genuine coverage they carry — the 409 and 412 conflict paths — so the fix removes only the negative claim and keeps the positive one | The 401 transition is now asserted in exactly one place; the `:note:` is what keeps that discoverable from here `[frontend/src/pages/AccountUpdatePage.test.tsx; frontend/src/AppComposition.test.tsx]` |
| All five session-reading controllers delegate to ONE shared `SessionContextSupport.require(request)` in `carddemo-common`, which throws `IllegalStateException` rather than returning a blank context; `store(request, context)` writes back only to an EXISTING session | Leave each controller's private resolver in place and fix the blank-context default in each; add a `@ModelAttribute` resolver; inject `SessionContext` as a session-scoped bean | Five controllers each carried their own resolver, and the gateway's returned a NEWLY CONSTRUCTED blank `SessionContext` when the session held none — so an unauthenticated menu request was served under a blank authority instead of being refused. Fixing that five times invites the sixth copy to drift; one shared helper makes the fail-closed behaviour a property of the library rather than a habit of each controller. A `@ModelAttribute` resolver or a session-scoped bean would both silently materialize a context on demand, which is precisely the failure mode being removed. `store` deliberately does NOT create a session, so a read-only request on a dead session cannot resurrect one | `require` throws rather than returning `Optional`, so a controller that forgets to call it fails loudly instead of proceeding blank; every protected route is already gated by the shared filter chain, so a request cannot legitimately arrive without a context, and the throw is therefore an invariant breach rather than an expected path. Seven test suites had ENCODED the blank-context default as expected behaviour and were corrected `[carddemo-common/src/main/java/com/carddemo/common/dto/SessionContextSupport.java; api-gateway/.../MenuController.java]` |
| `SessionPrincipalIndex.deregister` is made LIVE by a new `SessionIndexLogoutHandler` added to the gateway's logout chain, rather than by deleting the method or by softening the count it feeds | Delete `deregister` as dead code; keep it and reword `sessionsRevoked` so the number stops claiming to be a revocation count; call `deregister` from an `HttpSessionListener` | The method existed with zero call sites, so the `sessionsRevoked` figure the index reports could never decrease and the administrative revocation path was inert — a declared control that did nothing. Deleting it would have removed the capability the index exists for; rewording the metric would have documented the gap rather than closed it. A `LogoutHandler` runs inside Spring Security's own logout sequence, where the authenticated principal and the session id are both still available, which an `HttpSessionListener` cannot guarantee for an expiring session | The handler is registered only on the gateway, the single service that terminates logout; in the other services `deregister` remains a no-op because the index itself is a no-op there, which the class documents explicitly `[carddemo-common/.../security/SessionIndexLogoutHandler.java; api-gateway/.../config/SecurityConfig.java:L164]` |
| `SessionContextAuthenticationFilter` re-checks revocation AFTER reading the session attribute, closing the read-then-use window rather than documenting it | Accept the window as negligible; take a lock around the read; re-resolve the whole session | The window is small but real: a session revoked between the attribute read and its use would have been honoured for that one request, which is exactly the request an administrator revoking access cares about. A lock would serialize every authenticated request through a shared monitor for a check that is already cheap; re-resolving the session repeats the Redis round trip the filter just made | The re-check costs one additional index lookup per authenticated request `[carddemo-common/.../SessionContextAuthenticationFilter.java]` |
| `@EnableMethodSecurity` is added to `user-service`'s `SecurityConfig` so the `@PreAuthorize("hasRole('ADMIN')")` annotations already present on the service become enforcing | Delete the annotations and rely on the URL rules alone; move the checks into the service bodies as explicit `if` statements | The annotations were present and inert — Spring Boot does not enable method security by default — so the log's own claim that administrator-only user operations are enforced at the method level was false, and the only real gate was the URL pattern. Deleting them would have left the URL rule as the sole control and made the log's claim false in the other direction. Explicit `if` statements re-implement in nine places what one annotation expresses, and would not be visible to a security audit of the annotations | The annotation is the ONLY switch that makes those nine `@PreAuthorize` declarations effective, so removing it silently disables them again; two tests now invoke the beans directly, outside any HTTP request, so the method-level gate is asserted independently of the URL rules `[user-service/.../config/SecurityConfig.java:L77]` |
| The unused `StatementOutputResolver` and its test are DELETED, leaving the hardened `BatchOutputPathResolver` as the single output-path authority; the vestigial `carddemo.reporting.output-dir` key and its compose and Kubernetes projections go with it | Harden `StatementOutputResolver` to match `BatchOutputPathResolver`; keep it and wire the live writer to it instead | The class resolved a caller-supplied path with no traversal defence, had zero production call sites, and duplicated a component that already normalizes and confines the path — so it was an unreachable CWE‑22 hazard, not a control. Hardening it would produce two components with the same responsibility and one of them still unused; wiring the live writer to it would swap the hardened implementation for the unhardened one | The reporting service now has no reporting-specific output key at all; a future reporting-only root must reintroduce one deliberately rather than inherit a dead key `[reporting-service/.../StatementOutputResolver.java (deleted); carddemo-common/.../batch/BatchOutputPathResolver.java]` |
| The unused `carddemo-common` `config/BatchOutputConfig.java` is DELETED in favour of the live `batch/BatchPathConfig.java` | Keep both and mark one deprecated; merge them | Two configuration classes bound the same batch output root and only `BatchPathConfig` had consumers, so the other could only ever diverge from the value actually in force. There is nothing in the deleted class to merge that `BatchPathConfig` does not already do | The traceability row that pointed at the deleted file is repointed rather than dropped, so batch output-path coverage stays traceable `[carddemo-common/.../batch/BatchPathConfig.java]` |
| Both duplicate log statements that emitted an UNMASKED request path are deleted, leaving the masked `loggedPath`/`loggedMessage` pair as the only logging route out of `GlobalExceptionHandler` | Mask the duplicates too; downgrade them to `debug`; keep them behind a config flag | Each duplicate sat directly beneath a correct masked call, so it added no diagnostic information whatever — only an unmasked path and, in the PII-crypto case, a full stack trace. Masking or downgrading them keeps a redundant statement that a future edit can un-mask again; a flag makes the leak reachable by configuration. Deletion makes the property structural: `extractPath` now has exactly ONE caller, `loggedPath`, which wraps it in `SensitiveDataMasker.maskPan` | The optimistic-locking case was the higher risk of the two — `V7__cards_optimistic_lock.sql` put `@Version` on `cards` whose `@Id` is the card number, so two concurrent card updates produced a WARN carrying both the raw path and a JPA message embedding the number `[carddemo-common/.../config/GlobalExceptionHandler.java]` |
| Review-tracker identifiers and inline rationale narratives are stripped from all source and resource files, with the reasoning relocated here | Leave the identifiers in place; keep the narratives but drop the identifiers | Rule 1 places rationale in this log and not in code comments, and Rule 4 requires docstrings to state purpose, output and parameters without commentary; a comment naming an external tracker item is unreadable to anyone without that tracker and goes stale the moment the item closes. Relocating rather than deleting keeps the reasoning auditable | The strip removed parenthetical citations from mid-sentence and left twenty-one orphaned `#. ` fragments across twelve YAML files, each of which was subsequently rejoined to the sentence above it; the same class of damage is why the remaining edits in this remediation were applied as exact literal replacements rather than by pattern `[docker-compose.yml; k8s/deployment-*.yaml; */src/main/resources/application.yml]` |
| **REVISED.** Three suites that mocked shared production components now mount the REAL ones: `AdminMenuPage.test.tsx` drops its whole-`react-router` mock for a `LocationProbe` that reads the resolved path, and `TranViewPage.test.tsx` and `UserListPage.test.tsx` drop their `Layout` mocks and the chrome-capture scaffolding that went with them | Keep the mocks and assert on the spies; keep the `Layout` mock but assert the published chrome object | §26 and §27 of this log already reject exactly these two patterns — mocking `../components/Layout`, and mocking `useNavigate` to assert on the spy — because both assert that the page ASKED for something rather than that the user can SEE it. A navigate spy passes even when no route resolves; a mocked shell passes even when the shell renders nothing. Asserting on a captured chrome object has the same defect one level down | Mounting the real `Layout` in `UserListPage.test.tsx` surfaced three act warnings whose cause is specific and worth recording: Testing Library's async wrapper for `user-event` DISABLES the act environment for the duration of the callback, so the shell's state-based `setChrome` — a no-op under the mocked shell — flushed outside `act`. Substituting `fireEvent.change` inside an explicit `await act(async …)` in that suite's row-selection helper keeps the whole chain in one scope and returns the count to zero; the now-vestigial `settleChrome` helper, the unused `pressEnter` parameter and the unused `UserEventSession` type were removed with it `[frontend/src/pages/AdminMenuPage.test.tsx; TranViewPage.test.tsx; UserListPage.test.tsx]` |
| Every one of the seven write screens asserts that its entry fields are DISABLED while a write is in flight and that a second AID cannot issue a duplicate request | Cover the guard on one representative screen; assert only that the button is disabled | The guard is per-screen state, so a representative case proves nothing about the other six; the review found it asserted on one screen out of seven. Asserting only the button leaves the physical-key path uncovered, and the shell dispatches AID keys from the keyboard independently of the legend — which is the path a user actually repeats a submission on | Each case defers the write promise, asserts every entry field is disabled, fires the AID from BOTH the legend and the physical key, asserts the call count stays at one, then releases and asserts the fields are re-enabled; `toBeDisabled` assertions rose from 4 in one suite to 18 across eight. `UserUpdatePage` additionally needed a password typed, because `COUSR03C`'s sibling validation rejects a blank password before the write is ever attempted `[frontend/src/pages/TranAddPage.test.tsx; BillPayPage.test.tsx; AccountUpdatePage.test.tsx; CardUpdatePage.test.tsx; UserAddPage.test.tsx; UserUpdatePage.test.tsx; UserDeletePage.test.tsx]` |
| `AccountViewPage.test.tsx` is fed the MASKED identifiers the API actually returns, and asserts the raw values are absent from the document | Keep the raw fixture and assert the rendered text matches it; assert masking in the mapper's unit test only | The suite's fixture carried raw SSN, government-issued id and EFT account values, but `AccountMapper` masks all three before they leave the service — so the suite asserted a payload the SPA can never receive, and would have kept passing if the masking were removed. A mapper-level test proves the mapper masks; it does not prove the screen never renders an unmasked value it was handed | The fixture now pins the mask SHAPES (`***-**-6789`, `***********4321`, `******0042`), so a change to `PiiMasker`'s visible-tail length or mask character fails here as well as in the masker's own suite — intended, since the rendered shape is the user-visible contract `[frontend/src/pages/AccountViewPage.test.tsx; carddemo-common/.../PiiMasker.java; carddemo-common/.../AccountMapper.java]` |
| `CardListPage.test.tsx` pins the `COCRDLI` body captions verbatim with a non-normalizing text matcher | Assert the captions with the default normalizer; assert only the column headings | The default normalizer collapses runs of whitespace, and these captions are column-aligned 3270 text whose spacing IS the layout; a collapsing matcher would pass against a caption whose alignment had been lost. The heading and the two prompts were previously unasserted altogether | The heading assertion matches by accessible name and level and pins `id="card-list-heading"`, because the shell renders its own level-2 title line and a bare text match was ambiguous between the two `[app/bms/COCRDLI.bms; frontend/src/pages/CardListPage.test.tsx]` |
| **C‑01 reconciliation.** Canonical index 368 is `frontend/src/App.tsx`, and the designated change is DELIVERED — the role-resolving `homeRouteForRole` helper, the `HomeRedirect` element, the `ENTRY_ROUTE` (`/`) and `ADMIN_MENU_ROUTE` constants, the entry route and the role-resolved catch-all — rather than the index being re-pointed at `App.test.tsx` | Correct the canonical index to name `App.test.tsx`, which was genuinely new; declare index 368 satisfied by the pre-existing route table | Both governance documents already described an `App.tsx` containing `homeRouteForRole` and `HomeRedirect` and traced them to `COSGN00C`'s role-conditional `XCTL` transfer, so the file, not the index, was the thing out of step: re-pointing the index would have made the documents permanently wrong about the delivered design. Substituting the test file was explicitly rejected by the review and would in any case leave the `XCTL` transfer with no target construct | The route table grew from 21 to 22 `path=` entries (the entry route) and `App.test.tsx` from 60 to 74 cases. `/signon` remains deliberately unguarded against an already-authenticated visitor — see §35, which is unchanged by this row: the transfer semantics are expressed at `/` and at the catch-all, not by refusing the sign-on screen `[frontend/src/App.tsx:L100-L119,L165,L224; app/cbl/COSGN00C.cbl:L227,L231-L237]` |
| **C‑02 reconciliation.** The fourteen regression paths that do not exist in HEAD are recorded as a SPECIFICATION defect against the consolidated architecture, with each absent path given a traceability row naming the file that now carries its control — not reinstated as files | Recreate the fourteen paths so the inventory matches; drop them from the inventory silently | Every control the fourteen paths carried was verified present after consolidation: the seven database objects and all eleven tables in `carddemo-common`'s `V1__create_schema.sql`, the `{bcrypt}` user seeding in `V3__seed_test_data.sql`, and `CorrelationIdFilter`, `CorrelationIdContext` and `SessionRedisConfig` in `carddemo-common`. Recreating per-service migration files would give Flyway two sources for the same objects and reintroduce exactly the split history the consolidation removed. Dropping them silently would leave no record that the controls survived the move | The consolidation is why `auth-service` owns no migrations despite owning `security_users`; the shared history is the single ordering authority, which the service configurations now state correctly rather than claiming Flyway is disabled `[carddemo-common/src/main/resources/db/migration/V1__create_schema.sql; V3__seed_test_data.sql; carddemo-common/.../config/CorrelationIdFilter.java; SessionRedisConfig.java]` |
| The compose `frontend` service is gated behind a `frontend` PROFILE, so `docker compose up --build` brings up the datastores, the nine services and the observability stack, and the browser tier is opted into with `--profile frontend` | Start the frontend unconditionally with everything else; ship it in a separate compose file | The backend stack is what the integration tests, the metrics scrape and the health probes exercise, and it is what `README-target.md` documents as the default bring-up; making the browser tier optional keeps that default fast and keeps a frontend build failure from blocking a backend-only run. A second compose file would duplicate the network, the secret and the gateway dependency declarations | A reader who runs the documented default gets no SPA on `localhost:3000` and must know to pass the profile, which the comment above the service states explicitly. The image tag matches `k8s/deployment-frontend.yaml` so the manifests apply against the artifact compose builds `[docker-compose.yml:L529; k8s/deployment-frontend.yaml; README-target.md]` |
| CSRF is enforced at the API GATEWAY ONLY; each downstream service calls `csrf.disable()` while still accepting the session cookie, and the safety of that split rests on a TOPOLOGICAL precondition | Enforce CSRF independently in all nine services; make the services stateless and carry identity in a bearer token instead of a cookie | Per-service CSRF means nine token stores for one browser session and a token that must survive gateway routing, and the gateway is the only component a browser can reach. The split is therefore correct — but it is conditionally correct, and the condition is recorded here because it is invisible from any single service's configuration: NO downstream service publishes a host port, all nine are reachable only from the internal `carddemo-net`, and the gateway is the sole ingress. If any service were ever exposed directly — a debug port, a `NodePort`, an ingress rule per service — that service would accept cookie-authenticated cross-site writes with no CSRF defence at all | This is the one control in the system whose correctness depends on deployment topology rather than on code, so it cannot be verified by reading a service in isolation. Exposing a downstream service is the specific change that breaks it `[api-gateway/.../config/SecurityConfig.java; docker-compose.yml; k8s/service-*.yaml]` |
| The inert `carddemo.jwt` bearer-token channel is removed from `frontend/src/api/client.ts` | Keep it for a future token-based mode; keep it and document it as unused | The client read a token from storage and attached it as a bearer header, but no service has ever issued or accepted one — authentication is the session cookie throughout. An unused credential channel invites a future reader to populate it and believe it authenticates something, and it is a second place a credential could come to rest in browser storage | A token-based mode must reintroduce the channel deliberately, together with a service that issues the token `[frontend/src/api/client.ts]` |
| The `44×44` accessibility comment on `BillPayPage` and its two siblings is replaced with the `24×24` target the code actually implements, citing WCAG 2.5.8 AA | Enlarge the targets to 44×44 to match the comment; delete the comment | The comment asserted a 44×44 minimum that the stylesheet does not implement, so it read as a satisfied requirement while the code met a different one; 44×44 is the AAA-level 2.5.5 target, whereas the delivered 24×24 satisfies AA-level 2.5.8, which is the bar this project holds. Enlarging the targets would change the delivered layout to match a comment rather than a design decision, and the AAP names no design system to arbitrate. Deleting the comment loses the standard reference entirely | The three sibling comments now state one consistent, accurate rule; a future move to AAA must change the stylesheet and the comment together `[frontend/src/pages/BillPayPage.tsx; TranListPage.tsx; CardListPage.tsx]` |
| `eslint.config.js` gains a `'src/testing/**'` override extending the Jest-globals block | Add the harness to the existing test-file glob; disable the rule for the file | `src/testing/sessionHarness.ts` is test infrastructure that is not itself a `*.test.tsx` file, so it fell outside the glob that grants Jest globals and its `jest.fn()`-shaped parameter tripped `no-undef`. A path-scoped override keeps the grant narrow; widening the test glob would extend Jest globals to any future non-test file placed there, and a file-level disable would hide genuine undefined references | The override is keyed to the directory, so anything added under `src/testing/` inherits Jest globals `[frontend/eslint.config.js]` |

### 37.1 Findings declined on AAP precedence (end-to-end security gate)

Four findings of this review cannot be closed by changing code, because the change each asks for is
forbidden by the frozen AAP. Each row states the residual exposure plainly, names the AAP section
that governs, and records the mitigations that ARE in force, so the decision is auditable and can be
revisited by whoever owns the AAP. Rows in §19.1 decline the equivalent findings of the previous
checkpoint under the same reasoning; these rows restate the position against this review's numbering
and against the code as delivered today.

| Decision | Alternatives | Rationale | Risk |
|----------|--------------|-----------|------|
| **AAP PRECEDENCE (finding C‑07).** No `@PreAuthorize`, ownership predicate or session-derived filter is added to `GET`/`PUT /accounts/{id}`, `GET`/`PUT /cards/{cardNumber}`, `GET /transactions/{id}` or `POST /billpay` | Add an ownership predicate deriving the caller's customer id from the session and reject any key not linked to it; filter every repository read by the session principal; annotate each method with a per-resource expression | The legacy programs authorize by SCREEN REACHABILITY, never by row ownership: `app/csd/CARDDEMO.CSD` carries `RESSEC(NO)` and `CMDSEC(NO)` on all eighteen transaction definitions, so CICS performed no per-resource check, and `SEC-USR-TYPE` gates which SCREENS the menu offers rather than which rows a screen may read. AAP §0.6.7 specifies the security model exhaustively — a password encoder and the `'A'`/`'U'` → `ROLE_ADMIN`/`ROLE_USER` mapping — and specifies no entitlement model; §0.7.1 requires validation rules and workflow sequencing to be reproduced exactly, and §0.7.6 forbids modifying business logic beyond the technology conversion. A per-principal entitlement filter is therefore a NEW business rule, not a migration of an existing one | **RESIDUAL, STATED PLAINLY:** any authenticated CardDemo user can read or update any account, card or transaction by supplying its key, and can post a bill payment against any account. This is a genuine IDOR exposure that the delivered code does not prevent. The review's own adjudication is that it requires a human decision on the AAP's internal conflict between §0.7.1 functional equivalence and a general security mandate, and it is NOT within an implementing agent's authority to resolve by editing either the code or the AAP. Mitigations in force: authentication is required on every one of these routes, the routes are reachable only through the gateway, and administrator-only operations ARE enforced at both the URL and — since this pass — the method level. Closing this requires an AAP amendment adding an entitlement model `[app/csd/CARDDEMO.CSD; AAP §0.6.7, §0.7.1, §0.7.6]` |
| **AAP PRECEDENCE (finding C‑08).** The card number continues to travel as a path segment on `GET`/`PUT /cards/{cardNumber}`, and `GET /transactions/{id}` continues to return `tranCardNum` verbatim for the screen to render | Move the card number into a request body or an opaque server-side handle; mask the PAN in the transaction response; truncate to the last four digits everywhere | AAP §0.6.7 enumerates the fields to mask and encrypt — SSN, government-issued id and CVV — and the card PAN is deliberately NOT among them, while `app/bms/COTRN01.bms:L117-118` declares the card number as a displayed field on the transaction-view mapset, so masking it would change a frozen UI contract. §0.7.6 forbids changing data formats for downstream consumers, and the transaction DTO is one such contract | **RESIDUAL:** the PAN appears in the request line of `card-service`, so any component that logs raw request lines — a reverse proxy, an access log, an APM agent — could capture it. Mitigations verified in force: no SPA route carries a PAN (pinned by `frontend/src/api/wireContract.test.ts`), the browser reaches the API same-origin through the nginx `/api` prefix so the PAN is not exposed to a third-party origin, and application log masking is now COMPLETE — the two duplicate unmasked log statements that broke it are deleted in this pass, and `extractPath` has exactly one caller, wrapped in `SensitiveDataMasker.maskPan`. Closing the remainder requires an AAP amendment permitting a wire-shape change `[app/bms/COTRN01.bms:L117-118; AAP §0.6.7, §0.7.6]` |
